# Launchpad Design Dossier — Iteration 1

_Generated 2026-06-07 by the Launchpad Improvement Loop (see CHARTER.md)._  
_Method: 82 agents — 5 domain researchers (web-cited) ‖ 16 code scanners → 16 ideators → 1 skeptic hardening the rubric → adversarial scoring of every concept → synthesis + completeness critic. ~5.4M tokens._

This is the loop's **evidence base**. The actionable output — the ranked, status-flagged build order — lives in `build-queue.md`.

## Scoring rubric (hardened by an independent skeptic)

- **evidenceStrength** (weight 0.3) — good: The concept names a SPECIFIC, citable learning-science mechanism (e.g. retrieval practice / spacing / interleaving, RIASEC interest fit, WOOP/implementation-intentions, possible-selves, calibration/metacognition, Gottfredson circumscription, near-peer/Lin-Siegler struggle-first aspiration) AND the design actually instantiates that mechanism's active ingredient rather than its label. The claimed effect direction matches the literature (e.g. struggle-first stories RAISE low-income outcomes; achievement-only stories LOWER them). A skeptic could trace each pedagogical claim to a real primary source. Strongest when the concept's core loop is the intervention (e.g. a 60-second retrieval probe IS retrieval practice; an if-then cue IS an implementation intention). _Failure mode:_ Buzzword laundering: the title borrows a science term ('Active Recall Mode', 'Calibration Lab', 'de-circumscription') but the mechanism is decorative or inverted — e.g. a 'retrieval' feature that re-shows notes (fluency illusion, the very thing it claims to break), a 'near-peer' layer that is actually trophy/celebrity aspiration (backfires for the cohort), or a confidence slider relabelled as 'calibration' without an actual accuracy check. Also fails: vague 'evidence-based' with no nameable mechanism, or a real mechanism applied where it doesn't transfer. Do NOT reward a concept just because it uses the right vocabulary.
- **studentImpact** (weight 0.3) — good: Plausibly moves a real, valued outcome for the CORE DEIS audience: a measurable behaviour change (a plan actually done vs. read, a paper actually attempted, a deadline met), a calibration/knowledge gain the student can feel, or widened opportunity (a viable Plan B, an affordable+eligible route, a de-circumscribed option a student wouldn't have considered). Reaches students 'not ambitious yet' by lowering the entry cost and building self-efficacy/belonging; frames around autonomy and incremental progress; names outside help, never bootstraps. Impact is durable (resurfaces, spaces, re-plans) not one-shot. Estimate breadth x depth: how many students, how much it changes for them. _Failure mode:_ Impact is assumed not argued, or accrues to already-motivated/high-achieving students while alienating the core cohort with prestige/ambition-coded framing. Punitive gamification (streak-as-punisher) that demotivates the struggling student it should catch. 'Engagement' or time-in-app mistaken for learning. One-shot novelty with no behaviour change. A tool that surfaces options the student still can't afford/access (raises hope then dead-ends). Cannot name the outcome it would move or who it helps.
- **feasibility** (weight 0.18) — good: Buildable on the current stack (React 19/TS SPA, Firebase Auth+Firestore, no live GenAI) within a sane scope; reuses existing primitives (immersiveDeck HybridCard, ToolIconBlob, existing data files, near-peer roster pattern) and existing progress/Firestore patterns. Content is agent-authorable from citable primary sources (SEC papers, examinations.ie, named role models) and verifiable. Persistence model is sound — avoids the known progress-hook staleness trap (seeds from app-start snapshot) by using getDoc-on-mount. Honest about data it needs (real completion rate, absence dates, points trajectory) and where that data realistically comes from. _Failure mode:_ Quietly assumes capabilities the platform lacks: live LLM generation/personalised AI feedback, real-time external data (live CAO points, course fees, deadlines) with no maintenance plan, or self-updating content that will silently rot. Requires the USER to source/curate content by hand (violates agent-authored-content). Hand-waves persistence and will inherit the this-session-saves-vanish staleness bug. Unbounded scope dressed as one feature. Depends on data the student won't reliably provide.
- **designFit** (weight 0.14) — good: Honors the locked design DNA: mistake-first where it's a learning/technique tool (surface the plausible wrong answer → student commits → VISUAL reveal of the mark-loss → fix), animation that ENCODES meaning not decorates, self-generated relevance over told-to-you relevance, near-peer 'someone like me' done struggle-first and process-not-genius. Fits the Innovation-Zone immersive register (white HybridCards, pastel-blob icon, ink serif, orange CTA; no saturated header band, no emoji, no coloured left borders, no warm cream in module surfaces, #3A8D5F success token). Tone meets DEIS students where they are. The concept is coherent and distinct from its sibling concepts rather than a near-duplicate. _Failure mode:_ Right-answer-first exposition for a technique tool; decorative animation that doesn't carry the explanation; told-relevance instead of self-generated. Violates banned patterns (coloured left borders, warm cream surfaces, saturated header bands, emoji, off-token green). Prestige/exclusionary or trauma-foregrounding tone for the cohort. So similar to a sibling concept (e.g. the multiple 'someone like me' / 'more than one way in' / 'open door' variants) that it's redundant rather than differentiated — flag the overlap.
- **novelty** (weight 0.08) — good: Adds something genuinely new to the platform's coverage — a mechanism, audience-moment, or failure-mode it doesn't already address — rather than re-skinning an existing tool. Tie-breaker only: rewarded ONLY once evidenceStrength and studentImpact clear the bar. A novel framing of a SOUND mechanism scores here; a novel-but-shallow gimmick does not. _Failure mode:_ Novelty treated as a primary virtue or used to rescue a concept weak on evidence/impact (the 'flashy-but-shallow' trap this rubric must reject). Novelty that fragments the platform into many overlapping near-duplicate tools. Confusing 'I haven't seen this UI' with 'this teaches something new.' Never let a high novelty score lift a concept that scored low on the top-two criteria.

_Rejected as vanity/unmeasurable:_ Keeping novelty as a full peer criterion (the draft's implicit equal weighting). Rejected: the explicit task is to reward evidence and impact OVER novelty and to separate science-backed from flashy-but-shallow. An equal-weight novelty criterion is exactly the lever a shallow-but-flashy concept uses to win, so it was capped at 0.08 and made a post-threshold tie-breaker.; A standalone 'designFit/polish/aesthetic' heavyweight criterion. Rejected as partly a vanity axis for a CONCEPT-stage judgement: at concept stage, whether it can honour the design DNA matters, but visual polish is not yet real and shouldn't compete with evidence/impact. Kept design as a low-weight (0.14) coherence-and-banned-pattern check, not a beauty contest.; A generic 'innovation/wow-factor' or 'ambition' criterion. Rejected: unmeasurable, and 'ambition' framing actively conflicts with the DEIS-audience rule (students not ambitious yet). It would reward prestige-coded concepts the project explicitly wants to avoid.; Absolute independent 1-5 scoring of each concept in isolation as the FINAL ranking method. Noted as a weakness: for a hard-to-score taste/ranking set this drifts and rewards verbosity. Recommend the rubric's weighted score be used to seed a pairwise tournament (run in deterministic loop code, not a single context) for the final ordering, and that an independent skeptic vet borderline winners — per the dynamic-workflows guidance — rather than trusting one absolute score-sort.; An 'effort/cost-to-build' criterion separate from feasibility. Rejected as redundant with feasibility and risks penalising high-impact-but-larger concepts purely for size; scope concerns are folded into feasibility's 'unbounded scope dressed as one feature' failure mode instead.; Letting any single criterion be auto-disqualifying (hard gate). Considered for evidenceStrength but rejected as too brittle for concept-stage ideas; instead the heavy 0.60 combined weight on evidence+impact plus the novelty tie-breaker rule achieves the separation without a binary kill-switch that could discard a fixable concept.

## Research evidence base

### Career development & vocational psychology — what moves self-efficacy and realistic course choice in 15-18 year-olds, applied to the NextStepUni Launchpad tools

- **Claim:** Career interventions for 15-18s work, but the average effect is only modest (d ≈ 0.35) — and what matters is which active ingredients are present, not how slick the tool is. Brown & Ryan Krane identified FIVE critical components, and effect size rises near-linearly as you stack them: ~0.22 with none, ~0.45 with one, ~0.61 with two, ~0.99 with three. No study in either meta-analysis even contained all five.
  - _Evidence:_ Brown & Ryan Krane (2003) re-analysed 62 career-choice intervention studies and isolated five components — (1) written exercises/workbooks, (2) individualised interpretation & feedback, (3) world-of-work/occupational information, (4) modelling (exposure to role models), (5) attention to building support — finding mean effect ratings of .22 (0 components) → .45 (1) → .61 (2) → .99 (3). Whiston, Li, Mitts & Wright (2017) replicated this across 57 studies, weighted mean d = 0.352, with career-decision self-efficacy the largest single outcome, and both number of sessions AND treatment hours significant dose-response moderators.
  - _Source:_ Brown, S. D., & Ryan Krane, N. E. (2003). 'Critical Ingredients of Career Choice Interventions: More Analyses and New Hypotheses.' Journal of Vocational Behavior, 62(3), 411-428. AND Whiston, S. C., Li, Y., Mitts, N. G., & Wright, L. (2017). 'Effectiveness of career choice interventions: A meta-analytic replication and extension.' Journal of Vocational Behavior, 100, 175-184.
  - _Applies to:_ ALL Launchpad career tools, as a design audit checklist. Future Finder currently delivers ingredient #3 (occupational info) and partial #2 (a profile read-back) but is weak on written exercises, modelling and support-building. Your Possible Life (WOOP) is the written-exercise ingredient. How They Did It is the modelling ingredient. College Compass adds info + support-mapping. The strategic insight: a single tool that adds only ONE ingredient barely moves the needle (d≈0.45); the suite's value is in chaining all five across the loop. Audit each tool for which of the five it actually delivers, and deliberately add the missing ones (esp. individualised feedback + support-building, which are the most absent).

- **Claim:** Possible-selves / 'imagine your future life' tools do NOT change behaviour on their own. A vivid future self only motivates action when it is (a) BALANCED — an expected/hoped-for self explicitly paired with a feared self to avoid, (b) backed by concrete, plausible STRATEGIES that bridge today to that future, and (c) feels 'true to me' and connected to the student's social identity. Aspiration without a pathway is inert — or worse, demotivating.
  - _Evidence:_ Oyserman, Bybee & Terry's randomised 'School-to-Jobs' intervention with low-income 8th graders (n=141 treatment, 123 control) produced significant gains in academic initiative, standardised test scores and grades, and reductions in absences, misbehaviour and depression, SUSTAINED over a 2-year follow-up — but explicitly because it linked academic possible selves to feasible strategies, made identities feel personally and socially congruent, and reframed difficulty as expected ('feeling that schoolwork is difficult means I'm doing it right, not that it's not for me'). Only ~1/3 of youth spontaneously held a balanced possible self. The foundational theory is Markus & Nurius (1986).
  - _Source:_ Oyserman, D., Bybee, D., & Terry, K. (2006). 'Possible selves and academic outcomes: How and when possible selves impel action.' Journal of Personality and Social Psychology, 91(1), 188-204. Foundational: Markus, H., & Nurius, P. (1986). 'Possible selves.' American Psychologist, 41(9), 954-969.
  - _Applies to:_ Your Possible Life and Best Possible Self directly. Your Possible Life's WOOP structure (Wish-Reality-Obstacle-Plan) already encodes balance (the Obstacle = a near feared-self) and a Plan (strategies) — this is its single biggest evidence-based strength; protect it. Strengthen by: (1) making the student name a concrete, this-week strategy step, not just a long-horizon plan; (2) adding an explicit 'difficulty means you're on the right track' reframe so struggle doesn't read as 'this isn't for someone like me'; (3) anchoring the future self in the student's own words/identity, not a generic lifestyle render. Best Possible Self's WOOP planner is well-grounded; ensure the Obstacle field stays mandatory (it's the 'balanced' half).

- **Claim:** RIASEC interest matching is the right, validated scientific anchor for Future Finder — but congruence (interest-environment fit) is only a WEAK-to-MODERATE predictor of later satisfaction, and adolescent interests are not yet stable (test-retest only r ≈ .64 by ages 18-22). A matcher that presents a result as a verdict over-claims; it must present 'directions to explore', show a RANGE, and invite re-taking.
  - _Evidence:_ Holland's RIASEC underpins the public-domain O*NET Interest Profiler with strong reliability (short-form α ≈ .81) and replicated hexagonal structure, and interest-major congruence predicts college-major persistence (Allen & Robbins, 2008). BUT the congruence-satisfaction link is contested and modest — Tinsley (2000) labelled it 'The Congruence Myth' after meta-analytic correlations of fit-to-satisfaction hovered around r ≈ .15-.25; and Low, Yoon, Roberts & Rounds (2005) found vocational-interest rank-order stability is low through mid-adolescence, only reaching r ≈ .64 by ages 18-22. Su, Rounds & Armstrong (2009) further show large sex differences on the Things-People dimension (d ≈ 0.93), so a raw quiz can amplify gender-stereotyped routing unless items are activity-based and endorsement-balanced.
  - _Source:_ Holland, J. L. (1997). 'Making Vocational Choices.' / O*NET Interest Profiler Manual. Tinsley, H. E. A. (2000). 'The Congruence Myth: An Analysis of the Efficacy of the Person-Environment Fit Model.' Journal of Vocational Behavior, 56(2), 147-179. Low, K. S. D., Yoon, M., Roberts, B. W., & Rounds, J. (2005). 'The stability of vocational interests from early adolescence to middle adulthood.' Psychological Bulletin, 131(5), 713-737. Su, R., Rounds, J., & Armstrong, P. I. (2009). Psychological Bulletin, 135(6), 859-884.
  - _Applies to:_ Future Finder (the RIASEC re-base in docs/future-finder-redesign-plan.md and components/futureFinderRiasec.ts). The plan's instincts are correct and well-sourced — keep: activity-based (not job-title) items, gender-endorsement screening, the 'snapshot not verdict' framing, showing a range, and rewarding hexagon-adjacent near-matches. The Congruence-Myth and interest-instability evidence is the scientific justification for NEVER hard-routing and for the re-take invitation. Concretely: surface near-miss (hexagon-adjacent) high-fit courses, including counter-stereotypical ones, and label the match as 'worth exploring', never 'your best course'.

- **Claim:** The single most important honesty win is the tool's existing rule: NEVER blend interest-fit with CAO points into one score. Points-feasibility ('reach') and interest-fit are different axes; collapsing them silently demotes perfect-fit courses and reinforces circumscription — the well-documented adolescent process of prematurely eliminating whole fields as 'not for someone like me' (by prestige, gender, then perceived ability). Always also surface the lower-points / PLC / apprenticeship pathway to the SAME field.
  - _Evidence:_ Gottfredson's (1981) theory of circumscription and compromise shows adolescents progressively eliminate occupations by perceived prestige/ability ceiling and sex-type BEFORE considering personal interests, and that field of interest is sacrificed FIRST in compromise — so a tool that lets a points number quietly delete a high-interest field is actively reinforcing the most damaging part of the developmental process. Disadvantaged youth are disproportionately prone to this aspiration-attainment misalignment (Behavioural Insights Team / Careers & Enterprise Company, 2018).
  - _Source:_ Gottfredson, L. S. (1981). 'Circumscription and compromise: A developmental theory of occupational aspirations.' Journal of Counseling Psychology, 28(6), 545-579. Supporting: Behavioural Insights Team & The Careers & Enterprise Company (2018). 'Moments of Choice / Effective Careers Interventions for Disadvantaged Young People.'
  - _Applies to:_ Future Finder's Fit/Reach/Eligibility separation (futureFinderRiasec.ts: profileCorrelation is computed PURELY from interests; reachBucket and checkEligibility are independent) is exactly right and should be defended against any future 'just give me one ranked list' pressure — if one list is ever forced, sort by Fit and use feasibility only as a shown tie-breaker. The 'open' reach bucket for PLC/apprenticeship (points<=0) and surfacing same-field lower-points pathways is the de-circumscription mechanism. College Compass and Career Paths should mirror this: never present a field as closed because of points; always show the alternative route in (PLC→add-on degree, apprenticeship).

- **Claim:** Self-efficacy — not interest or 'aspiration' — is the lever most reliably moved by good career tools, and Bandura's four sources rank-order how to move it: mastery experiences (strongest) > vicarious experience via SIMILAR models > verbal persuasion > managing emotional arousal. The design implication is concrete: give students small wins and relatable near-peer/'people like me' role models, not generic pep-talk encouragement.
  - _Evidence:_ Bandura (1997) established the four sources with mastery experiences as the most powerful and vicarious experience strongest when the model is perceived as similar to the self. In Lent, Brown & Hackett's Social Cognitive Career Theory, self-efficacy and outcome expectations jointly shape interests and choice goals (meta-analytic interest-to-goal true-score r ≈ .60; self-efficacy-to-goal ≈ .40); Sheu et al. (2010) confirmed these paths across all Holland themes. Whiston et al. (2017) found career-decision self-efficacy was the outcome with the largest intervention effect — i.e. it's both pivotal and movable.
  - _Source:_ Bandura, A. (1997). 'Self-Efficacy: The Exercise of Control.' W. H. Freeman. Lent, R. W., Brown, S. D., & Hackett, G. (1994). 'Toward a unifying social cognitive theory of career and academic interest, choice, and performance.' Journal of Vocational Behavior, 45(1), 79-122. Sheu, H.-B., et al. (2010). Journal of Vocational Behavior, 76(2), 252-264.
  - _Applies to:_ How They Did It (modelling) is the vicarious-experience tool — its evidence value depends entirely on model SIMILARITY: feature relatable DEIS/local/near-peer journeys, not distant celebrity success, so students read 'someone like me did this'. Your Possible Life / Best Possible Self should manufacture small mastery experiences (a completed plan step the student can check off this week) rather than only verbal persuasion. The whole suite should treat 'raise career-decision self-efficacy' as its primary measurable outcome, because it is the most movable and the strongest SCCT mediator of realistic choice.

- **Claim:** For DEIS students specifically, the evidence overturns the naive 'raise their aspirations' model: disadvantaged young people do NOT generally have lower aspirations — they have an aspiration-attainment/knowledge gap (they don't know the concrete pathways, costs and supports). Interventions that just inflate aspirations or self-esteem are ineffective or counterproductive; what works is concrete pathway knowledge, removing logistical/financial barriers, relatable models, and personalised transition support.
  - _Evidence:_ Gorard, See & Davies (2012), synthesising 166,000+ research reports for the Joseph Rowntree Foundation/EEF, found NO causal link from young people's (or parents') aspirations to educational outcomes, and warned that 'policies based on enhancing motivation, self-efficacy and self-esteem may be ineffective or worse'. The Behavioural Insights Team/CEC review of disadvantaged youth concluded effective interventions cluster into building aspirations + overcoming stereotypes, parental engagement, peer support, EMPLOYER ENCOUNTERS, and personalised transition support — i.e. concrete knowledge and contact, not exhortation. Stephens, Hamedani & Destin (2014) showed a one-hour 'difference-education' intervention (framing how a working-class background shapes the college experience and where to seek help) ELIMINATED the social-class GPA gap in a RCT (N=168), working through resource-seeking behaviour.
  - _Source:_ Gorard, S., See, B. H., & Davies, P. (2012). 'The impact of attitudes and aspirations on educational attainment and participation.' Joseph Rowntree Foundation. Stephens, N. M., Hamedani, M. G., & Destin, M. (2014). 'Closing the Social-Class Achievement Gap: A Difference-Education Intervention.' Psychological Science, 25(4), 943-949.
  - _Applies to:_ Cross-cutting DEIS design principle for the whole Launchpad, but most directly College Compass (HEAR/DARE/SUSI deadlines, eligibility self-checks, the document checklist) — this IS the 'concrete pathway knowledge + barrier removal' that the evidence says actually works for this audience, so it may be the highest-leverage tool in the suite. The framing should be Stephens-style 'difference-education': normalise that not knowing the system is about the system, not the student, and point to where to get help. Avoid pure aspiration/lifestyle inflation in Your Possible Life (money as 'one ingredient, never a worth-score' is the right guardrail). Career Paths should foreground the concrete 'how you get there' and honest pros/cons over glamour.

- **Claim:** Be skeptical of bolting on trendy motivational mechanics. Growth-mindset interventions have a contested, on-average TINY effect (meta-analytic d ≈ 0.08, r ≈ .09), and even the strong national RCT only found ~0.11 SD on grades — and ONLY for lower-achieving students in supportive peer climates. Learning-styles 'matching' is a debunked neuromyth with no supporting evidence. Neither should be a load-bearing claim or a gamified gimmick in a career tool.
  - _Evidence:_ Sisk, Burgoyne, Sun, Butler & Macnamara (2018) meta-analysed 129+ studies (N≈365,915) and found mindset explained ~1% of achievement variance, with intervention effects averaging d ≈ 0.08. Yeager et al.'s (2019) National Study of Learning Mindsets RCT (N=12,490, nationally representative 9th graders) found ~0.11 SD on grades, rising to ~0.18-0.25 SD only for at-risk students in growth-supportive peer norms — a real but small, HEAVILY moderated effect. Pashler, McDaniel, Rohrer & Bjork (2008) found no credible evidence for the learning-styles meshing hypothesis.
  - _Source:_ Sisk, V. F., Burgoyne, A. P., Sun, J., Butler, J. L., & Macnamara, B. N. (2018). 'To What Extent and Under Which Circumstances Are Growth Mind-Sets Important to Academic Achievement? Two Meta-Analyses.' Psychological Science, 29(4), 549-571. Yeager, D. S., et al. (2019). 'A national experiment reveals where a growth mindset improves achievement.' Nature, 573, 364-369. Pashler, H., McDaniel, M., Rohrer, D., & Bjork, R. (2008). Psychological Science in the Public Interest, 9(3), 105-119.
  - _Applies to:_ A guardrail across the Launchpad and especially any 'mindset'/motivation copy. If a growth-mindset reframe is used (e.g. the Oyserman-style 'difficulty is expected' message in Your Possible Life), keep it light, targeted at struggling students, and don't over-promise — and never make learning-styles or a generic mindset quiz a tool. Prefer the better-evidenced levers (concrete pathway knowledge, mastery steps, similar role models, utility-value writing) over fashionable but weak ones. This protects the project's scientific credibility for the PwC pitch.

- **Claim:** A cheap, high-leverage addition that fits the suite: utility-value writing. Having students write 1-2 paragraphs in their OWN words about how an effort/subject connects to their personal life and future raises interest, grades and persistence — and the gains are LARGEST for low-expectancy/low-performing students, the exact DEIS profile. This is the empirical engine that makes 'connect effort now to the life it makes possible' actually work.
  - _Evidence:_ Hulleman & Harackiewicz (2009, Science) randomised high-school science students to write either a summary (control) or about the personal relevance of the material (utility-value); the relevance-writing group showed higher interest and grades, with effects CONCENTRATED among students who had low success expectations. Harackiewicz, Rozek, Hulleman & Hyde (2012) extended this to a parent-delivered version raising STEM course-taking. Crucially, student-self-generated relevance outperforms being told the relevance.
  - _Source:_ Hulleman, C. S., & Harackiewicz, J. M. (2009). 'Promoting interest and performance in high school science classes.' Science, 326(5958), 1410-1412. Harackiewicz, J. M., Rozek, C. S., Hulleman, C. S., & Hyde, J. S. (2012). Psychological Science, 23(8), 899-906.
  - _Applies to:_ Your Possible Life directly — its core thesis ('connect effort NOW to the life it makes possible') is essentially a utility-value intervention, so it should require the student to write/articulate the link in their OWN words (the 'why' field already in the component) rather than the tool asserting the link for them; that self-generation is what the evidence shows drives the effect, and it doubles as Brown & Ryan Krane's 'written exercise' ingredient. Could also seed a short relevance-writing prompt into Career Paths ('why might this path matter to you?') and into Future Finder's results.

- **Claim:** These are tools for 15-18 year-olds in the EXPLORATION stage, so the design must favour breadth of exploration over premature commitment. A matcher that delivers a tidy single answer risks inducing identity FORECLOSURE — a premature, externally-driven commitment without exploration, which is the developmentally 'unsuccessful' outcome. The right posture is moratorium-supporting: try many directions, hold them loosely, decide later.
  - _Evidence:_ Marcia's (1966) identity-status model defines foreclosure as commitment without exploration (often complying with authority/parent expectations) and treats it as a less mature, undesirable status, versus moratorium (active exploration) en route to achievement. Super's developmental stages place 15-18s firmly in Exploration, where the task is to widen and crystallise options, not finalise them. Combined with the adolescent interest-instability evidence (Low et al., 2005), this argues for option-expanding, re-takeable tools.
  - _Source:_ Marcia, J. E. (1966). 'Development and validation of ego-identity status.' Journal of Personality and Social Psychology, 3(5), 551-558. Super, D. E. (1980). 'A life-span, life-space approach to career development.' Journal of Vocational Behavior, 16(3), 282-298.
  - _Applies to:_ Future Finder's explicit 'directions to explore / snapshot not verdict / re-take' framing is the correct foreclosure-avoiding design — keep and reinforce it (e.g. always show ~10 options per level, never one). Career Paths' swipe-deck breadth and How They Did It's multiple journeys support moratorium-style exploration. Guard against any UX that pressures a single early commitment (e.g. 'lock in your course') — frame everything as decision SUPPORT handed to a qualified guidance counsellor, consistent with the project's minors/consent posture.

  **Design principles:** Stack the five Brown & Ryan Krane ingredients across the suite, don't chase polish. Effect size scales with how many of {written exercise, individualised feedback, world-of-work info, similar-model exposure, support-building} a student encounters — so audit each tool for which it delivers and deliberately add the two that are most absent across the Launchpad: individualised feedback and support-building (e.g. 'here's what your profile suggests AND who to talk to / where to get help next'). · Never blend interest-fit with points into one number, and never let a points score delete a field. Keep Future Finder's Fit / Reach / Eligibility as three independent axes combined only at display; always surface the same-field lower-points/PLC/apprenticeship route beside any 'reach' course. This is the concrete antidote to Gottfredson circumscription for a DEIS audience. · Treat raising career-decision self-efficacy as the primary, measurable goal — it's the most movable outcome and the strongest SCCT mediator of realistic choice. Move it Bandura's way: manufacture small mastery wins (a checkable plan step this week), and lead with SIMILAR near-peer/local/DEIS role models (vicarious) over generic encouragement (verbal persuasion is the weaker lever). · Make the student generate the future, the strategy, and the relevance in their own words; the tool must not just assert them. Possible-selves and utility-value evidence both show self-generated, balanced (hope paired with a named obstacle), strategy-backed, 'true-to-me' content drives behaviour — abstract aspiration or tool-asserted relevance does not. Protect Your Possible Life's WOOP obstacle field and its free-text 'why' as the load-bearing mechanics. · Frame everything as a re-takeable snapshot and decision SUPPORT, not a verdict. Adolescent interests aren't stable and congruence only weakly predicts satisfaction, so present 'directions to explore', show a range (~10+ options), reward hexagon-adjacent and counter-stereotypical near-matches, invite re-taking, and hand decisions to a guidance counsellor — avoiding identity foreclosure and over-claiming. · Design for the DEIS reality: the gap is knowledge and barriers, not aspiration. Lead with concrete pathway facts, deadlines, costs, eligibility (HEAR/DARE/SUSI) and 'where to get help', in Stephens-style difference-education framing ('not knowing the system is about the system, not about you'). Avoid aspiration/lifestyle inflation and money-as-worth-score. College Compass is plausibly the highest-leverage tool because it is exactly this. · Use trendy motivational mechanics with extreme restraint and honest sizing. Growth-mindset effects are tiny and heavily moderated (real only for struggling students in supportive climates); learning-styles matching is a debunked myth. Don't build a mindset quiz or a learning-styles feature; if a 'difficulty is expected' reframe is used, keep it light and targeted. Use animation/gamification only to encode meaning (mistake-first, mark-loss made visible), never as a dopamine gimmick — this protects credibility for the PwC pitch. · Honour activity-based, gender-balanced items and surface counter-stereotypical high-fit options on purpose. People-Things interest sex differences are large enough that a naive quiz amplifies stereotyped routing; activity statements (not job titles), endorsement-difference screening, and deliberately showing relatable counter-stereotypical models/courses turn the tool into an option-EXPANDER rather than a stereotype-confirmer.

### Aspiration, role models & belonging for low-SES / first-generation students — what raises college-going intent in DEIS-type cohorts without exclusionary high-ac

- **Claim:** The 'aspiration gap' is mostly a myth: disadvantaged students already have high aspirations. The real gap is between aspiration and the knowledge/expectation/pathway to reach it. Pure aspiration-raising shows little-to-no effect on attainment; effective programmes attach a concrete academic/navigational component.
  - _Evidence:_ Kintrea, St Clair & Houston (JRF, 2011) found poorer children and families 'often have high aspirations' and the difficulty is 'in knowing how to fulfil their ambitions'; St Clair et al. advocate careers advice, work experience and 'day to day' support to learn how to attain goals rather than aspiration-raising. The EEF Teaching & Learning Toolkit concludes interventions to raise aspirations 'appear to have little to no positive impact on educational attainment' and that 'effective approaches almost always have a significant academic component', so 'raising aspirations in isolation will not be effective'. Carter-Wall & Whitfield (JRF, 2012) reach the same conclusion.
  - _Source:_ Kintrea, St Clair & Houston (2011), 'The influence of parents, places and poverty on educational attitudes and aspirations' (JRF); Education Endowment Foundation, 'Aspiration interventions', Teaching & Learning Toolkit; Carter-Wall & Whitfield (2012), 'The role of aspirations, attitudes and behaviour in closing the educational attainment gap' (JRF).
  - _Applies to:_ This is the load-bearing design constraint for the whole aspiration cluster (Your Possible Life, Career Paths, How They Did It, College Compass, North Star). These tools should NOT be 'dream big' motivators. Every aspirational surface must terminate in a concrete navigational next step — a CAO route, points target, course match, application step, or 'how they got there' mechanic — never a lifestyle-fantasy generator. Your Possible Life's WOOP framing (Wish→Reality→Plan) and Career Paths' link to real CAO courses are exactly right; the principle says guard against any tool drifting into pure inspiration.

- **Claim:** Outstanding role models inspire only when their success seems ATTAINABLE (the viewer still has time, or believes ability grows); when success looks unattainable, the same star DEFLATES and lowers self-view. Inspiration also requires the model's domain to be self-relevant.
  - _Evidence:_ Lockwood & Kunda (1997): 'relevant superstars provoke self-enhancement and inspiration when their success seems attainable but self-deflation when it seems unattainable'; participants' self-views were affected 'only when the star's domain of excellence was self-relevant', and inspiration occurred when 'participants either still had enough time to achieve comparable success or believed their own abilities could improve over time.'
  - _Source:_ Lockwood, P. & Kunda, Z. (1997), 'Superstars and Me: Predicting the Impact of Role Models on the Self', Journal of Personality and Social Psychology, 73(1), 91–103.
  - _Applies to:_ How They Did It and Career Paths: the role-model/case-study profiles must foreground the attainable PATH (the early struggles, the specific steps, 'this person started where you are'), not the polished end-state. A profile that leads with elite outcomes ('became a surgeon / earns €120k') without the relatable on-ramp risks deflating DEIS students rather than inspiring them. Pair every aspirational figure with an explicit 'you still have time + ability grows' frame (ties to the project's growth-mindset modules).

- **Claim:** Relatability/similarity of the role model matters most for members of negatively-stereotyped or under-represented groups: a competent SAME-group model buffers performance and raises self-appraised ability, while members of non-stereotyped groups are far less dependent on matched models.
  - _Evidence:_ Marx & Roman (2002): women's maths performance was protected by a competent female experimenter (14.46 vs 13.56 correct) and the buffering came from perceived competence, which 'in turn led to successful performance'. Lockwood (2006): 'female participants were more inspired by outstanding female than male role models; in contrast, gender did not determine the impact of role models on male participants.' Near-peer mentoring research (Plaskett et al. 2018; evidence-based-mentoring reviews) finds first-gen/low-income students benefit from 'competent, relatable models who can draw on their own experiences to help build self-efficacy without stigmatization,' especially mentors who are themselves successful first-gen/low-income upperclassmen.
  - _Source:_ Marx, D. M. & Roman, J. S. (2002), 'Female Role Models: Protecting Women's Math Test Performance', PSPB, 28(9), 1183–1193; Lockwood, P. (2006), '"Someone Like Me Can Be Successful"', Psychology of Women Quarterly, 30(1), 36–46; Plaskett, Bali, Nakkula & Harris (2018), 'Peer mentoring to support first-generation low-income college students', Phi Delta Kappan.
  - _Applies to:_ How They Did It and Career Paths: tag/profile real people who share salient features with DEIS students (first-in-family, from a non-grind-school / rural / working-class background, took the 'long way round' via PLC/repeat/access route). A filter or match that surfaces 'someone like me' profiles will out-perform generic celebrity exemplars for this cohort. Avoid implying the matched model is the ONLY route (don't over-narrow), but make the relatable on-ramp the default first card.

- **Claim:** Possible-selves interventions that make a desired future SELF vivid AND link it to concrete current strategies/obstacles produce durable academic gains in low-income adolescents — the effect comes from the action-linkage, not the daydream.
  - _Evidence:_ Oyserman, Bybee & Terry's School-to-Jobs randomized field experiment (141 treatment / 123 control low-income 8th graders) improved standardized scores, grades and academic initiative and reduced absences/misbehaviour/depression, with effects sustained over a 2-year follow-up and 'mediated by change in possible selves.' Crucially the curriculum paired future-self imagery with the strategies and obstacles to get there.
  - _Source:_ Oyserman, D., Bybee, D. & Terry, K. (2006), 'Possible Selves and Academic Outcomes: How and When Possible Selves Impel Action', Journal of Personality and Social Psychology, 91(1), 188–204 (and the School-to-Jobs RCT, Oyserman, Terry & Bybee, 2002, Journal of Adolescence).
  - _Applies to:_ Your Possible Life, Best Possible Self, North Star onboarding, Linking Study & Future Goals: these are validated mechanisms when (and only when) the future image is bound to present-day strategies and named obstacles. Best Possible Self's WOOP planner and North Star's 'what's driving you' → vision board → action linkage are on-evidence. The design imperative: never let the 'imagine your future' step stand alone — always force the bridge to a concrete this-year strategy + obstacle (mental contrasting / WOOP).

- **Claim:** Making the path to college look OPEN and affordable (financial aid, points achievable, route exists) increases current school effort in low-income adolescents; making it look closed/expensive suppresses effort. Belief that effort pays off is contingent on a visible open pathway.
  - _Evidence:_ Destin & Oyserman (2009), 'From Assets to School Outcomes': low-income adolescents primed that college could be paid for via need-based aid (open path) expected higher grades and planned more homework than those in a closed/expensive-path or control condition. In Destin & Oyserman's 'Incentivizing education' (2010), an education-dependent earnings frame raised planned effort (Study 2, M=1.67 vs 1.24, p<.05); under identity-based motivation, effort feels like 'an investment toward attaining an education-dependent future identity, and not like a future identity-irrelevant chore.'
  - _Source:_ Destin, M. & Oyserman, D. (2009), 'From Assets to School Outcomes: How Finances Shape Children's Perceived Possibilities and Intentions', Psychological Science, 20(4), 414–418; Destin, M. & Oyserman, D. (2010), 'Incentivizing Education: Seeing Schoolwork as an Investment, Not a Chore', Journal of Experimental Social Psychology, 46(5), 846–849.
  - _Applies to:_ College Compass, CAO Simulator, Points Passport, Your Possible Life's 'money is one ingredient' framing: these must actively show the path is OPEN — SUSI/grant eligibility, achievable points routes, PLC/access/HEAR pathways — not just present aspirational destinations. The single highest-leverage content add is an explicit affordability/eligibility layer ('here's how someone with your situation actually pays for and gets into this'). This is also the evidence base for the project's progress-and-effort framing over fixed outcomes.

- **Claim:** A brief, well-targeted social-belonging intervention — framing early struggle as common and TEMPORARY, voiced by senior students who felt the same and then belonged — can durably raise stigmatized students' GPA and close achievement gaps, with the student self-generating the message.
  - _Evidence:_ Walton & Cohen (2011, Science): a brief belonging intervention using senior students' survey reflections (belonging worries fade with time) 'raised African Americans' grade-point average (GPA) relative to multiple control groups and halved the minority achievement gap' over 3 years and improved self-reported health; students showed 'no awareness' of its impact. Stephens, Hamedani & Destin (2014, Psychological Science) 'difference-education' used senior students' real-life stories about how diverse backgrounds shape college experience; it 'eliminated the social-class achievement gap by increasing first-generation students' tendency to seek support.'
  - _Source:_ Walton, G. M. & Cohen, G. L. (2011), 'A Brief Social-Belonging Intervention Improves Academic and Health Outcomes of Minority Students', Science, 331(6023), 1447–1451; Stephens, N. M., Hamedani, M. G. & Destin, M. (2014), 'Closing the Social-Class Achievement Gap', Psychological Science, 25(4), 943–953.
  - _Applies to:_ How They Did It and a belonging layer across the Launchpad/onboarding: the most evidence-backed format is short first-person stories from near-peers/first-gen students saying 'I felt out of place / behind too, and it got better' plus what they did. Frame transition worries (CAO stress, 'am I university material', impostor feelings) as normal-and-transient, and let the student articulate it themselves (a reflection prompt) rather than being told. This is distinct from, and stronger than, generic motivational copy.

- **Claim:** Hope is operationally two trainable cognitions — agency ('I can') and pathways ('I see routes, including around obstacles') — and academic-specific hope correlates with GPA (~r=.39), markedly higher than generalized hope.
  - _Evidence:_ Snyder's hope theory defines hope as 'a sense of successful agency (goal-directed energy) combined with pathways (the perceived ability to plan ways to meet goals)'; hopeful thinkers 'imagine multiple workable pathways' and persevere through obstacles. A meta-analysis found hope's average correlation with academic achievement was .19, rising to .39 when measured with academic-specific hope scales.
  - _Source:_ Snyder, C. R. (2002), 'Hope Theory: Rainbows in the Mind', Psychological Inquiry, 13(4), 249–275; Rand, Shanahan, Fischer & Fortney meta-analytic summaries of hope–GPA associations (see also Snyder et al., 2002, Journal of Educational Psychology).
  - _Applies to:_ Your Possible Life, College Compass, Future Finder, planning tools: design for BOTH components. Pathways = always show multiple routes to a goal and an explicit 'if this is blocked, here's another way' (CAO Plan B, PLC→degree, repeat year, alternative courses for the same career) so a single setback doesn't read as the end. Agency = let students record their own concrete next action. The 'multiple flexible pathways' finding is the antidote to the all-or-nothing points anxiety that can crush DEIS students.

- **Claim:** SKEPTICAL ANCHOR: psychological 'nudge' interventions (growth-mindset, belonging, possible-selves) have small and HETEROGENEOUS average effects — they reliably help disadvantaged/at-risk students MORE than others, but only when the surrounding context (peers, content, real opportunity) supports the message. They are not magic and fail if framed as a substitute for skills, teaching, or real pathways.
  - _Evidence:_ Sisk et al. (2018) meta-analysis: overall mindset-intervention effect on achievement was small, d=0.08, but 'students with previous failed classes and students experiencing economic disadvantage benefitted significantly more than their peers.' Yeager et al. (2019, Nature, NSLM, ~12,000 9th-graders) found the growth-mindset effect on lower-achievers' D/F rates was a 5.3-point reduction (~0.10 GPA, 0.11 SD) but 'chiefly when peers displayed a norm of challenge-seeking'; a 'peer culture that did not share or support growth-mindset behaviors' nullified it. Yeager et al. (2022) formalize this as Mindset × Context.
  - _Source:_ Sisk, V. F., Burgoyne, A. P., Sun, J., Butler, J. L. & Macnamara, B. N. (2018), 'To What Extent and Under Which Circumstances Are Growth Mind-Sets Important to Academic Achievement? Two Meta-Analyses', Psychological Science, 29(4), 549–571; Yeager, D. S. et al. (2019), 'A national experiment reveals where a growth mindset improves achievement', Nature, 573, 364–369.
  - _Applies to:_ Whole Launchpad + the project's growth/mindset modules: set realistic expectations, target the cohort that benefits (DEIS/at-risk), and never ship a belief-change tool without the supporting scaffolding (real CAO routes, study skills, a peer norm of trying). Effect sizes are small per-tool; the value is cumulative and context-dependent. Avoid over-claiming and avoid 'just believe in yourself' framing divorced from skills and opportunity — the evidence says that version doesn't work.

  **Design principles:** Aspiration must always terminate in a concrete next step. The evidence is unambiguous that pure aspiration-raising does not move attainment (EEF; Kintrea/JRF). Every aspirational surface (Your Possible Life, Career Paths, North Star) must hand off to a navigational mechanic — a CAO route, a points target, an application step, a 'how they got there' breakdown — never end on a daydream. · Lead with the attainable on-ramp, not the polished destination. Lockwood & Kunda show elite end-states deflate when they look unattainable. In How They Did It / Career Paths, the FIRST thing a student sees should be the relatable starting point and the specific path ('she repeated, did a PLC, then got in'), with the impressive outcome as the payoff, paired with an explicit 'you still have time and ability grows' frame. · Default to 'someone like me' role models for this cohort. Similarity (first-in-family, working-class, non-grind-school, took an access/PLC route) is what buffers stereotyped/under-represented students (Marx & Roman; Lockwood 2006; near-peer mentoring). Build a relatability tag/match so DEIS students meet matched near-peers first — but never imply the matched route is the only route. · Bind every future-self image to a present strategy and a named obstacle (WOOP / mental contrasting). Possible-selves effects come from action-linkage, not imagery (Oyserman). Best Possible Self and Your Possible Life are on-evidence precisely because of their Wish→Reality/Obstacle→Plan structure; never let an 'imagine your future' step stand alone. · Make the path visibly OPEN. Show affordability and eligibility, not just destinations. Destin & Oyserman: an open/affordable path raises effort; a closed/expensive one suppresses it. Add an explicit SUSI/grant, HEAR/DARE, PLC→degree and 'achievable points' layer to College Compass / CAO Simulator / Points Passport so 'I can actually get there and pay for it' is concrete. · Normalize struggle as common and transient, in near-peers' own voices, and let the student self-generate the message. Walton & Cohen and Stephens (difference-education) get durable gap-closing from short first-person 'I felt behind too, and it got better + here's what I did' stories plus a reflection prompt — not from being told to feel confident. Add a belonging layer to onboarding and How They Did It in this exact format. · Always offer multiple, flexible pathways and an explicit Plan B. Hope theory's 'pathways' component and the meta-analytic GPA link (~.39 for academic hope) mean a single blocked route must never read as the end. CAO Plan B, alternative courses for the same career, repeat/PLC/access routes, and a recorded next action (the agency component) should be first-class features, especially to defuse all-or-nothing points anxiety. · Calibrate claims and target the cohort; never sell belief-change as a substitute for skills or real opportunity. Per-tool effects are small and heterogeneous (Sisk d=0.08; Yeager NSLM works mainly where peers/context support it). Frame around autonomy, incremental progress and belonging for DEIS students specifically; pair every mindset/belonging surface with real scaffolding (routes, study skills, a peer norm of trying); avoid 'just believe in yourself' copy and avoid exclusionary high-achiever framing that signals 'this isn't for people like me.'

### Self-regulated learning & study planning — what turns a static timetable into a tool that changes study behaviour

- **Claim:** Of ten common study techniques, only practice testing (retrieval) and distributed (spaced) practice earned a 'high utility' rating; interleaving and elaborative interrogation were 'moderate'; rereading, highlighting and summarising were rated LOW utility despite being the strategies students use most. A timetable should therefore schedule retrieval + spacing as the default session type, not generic 'study Maths'.
  - _Evidence:_ Dunlosky et al. systematically reviewed each technique across age, ability and material, rating practice testing and distributed practice high utility because they generalise broadly and boost real achievement, while the popular passive techniques (rereading, highlighting, summarising) were low utility.
  - _Source:_ Dunlosky, J., Rawson, K. A., Marsh, E. J., Nathan, M. J., & Willingham, D. T. (2013). Improving Students' Learning With Effective Learning Techniques. Psychological Science in the Public Interest, 14(1), 4–58.
  - _Applies to:_ planner (SpacedRepetitionTimetable / timetableAlgorithm.ts sessionType: 'new-learning'|'practice'|'revision') and study/StrategyPickerStep.tsx: the tool already labels block types and surfaces strategy hints, but should explicitly bias toward retrieval+spacing and visibly down-rank rereading/highlighting in StrategyPicker copy, naming WHY (utility tier), not just listing strategies neutrally.

- **Claim:** Retrieval practice (self-testing) beats restudying for long-term retention, and the advantage GROWS with delay — at 5 minutes restudy can win, but at 2 days–1 week testing wins decisively. This is the single strongest justification for making the planner's sessions active-recall sessions rather than passive review blocks.
  - _Evidence:_ Students who took recall tests on prose passages out-retained those who restudied the same number of times; restudy helped at a 5-min delay but testing produced substantially greater retention at 2-day and 1-week delays. Karpicke & Roediger (2008) further showed repeated testing produced a large delayed-recall benefit while repeated restudy after initial learning produced none.
  - _Source:_ Roediger, H. L., & Karpicke, J. D. (2006). Test-Enhanced Learning: Taking Memory Tests Improves Long-Term Retention. Psychological Science, 17(3), 249–255; and Karpicke, J. D., & Roediger, H. L. (2008). The Critical Importance of Retrieval for Learning. Science, 319, 966–968.
  - _Applies to:_ planner + study/StudySessionView.tsx (which already injects TeachBack cards mid-session): make the in-session prompt a retrieval prompt ('close the book, write what you remember') rather than a re-read prompt; the StudyDebrief confidence-gain signal should be tied to a recall attempt, not self-rated review.

- **Claim:** Spacing has a quantifiable optimal gap: the ideal inter-study interval scales with how far away the test is — roughly 10–20% of the retention interval (e.g. for a test ~1 month away, review every few days; for an exam ~1 year away, gaps of weeks). A fixed weekly cadence is a crude approximation; the planner's SM-2 intervals should widen as the exam recedes and tighten as it nears.
  - _Evidence:_ Meta-analysis of 317 experiments (839 comparisons) found inter-study interval and retention interval operate jointly: the gap producing maximal retention increased as the test delay increased, with the optimum gap about 20% of the delay at a few weeks, falling toward ~5–10% at one year.
  - _Source:_ Cepeda, N. J., Pashler, H., Vul, E., Wixted, J. T., & Rohrer, D. (2006). Distributed Practice in Verbal Recall Tasks: A Review and Quantitative Synthesis. Psychological Bulletin, 132(3), 354–380.
  - _Applies to:_ timetableAlgorithm.ts: the SM-2 interval logic (intervals 1,3,7,14,28… capped at 60d) is sound but exam-proximity-agnostic for spacing; computeIntensityFactor already ramps load by weeksUntilExam — extend the same proximity signal to the review GAP so spacing optimises against the exam date, the explicit Cepeda mechanism.

- **Claim:** Interleaving (mixing problem types) outperforms blocking on delayed tests and provides near-immunity to forgetting — but it feels harder and slower, so students avoid it. The planner already interleaves subjects within a day; the bigger untapped win is interleaving TOPIC/problem types within a subject session.
  - _Evidence:_ In a randomised trial with 126 seventh-graders given identical problems, the higher dose of interleaved practice produced higher scores after 1-day and 1-month delays; a 30-fold increase in test delay cut scores by less than a tenth (80%→74%).
  - _Source:_ Rohrer, D., Dedrick, R. F., & Stershic, S. (2015). Interleaved Practice Improves Mathematics Learning. Journal of Educational Psychology, 107(3), 900–908.
  - _Applies to:_ timetableAlgorithm.ts interleaveBlocks() interleaves SUBJECTS within a day (good), and suggestedTopics surfaces shaky topics — extend to prescribe MIXED topic sets within a problem-practice block ('alternate question types', not 'do all of topic X'), and warn students in-copy that the harder feeling is the point (desirable difficulty).

- **Claim:** The core reason a static timetable fails is the metacognitive illusion: students systematically misjudge which strategies work, because the EASY-feeling ones (massing, rereading) produce fluency that masquerades as learning. A planner must therefore correct judgement with objective feedback — not just let students self-rate.
  - _Evidence:_ Kornell & Bjork (2008): after spaced practice produced better inductive learning than massed, 64 of 72 participants who saw a difference still believed massing had worked better — the fluency of massed repetition created a false sense of mastery. Karpicke & Roediger (2008): students' predictions of their own performance were uncorrelated with actual performance.
  - _Source:_ Kornell, N., & Bjork, R. A. (2008). Learning Concepts and Categories: Is Spacing the 'Enemy of Induction'? Psychological Science, 19(6), 585–592; Karpicke & Roediger (2008), Science, 319, 966–968.
  - _Applies to:_ StudyDebrief.tsx computes a 'best strategy per subject' from confidenceBefore/After gain — confidence is exactly the illusory signal Kornell/Bjork warn about. Anchor the debrief to a recall-success measure (could you retrieve it cold?) rather than self-rated confidence, or at minimum show the divergence so students learn their own fluency illusion.

- **Claim:** Implementation intentions ('if/when situation X, then I will do Y') have a medium-to-large effect (d ≈ 0.65 across 94 studies, >8,000 participants) on actually enacting goals — far more than forming a goal intention alone. This is the strongest lever for converting a planned study block into a completed one.
  - _Evidence:_ Gollwitzer & Sheeran's meta-analysis of 94 independent tests found implementation intentions improved goal attainment by d = .65 over goal intentions alone, by pre-specifying the when/where/how and delegating action initiation to the situational cue.
  - _Source:_ Gollwitzer, P. M., & Sheeran, P. (2006). Implementation Intentions and Goal Achievement: A Meta-Analysis of Effects and Processes. Advances in Experimental Social Psychology, 38, 69–119.
  - _Applies to:_ Catch-Up Lane (CatchUpLane/Comeback.tsx) and ComebackEngine.tsx already build an explicit 'if-then plan' — this is exactly the evidence-backed mechanism, so it should be PROMOTED from the comeback-only flow into the main planner: every scheduled block should let the student attach a concrete cue ('when I get home and put my bag down, then I do my 45-min Biology block at the kitchen table').

- **Claim:** Good intentions alone barely move behaviour: intentions explain under ~30% of variance in action, and medium-to-large changes in intention produce only small-to-medium changes in behaviour. A timetable IS a stated intention — so without a cue-binding/volitional layer it will predictably under-deliver. This is the mechanistic answer to 'why doesn't my timetable work'.
  - _Evidence:_ Sheeran & Webb's review synthesises that intentions account for <30% of behavioural variance and that short-term desires, habits and social norms routinely override intentions; concrete, autonomy-framed, mastery-framed, personally-held goals translate better than abstract or socially-pressured ones.
  - _Source:_ Sheeran, P., & Webb, T. L. (2016). The Intention–Behavior Gap. Social and Personality Psychology Compass, 10(9), 503–518.
  - _Applies to:_ planner overall: justifies adding obstacle-anticipation + if-then cue binding to every block; and (DEIS framing per project memory) supports autonomy/mastery copy over performance/social-pressure copy. Streaks (computeStreak) lean on social-norm/loss-aversion pressure — keep them, but pair with personally-held 'why' anchors as ComebackEngine already does (the 'anchor' field).

- **Claim:** Students reliably underestimate how long study tasks take — the planning fallacy. In the classic study, students predicted 33.9 days and took 55.5 (≈64% longer); only 30% finished by even their '99%-certain' date. A planner that asks 'how long will this take?' will be systematically optimistic; the remedy is reference-class / outside-view estimation from the student's own past completions.
  - _Evidence:_ Buehler, Griffin & Ross found students focused on plan-based best-case scenarios and discounted their own relevant past experience, producing large completion-time underestimates that persisted even under high-confidence (99%) instructions.
  - _Source:_ Buehler, R., Griffin, D., & Ross, M. (1994). Exploring the 'Planning Fallacy': Why People Underestimate Their Task Completion Times. Journal of Personality and Social Psychology, 67(3), 366–381.
  - _Applies to:_ timetableAlgorithm.ts already enforces sustainability caps (maxWeeklyHours, weekday/weekend block caps) which guard against over-planning — extend with an OUTSIDE-VIEW feedback loop: compare planned vs actually-completed blocks (the completions data already exists) and gently recalibrate next week's load to the student's real historic completion rate, surfaced honestly ('you usually finish ~6 of 9 — here's a plan you'll actually complete').

- **Claim:** Self-regulated learning is a CYCLE (forethought → performance → self-reflection), where reflection feeds the next plan. A timetable that only does the 'forethought' phase (generate a plan) and skips structured performance-monitoring and reflection is missing two-thirds of the loop — which is why plans drift.
  - _Evidence:_ Zimmerman's cyclical model: forethought (task analysis, goal-setting, planning, motivational beliefs) → performance (self-control + self-monitoring during the task) → self-reflection (self-evaluation, causal attribution), with reflections explicitly feeding back into the next forethought phase.
  - _Source:_ Zimmerman, B. J. (2002). Becoming a Self-Regulated Learner: An Overview. Theory Into Practice, 41(2), 64–70.
  - _Applies to:_ The Launchpad already has the pieces but disconnected: planner = forethought, StudySessionView (mid-session TeachBack/strategy prompts) = performance/monitoring, StudyDebrief = reflection. Closing the loop means routing the debrief's attributions back into next week's plan generation (it partially does via computeStrategyHints) — make the cycle explicit and visible to the student so they see plan→do→reflect→better-plan as one designed loop.

- **Claim:** SKEPTICAL FLAG — Growth mindset, often bundled into study-motivation tools, has a very weak average effect on achievement; don't over-rely on mindset framing as the behaviour-change engine. Reserve any mindset effort for the at-risk/low-SES subgroup, which fits the DEIS audience but should be evidenced, not assumed.
  - _Evidence:_ Sisk et al. (2018) two meta-analyses: mindset–achievement correlation was weak (mindset explained ~1% of achievement variance across 129 studies, N=365,915) and the intervention effect was d ≈ 0.08; effects were only meaningfully present for low-SES or academically at-risk students. Macnamara & Burgoyne (2023) similarly found largely non-significant intervention effects.
  - _Source:_ Sisk, V. F., Burgoyne, A. P., Sun, J., Butler, J. L., & Macnamara, B. N. (2018). To What Extent and Under Which Circumstances Are Growth Mind-Sets Important to Academic Achievement? Two Meta-Analyses. Psychological Science, 29(4), 549–571.
  - _Applies to:_ ComebackEngine.tsx / Catch-Up Lane motivational copy and any 'mindset' module feeding the planner: lean the behaviour-change weight on implementation intentions and retrieval/spacing scheduling (strong evidence) rather than mindset messaging (weak/conditional), while it's defensible to keep light mindset framing specifically for the DEIS/at-risk cohort the platform serves.

- **Claim:** SKEPTICAL FLAG — Gamification (points, badges, streaks) helps, but only with small effects, and the BEHAVIOURAL effects are the least stable; the durable gains come from game-fiction + meaningful social interaction (cooperation+competition), not raw points/leaderboards. Treat streaks as a supporting nudge, not the mechanism.
  - _Evidence:_ Sailer & Homner's meta-analysis found small effects on cognitive (g=0.49), motivational (g=0.36) and behavioural (g=0.25) outcomes; behavioural and motivational effects were the LEAST stable under high-rigour subsplits, and game-fiction + combined competition/collaboration were the significant moderators of behavioural gains. One-off short interventions had negligible effect.
  - _Source:_ Sailer, M., & Homner, L. (2020). The Gamification of Learning: A Meta-Analysis. Educational Psychology Review, 32, 1–36.
  - _Applies to:_ planner streak system (computeStreak, points/skip-session passes, WeeklyGoals.tsx): keep streaks as a light nudge but don't expect them to carry behaviour change; the evidence says invest instead in meaningful narrative ('anchor'/why already in ComebackEngine) and social/relatedness features over isolated points — and avoid letting a broken streak become a punitive de-motivator (loss aversion can backfire for the at-risk cohort).

- **Claim:** SKEPTICAL FLAG — Learning-styles matching ('I'm a visual learner, schedule visual study') has no credible evidence and should never be a scheduling input. The meshing hypothesis (matching instruction to a measured style improves outcomes) fails the few studies that tested it experimentally.
  - _Evidence:_ Pashler, McDaniel, Rohrer & Bjork reviewed the literature and found virtually no studies using the crossover-interaction design needed to validate learning styles, and those that did failed to support it; they concluded there is no adequate evidence base to justify using learning-styles assessments in education.
  - _Source:_ Pashler, H., McDaniel, M., Rohrer, D., & Bjork, R. (2008). Learning Styles: Concepts and Evidence. Psychological Science in the Public Interest, 9(3), 105–119.
  - _Applies to:_ StrategyPickerStep.tsx and any onboarding/North Star step that profiles the learner: do NOT add a 'what kind of learner are you' style quiz to drive session types; drive session types by the technique-utility evidence (retrieval/spacing/interleaving) and by per-subject performance signals (topicMastery, debrief), not by self-reported style.

  **Design principles:** Schedule the EVIDENCE, not the subject. A study block should default to a high-utility action (retrieval practice / spaced review / interleaved problem sets), not a neutral 'study Biology' slot. The planner already has sessionType ('new-learning'|'practice'|'revision') — make 'practice' mean active recall and 'revision' mean spaced retrieval, and tell the student which technique and why (utility tier). (Dunlosky 2013; Roediger & Karpicke 2006) · Bind every block to an if-then cue. The single highest-leverage, best-evidenced upgrade is implementation intentions (d≈0.65): for each scheduled block let the student write 'when [specific cue], then I will [this block, here]'. Promote the if-then plan that already exists in the Comeback / Catch-Up Lane flow into the mainstream planner. (Gollwitzer & Sheeran 2006; Sheeran & Webb 2016) · Close the self-regulation loop in software. Plan (forethought) → in-session monitoring/prompts (performance) → debrief/attribution (reflection) → feed reflection back into next week's plan. Make the cycle visible to the student as one designed loop, so they experience plan→do→reflect→re-plan rather than a one-shot timetable. (Zimmerman 2002) · Correct the fluency illusion with objective signals. Never let self-rated confidence be the only measure of whether a session 'worked' — confidence is exactly the misleading fluency cue. Anchor the debrief to a cold-recall attempt and, where possible, show students where their judgement diverged from their recall so they learn to distrust easy-feeling study. (Kornell & Bjork 2008; Karpicke & Roediger 2008) · Take the outside view on time and load. Assume students will under-estimate task time (~60%+) and over-plan. Use their own completion history (the completions data already stored) to recalibrate next week's plan to their real completion rate, and present it honestly as 'a plan you'll actually finish'. Keep the existing sustainability caps. (Buehler, Griffin & Ross 1994) · Optimise spacing against the exam date, not a fixed week. Widen review gaps when the exam is far and tighten them as it nears — roughly 10–20% of the time-to-test. The SM-2 engine and intensity factor are the right hooks; extend the proximity signal from session COUNT to session GAP. (Cepeda et al. 2006) · Interleave within subjects, and name the difficulty as the point. Mix topic/problem types inside practice blocks (not just subjects across the day), and pre-empt the 'this feels harder/slower' reaction with copy that frames it as a desirable difficulty that buys forgetting-resistance. (Rohrer, Dedrick & Stershic 2015; Kornell & Bjork 2008) · Use gamification as a light nudge, not the engine — and don't let it punish. Streaks/points give small, unstable behavioural effects; the durable motivators are a personal 'why' anchor and relatedness/social features. Guard against a broken streak demotivating the at-risk DEIS cohort (loss aversion can backfire). (Sailer & Homner 2020) · Do not profile learners by 'style', and weight mindset framing modestly. Drive scheduling by technique-utility evidence and per-subject performance, never by self-reported learning style (no evidence). Treat growth-mindset copy as a small, conditional lever justified mainly for the at-risk/low-SES cohort — not the behaviour-change mechanism. (Pashler et al. 2008; Sisk et al. 2018) · Frame for autonomy, mastery and a personal why (DEIS-aligned). The intention-behaviour literature shows concrete, autonomy- and mastery-framed, personally-held goals translate to action better than abstract or socially-pressured ones — which converges with the project's DEIS guidance to avoid exclusionary high-achievement copy. (Sheeran & Webb 2016)

### Exam performance, deliberate practice & metacognition — what makes exam-technique practice transfer to the real Leaving Cert paper (grounding the Launchpad tool

- **Claim:** Practice that retrieves answers from a blank box (generative/free recall) produces far stronger durable learning than re-reading, and is the single best-evidenced lever for exam-technique tools — but the benefit shrinks if practice items are easy to retrieve and the format differs from the real test.
  - _Evidence:_ Adesope, Trevisan & Sundararajan's meta-analysis of 272 effects from 188 experiments found practice testing beats restudying with a weighted mean g = +0.51 (and +0.93 vs no activity). Crucially for design, the effect was MODERATED by format: multiple-choice practice tests showed g = +0.70 vs short-answer/recall g = +0.48. Roediger & Karpicke (2006) showed a single act of retrieval outperforms repeated reading on delayed retention. The format moderator is a genuine caution: Exam Reps uses free-recall/short-answer, which carries a smaller raw effect than MC — its justification is transfer (see TAP finding), not headline effect size.
  - _Source:_ Adesope, Trevisan & Sundararajan (2017), 'Rethinking the Use of Tests: A Meta-Analysis of Practice Testing', Review of Educational Research 87(3), 659-701; Roediger & Karpicke (2006), 'Test-Enhanced Learning: Taking Memory Tests Improves Long-Term Retention', Psychological Science 17(3), 249-255.
  - _Applies to:_ Exam Reps (core mechanic — write-from-a-blank-box generative retrieval). Validates the 'no MCQ, write what you'd actually write' design. Caveat to surface internally: free-recall has a smaller raw effect than MC, so its payoff comes from format-match (transfer), not from retrieval alone — keep reps in authentic written form, never downgrade to multiple choice for engagement.

- **Claim:** Exam-technique practice transfers to the real paper mainly when the practice conditions MATCH the conditions of the real exam (same question wording, same marking grammar, same written-answer format) — generic skill drilling rarely transfers far. This 'transfer-appropriate processing' is the strongest single justification for practising on authentic past-paper questions self-marked against the real scheme.
  - _Evidence:_ Morris, Bransford & Franks (1977) showed memory performance depends on the OVERLAP between encoding and retrieval processing, not absolute depth: rhyme-encoding beat semantic-encoding when the test was a rhyme test. Barnett & Ceci's (2002) far-transfer taxonomy concludes that far transfer (different surface features, context, format) usually fails — 'what you teach is what you get, and where you teach it is where you get it' — while near transfer (shared surface AND structure) succeeds relatively often. Exam technique is therefore best trained on items that share the real paper's surface (command words, marking scheme, written format) as well as its structure.
  - _Source:_ Morris, Bransford & Franks (1977), 'Levels of processing versus transfer-appropriate processing', Journal of Verbal Learning and Verbal Behavior 16(5), 519-533; Barnett & Ceci (2002), 'When and where do we apply what we learn? A taxonomy for far transfer', Psychological Bulletin 128(4), 612-637.
  - _Applies to:_ All three tools, but it is the central design rationale for Exam Reps (real SEC questions, self-marked against the actual marking scheme, written in exam format) and Command-Word Reflex (command words trained ON authentic question stems, not in the abstract). It also flags a risk for Syllabus X-Ray: prioritising topics is meta-strategic and sits far from the act of answering, so it should hand off into rep practice rather than stand alone.

- **Claim:** Committing a confidence rating BEFORE seeing the answer is a defensible calibration mechanic — but be skeptical: practice-test feedback alone does NOT reliably recalibrate the weakest students (who keep overestimating), and can push strong students into underconfidence. Calibration is a long-game habit, not a one-rep fix.
  - _Evidence:_ Osterhage (2021) found in introductory biology that completing practice tests did NOT significantly improve performance predictions for the lowest performers (the Dunning-Kruger pattern persisted; mean discrepancy fell only from 10.0 to 6.6 percentage points and overestimation remained), while high achievers who took practice tests became MORE underconfident (effect size -0.34); combining quartiles, practice-test takers were significantly MORE miscalibrated (p = .02). More broadly, low achievers show larger overconfidence than high achievers, and miscalibration leads students to under-study.
  - _Source:_ Osterhage (2021), 'Persistent Miscalibration for Low and High Achievers Despite Practice Test Feedback in an Introductory Biology Course', Journal of Microbiology & Biology Education 22(2), e00139-21.
  - _Applies to:_ Exam Reps' confidence-before-reveal + the 'you felt surer than the marks landed' calibration line. Keep it, but (a) frame it as a habit tracked over many reps, not a verdict on one; (b) handle the strong-student case — the current copy 'Better than you thought — trust that more' is well-aimed at underconfident high achievers; (c) for persistently overconfident weak students, don't expect the number alone to fix it — pair it with the concrete missed-marks breakdown ('marks on the table') so the gap is visible at the item level, not just as a self-rating.

- **Claim:** Feedback is high-leverage but genuinely double-edged: about one-third of feedback interventions REDUCE performance, especially when they direct attention to the self (the learner's ego/ability) rather than to the task and the next move. Item-level, task-focused, 'here is exactly which marks you missed and why' feedback is the safe, high-yield form.
  - _Evidence:_ Kluger & DeNisi's meta-analysis (607 effect sizes, 23,663 observations) found feedback improved performance on average (d = 0.41) but OVER ONE-THIRD of interventions DECREASED it — feedback that cues attention to the self rather than the task is a primary culprit. Hattie & Timperley (2007) report feedback effects in the 0.70-0.79 range but stress feedback works at the task/process/self-regulation level and is weakest (often harmful) at the 'self' level; a 2019 re-analysis (994 effect sizes, 435 studies) found a more modest mean d = 0.48 with high variability and outliers in BOTH directions.
  - _Source:_ Kluger & DeNisi (1996), 'The effects of feedback interventions on performance: A historical review, a meta-analysis, and a preliminary feedback intervention theory', Psychological Bulletin 119(2), 254-284; Hattie & Timperley (2007), 'The Power of Feedback', Review of Educational Research 77(1), 81-112; Wisniewski, Zierer & Hattie (2020), 'The Power of Feedback Revisited', Frontiers in Psychology 10:3087.
  - _Applies to:_ Exam Reps' self-marking against the scheme + 'the one lesson' debrief, and Command-Word Reflex's 'examiner-flagged trap' reveal. Strong support for the design's task-level focus (which marking-scheme ribbon did you have/not have, what's the single lesson). Design rule to enforce: keep feedback on the mark and the next move; never let copy slide into self/ability judgements ('you're weak at this'). The mistake-first 'marks on the table' framing is correctly task-directed.

- **Claim:** Leading with the wrong answer is evidence-based, not just engaging: making a generative attempt and getting it wrong BEFORE seeing the correct answer (errorful generation / the pretesting effect) enhances later learning, provided corrective feedback follows. This validates the 'mistake-first' pedagogy directly.
  - _Evidence:_ Kornell, Hays & Bjork (2009) showed that unsuccessful retrieval attempts followed by feedback produced BETTER subsequent retention than study-only conditions — failing first is a 'desirable difficulty', not wasted effort. R. Bjork's desirable-difficulties framework explains why effortful, error-prone practice conditions can depress practice-time performance yet improve durable retention and transfer. The boundary condition matters: the attempt must be low-stakes and FOLLOWED BY clear corrective feedback, or the error can stick.
  - _Source:_ Kornell, Hays & Bjork (2009), 'Unsuccessful Retrieval Attempts Enhance Subsequent Learning', Journal of Experimental Psychology: Learning, Memory, and Cognition 35(4), 989-998; Bjork & Bjork (2011/2020), 'Making Things Hard on Yourself, But in a Good Way: Creating Desirable Difficulties to Enhance Learning' / 'Desirable Difficulties in Theory and Practice'.
  - _Applies to:_ The project-wide 'mistake-first' design rule, and specifically Exam Reps (attempt the full answer cold, then self-mark) and Command-Word Reflex (spot the command word / fall for the trap, then the reveal). The evidence requires the corrective feedback to be immediate and clear — both tools satisfy this. Reinforces NOT pre-teaching the answer before the attempt.

- **Claim:** Students lose marks they could have earned by misreading the command word — answering 'describe' when the paper said 'evaluate' caps the score regardless of content quality. Command words are a hidden, teachable curriculum and a high-yield near-transfer target.
  - _Evidence:_ Examining-body guidance and exam-skills syntheses document that command terms (describe / explain / evaluate / discuss) are precise instructions, not synonyms — 'writing a brilliant description when the question asks for evaluation will cap your score, no matter how insightful your answer' — and that students frequently have the knowledge but lose marks on command-word misinterpretation; the recommendation is to teach command verbs explicitly. This matches the Leaving Cert Chief Examiner reports synthesised in the project's own examiner-reports library.
  - _Source:_ Barnett & Ceci (2002) (near-transfer mechanism, as above) applied to command-word decoding; corroborated by examining-body command-word guidance (e.g. AQA command words documentation; ABMA Education, 'A Guide to Examination Command Words', 2014) and the project's SEC Chief Examiner reports library.
  - _Applies to:_ Command-Word Reflex is squarely justified — training command-word recognition on authentic question stems is a NEAR-transfer task (shared surface + structure), exactly the kind Barnett & Ceci say succeeds. Also strengthens Exam Reps' tappable command-word highlight. Design note: keep the practice item ON the real stem (as both tools do) rather than defining the word abstractly, or the near-transfer advantage is lost.

- **Claim:** Be honest about the ceiling of 'practice volume': accumulated deliberate practice explains only a small share of performance variance in education — far less than the popular '10,000 hours' story implies. The lever is the QUALITY and specificity of each rep (authentic, feedback-rich, error-corrected), not the count of reps banked.
  - _Evidence:_ Macnamara, Hambrick & Oswald's (2014) meta-analysis found deliberate practice explained 26% of variance in games, 21% music, 18% sports, but only 4% in education and <1% in professions — concluding deliberate practice is important but 'not as important as has been argued'. A pre-registered re-analysis of Ericsson's original 1993 violin data (Macnamara & Maitra, 2019) failed to replicate the core dose-response finding and found a substantially smaller effect.
