  - _Source:_ Macnamara, Hambrick & Oswald (2014), 'Deliberate Practice and Performance in Music, Games, Sports, Education, and Professions: A Meta-Analysis', Psychological Science 25(8), 1608-1618; Macnamara & Maitra (2019), 'The role of deliberate practice in expert performance: revisiting Ericsson, Krampe & Tesch-Römer (1993)', Royal Society Open Science 6(8), 190327.
  - _Applies to:_ Tempers Exam Reps' 'marks banked all-time' counter and any framing that equates more reps with better outcomes. Keep the bank as a low-pressure motivator, but the product's claim to efficacy should rest on rep QUALITY (authentic items + scheme-accurate self-marking + error correction), not volume. Also a caution against gamifying raw rep-count. For the DEIS audience, frame progress as incremental mark-gain and habit, not a leaderboard of hours.

- **Claim:** Interleaving topics (mixing question types so the student must first decide WHICH approach to use) outperforms blocked practice and is near-immune to forgetting — and it trains the exact skill the real paper demands: choosing a strategy without a cue. This validates Exam Reps handling spacing/interleaving invisibly.
  - _Evidence:_ Rohrer, Dedrick & Stershic (2015), a randomised classroom study with 126 seventh-graders over three months, found a heavier dose of interleaved practice produced higher scores on tests at both one-day and one-month delays, with interleaving giving 'near immunity against forgetting' (scores dropped only ~80% to ~74% across a 30-fold increase in delay). The proposed mechanism is directly exam-relevant: blocked practice lets students infer the method before reading the problem, whereas interleaving forces them to SELECT the right strategy — as they must on a real mixed exam paper.
  - _Source:_ Rohrer, Dedrick & Stershic (2015), 'Interleaved Practice Improves Mathematics Learning', Journal of Educational Psychology 107(3), 900-908.
  - _Applies to:_ Exam Reps' design choice to handle 'spacing/interleaving invisibly; the student never chooses or schedules anything' is well-grounded — but the current code cycles within a single chosen topic ((cardIndex + 1) % activeCards.length). To realise the interleaving benefit, the next-question picker should mix topics/question-types within a subject, not just rotate within one topic. Syllabus X-Ray's topic prioritisation could feed a weighted interleaved queue rather than narrowing practice to one topic.

- **Claim:** Regular low-stakes retrieval practice REDUCES rather than increases test anxiety — directly countering the worry that a 'mark yourself like the examiner' tool will stress DEIS students. And the anxiety that hurts performance is the cognitive 'worry' component, which exposure-style rehearsal and clear task focus can address.
  - _Evidence:_ Agarwal et al. (2014), across 1,408 students aged 11-18, found classroom retrieval-practice programs REDUCED test anxiety: 72% of students reported quizzing made them LESS nervous for unit tests and exams, consistent across content areas, gender, and students receiving special services. von der Embse et al.'s (2018) 30-year meta-analytic review confirms test anxiety negatively relates to performance, and that the COGNITIVE/worry component (r ≈ -.24 to -.32) is more strongly tied to performance than the affective-physiological component — so reducing worry via familiarity and task focus is the right target.
  - _Source:_ Agarwal, D'Antonio, Roediger, McDermott & McDaniel (2014), 'Classroom-based programs of retrieval practice reduce middle school and high school students' test anxiety', Journal of Applied Research in Memory and Cognition 3(3), 131-139; von der Embse, Jester, Roy & Post (2018), 'Test anxiety effects, predictors, and correlates: A 30-year meta-analytic review', Journal of Affective Disorders 227, 483-493.
  - _Applies to:_ Reassurance + design constraint for Exam Reps and Command-Word Reflex: the rep loop should stay LOW-STAKES (the soft non-red timer arc framed as 'your exam budget — finish if you need' is exactly right; do not add hard countdowns, scores-as-grades, or red pressure cues). Repeated authentic-format exposure is itself anxiety-reducing. For the DEIS audience this is a feature: reps build familiarity-driven calm, which the worry-component evidence says is where the performance payoff is.

- **Claim:** Worked examples beat unguided problem-solving for NOVICES (they cut extraneous cognitive load), but the advantage reverses for more competent learners, who learn more from generating answers themselves (the expertise-reversal effect). A one-size practice mode is therefore wrong — support should fade as competence grows.
  - _Evidence:_ Sweller & Cooper established that low-prior-knowledge learners benefit more from studying worked examples than from solving problems, by reducing extraneous load. The expertise-reversal effect (Kalyuga and colleagues) shows worked examples become redundant or harmful for more advanced learners, who benefit more from problem-solving — prior knowledge moderates the size AND direction of the worked-example effect.
  - _Source:_ Sweller & Cooper (1985), 'The use of worked examples as a substitute for problem solving in learning algebra', Cognition and Instruction 2(1), 59-89; Kalyuga, Ayres, Chandler & Sweller (2003), 'The Expertise Reversal Effect', Educational Psychologist 38(1), 23-31.
  - _Applies to:_ Exam Reps currently throws every student straight into a cold generative attempt. For a struggling DEIS student with low prior knowledge on a topic, a worked-example scaffold FIRST (study a fully marked model answer, then attempt) would lower load and improve early learning; for competent students, jump straight to the cold attempt. Use the calibration/capture signal already tracked to FADE the scaffold (faded worked examples → completion problems → full generation). This is a high-value enhancement, not a rebuild.

  **Design principles:** Match the practice to the paper, not to engagement metrics. Transfer-appropriate processing (Morris et al. 1977) and Barnett & Ceci's near-transfer finding mean reps must share the real exam's surface AND structure: authentic SEC questions, written answers in exam format, self-marked against the actual marking scheme. Resist downgrading to multiple-choice for ease — it raises the raw testing-effect number but breaks the transfer that is the whole point. · Make the student generate before they see anything. Errorful generation + the pretesting effect (Kornell, Hays & Bjork 2009) mean the cold attempt is doing the learning work, so never pre-teach the answer or offer hints before the attempt. The corrective feedback that follows must be immediate and clear, or the error sticks — keep the attempt→mark→lesson sequence tight. · Keep all feedback task-level, never self-level. One-third of feedback interventions backfire (Kluger & DeNisi 1996), chiefly when they cue the ego. Speak only about the mark and the next move ('which scheme point you had/missed', 'the one lesson'); ban ability/identity verdicts. The 'marks on the table' framing is correctly task-directed — hold that line everywhere. · Treat calibration as a tracked habit, not a one-rep verdict. Practice-test feedback alone does not recalibrate weak students and can make strong ones underconfident (Osterhage 2021). Pair the confidence rating with a concrete item-level missed-marks breakdown, average calibration over many reps, and tune copy separately for over- vs under-confident cases rather than implying a single rep proves anything. · Sell quality, not volume. Deliberate practice explains only ~4% of variance in education (Macnamara et al. 2014) and the 10,000-hours dose-response failed to replicate (Macnamara & Maitra 2019). Keep rep counters as gentle motivation, but never gamify raw count or hours; the efficacy claim rests on authentic items + scheme-accurate marking + error correction. For DEIS users, frame progress as incremental mark-gain and belonging, not a leaderboard. · Interleave topics inside a subject, invisibly. Interleaving is near-immune to forgetting and trains strategy-selection — the exact skill a mixed real paper demands (Rohrer et al. 2015). The next-question picker should mix topics/question-types within a subject rather than rotating within one topic, and Syllabus X-Ray's priorities can weight that interleaved queue. Keep scheduling invisible — students should never have to plan it. · Keep the loop low-stakes to lower the worry that actually costs marks. Retrieval practice reduces test anxiety (Agarwal et al. 2014) and the worry component is what most hurts performance (von der Embse et al. 2018). The soft, non-red timer framed as a 'budget you can overrun' is correct; never add hard countdowns, red pressure cues, or scores presented as grades. Familiarity-through-reps is itself the anxiety intervention. · Fade the scaffold as competence grows. The worked-example effect helps novices but reverses for the competent (Sweller & Cooper 1985; Kalyuga et al. 2003). Offer a marked model-answer scaffold first for low-prior-knowledge attempts, then fade to completion problems, then to full cold generation — driven by the capture/calibration signal already tracked. One fixed practice mode under-serves both ends of the DEIS ability range. · Train command-word decoding on the real stem. Command-word misreading caps otherwise-good answers, and decoding is a near-transfer skill that succeeds precisely because it shares the paper's surface (examining-body guidance; Barnett & Ceci 2002). Always anchor the command-word lesson to an authentic question (as Command-Word Reflex and Exam Reps' tappable highlight do); never teach the verb in the abstract. · Let meta-strategy hand off into doing. Topic prioritisation (Syllabus X-Ray) is valuable but sits far from the act of answering, where transfer is weakest. Make the prioritiser a launchpad into rep practice — e.g. 'Start a rep on this high-value topic' — so the planning insight cashes out in near-transfer retrieval rather than ending as a static dashboard.

### Motivation, goal-setting & behaviour change — connecting distant outcomes (CAO points, a future life) to present effort in the NextStepUni Launchpad tools.

- **Claim:** Specific + difficult goals beat 'do your best' goals in ~90% of studies, and goal difficulty correlates with performance at r≈0.82 UP TO the limit of ability — but only when commitment and feedback are present; vague aspirations ('do well in the Leaving Cert') produce no reliable lift.
  - _Evidence:_ Locke & Latham's 35-year synthesis reports specific/difficult goals outperformed 'do your best' instructions in ~90% of studies; within ability limits, goal difficulty and performance correlate r≈0.82, but the correlation collapses to ~0.11 once goals exceed ability (i.e. impossible CAO targets demotivate). Effects depend on three moderators: goal commitment, feedback on progress, and task complexity.
  - _Source:_ Locke, E. A., & Latham, G. P. (2002). Building a Practically Useful Theory of Goal Setting and Task Motivation: A 35-Year Odyssey. American Psychologist, 57(9), 705-717.
  - _Applies to:_ Points Passport, College Compass, Your Possible Life. Points Passport should turn a vague 'do better' into a specific, calibrated points target with visible progress feedback. College Compass's 'runway' framing must avoid setting a points goal so far above current mocks that it tips into the demotivating impossible-goal zone — show the gap as a sequence of reachable steps, and let students set their own difficulty.

- **Claim:** Distal goals alone do almost nothing for motivation, competence, self-efficacy or interest; PROXIMAL sub-goals do the heavy lifting. In the foundational experiment, children given near-term sub-goals gained skill, self-efficacy AND intrinsic interest, while a distant end-goal was statistically indistinguishable from no goal at all.
  - _Evidence:_ Bandura & Schunk (1981) gave 7-10 year-olds with maths deficits either proximal sub-goals (one set per session), a distal goal (finish all by the last session), or no goal. Proximal sub-goals produced the highest subtraction skill, self-efficacy AND intrinsic interest; 'distal goals had no demonstrable effects' — they behaved like the no-goal control.
  - _Source:_ Bandura, A., & Schunk, D. H. (1981). Cultivating Competence, Self-Efficacy, and Intrinsic Interest Through Proximal Self-Motivation. Journal of Personality and Social Psychology, 41(3), 586-598.
  - _Applies to:_ This is the central design tension of the whole Launchpad: the tools sell DISTAL outcomes (CAO points, a career, 'Your Possible Life'). The evidence says distal outcomes only motivate when decomposed into proximal sub-goals. Your Possible Life and College Compass must not stop at painting the distant life/runway — each must terminate in a concrete this-week / this-topic sub-goal. Catch-Up Lane ('one quick topic at a time') is already the proximal-sub-goal pattern done right and should be the model the distal tools borrow from.

- **Claim:** Present bias / temporal discounting is the core obstacle: people treat their future self almost like a stranger, so distant rewards (a future career) get steeply discounted against present effort. The fix with the best evidence is increasing 'future self-continuity' — making the future self vivid, similar and positive — which predicts less discounting and more future-oriented behaviour.
  - _Evidence:_ Hershfield et al. found higher future self-continuity predicts reduced discounting of future rewards and greater real-world asset accumulation (controlling for age/education); a randomised experiment showed that letting people interact with vivid, age-progressed renderings of their future selves increased allocation of resources to the future. The construct is measured on similarity, vividness and positivity subscales.
  - _Source:_ Hershfield, H. E., Goldstein, D. G., Sharpe, W. F., Fox, J., Yeykelis, L., Carstensen, L. L., & Bailenson, J. N. (2011). Increasing Saving Behavior Through Age-Progressed Renderings of the Future Self. Journal of Marketing Research, 48(SPL), S23-S37. (See also Ersner-Hershfield et al., 2009, Judgment and Decision Making.)
  - _Applies to:_ Your Possible Life is precisely a future-self-continuity intervention. The evidence says its job is to make the future self VIVID, SIMILAR and POSITIVE (concrete day-in-the-life detail, an attainable not fantastical self, an aspirational-but-warm tone) and then immediately bridge to a present action — otherwise discounting reasserts itself the moment the student closes the tool. Vividness without a present-action bridge changes feeling, not behaviour.

- **Claim:** Simply imagining a positive future BACKFIRES if it is pure positive fantasy; the evidence-backed protocol is Mental Contrasting (vividly imagine the wish AND the obstacle) joined to Implementation Intentions (an if-then plan) — 'WOOP'. Combined, it yields a small-to-medium effect on goal attainment across academic, health and personal domains.
  - _Evidence:_ A meta-analysis of 24 independent trials (N≈15,907) found Mental Contrasting with Implementation Intentions (MCII/WOOP) produced g≈0.34 on goal attainment across domains, including academic; mental contrasting works by energising effort proportional to expectation of success. Separately, Gollwitzer & Sheeran's meta-analysis of 94 tests found if-then implementation intentions alone had a medium-to-large effect (d=0.65) on goal attainment.
  - _Source:_ Wang, G., Wang, Y., & Gai, X. (2021). A Meta-Analysis of the Effects of Mental Contrasting With Implementation Intentions on Goal Attainment. Frontiers in Psychology, 12, 565202. AND Gollwitzer, P. M., & Sheeran, P. (2006). Implementation Intentions and Goal Achievement: A Meta-Analysis of Effects and Processes. Advances in Experimental Social Psychology, 38, 69-119.
  - _Applies to:_ Your Possible Life should NOT end on the dream alone (pure positive fantasy is the documented failure mode). Add the contrast step ('what's the obstacle this week?') and an if-then plan ('IF it's Tuesday 7pm THEN I do one Exam Rep'). This is a small, cheap, high-leverage addition with stronger evidence than the inspirational vision itself. Catch-Up Lane and Points Passport can also bolt an if-then trigger onto each committed sub-goal.

- **Claim:** Connecting learning to a student's own life (utility value) reliably raises interest AND grades — and the effect is LARGEST for the students with low confidence/low expectations, exactly NextStepUni's DEIS audience. Crucially it works best when students GENERATE the connection themselves, not when relevance is asserted at them.
  - _Evidence:_ Hulleman & Harackiewicz's randomised intervention had students write about how course content related to their own lives (vs. writing a summary). It raised interest and performance, with significantly stronger effects for students who held low expectations of success; the self-generated framing is what carried the effect.
  - _Source:_ Hulleman, C. S., & Harackiewicz, J. M. (2009). Promoting Interest and Performance in High School Science Classes. Science, 326(5958), 1410-1412. (See also Harackiewicz, Canning et al., 2016, Journal of Personality and Social Psychology.)
  - _Applies to:_ Future Finder (RIASEC matching), Exploring Options/Career Paths and Your Possible Life are all utility-value engines. The design implication: don't just TELL students 'this course is useful' — prompt them to write/select in their own words why a matched course or career matters to them. A short self-generated 'why this matters to me' step on the Future Finder results screen is a higher-evidence feature than a longer ranked list, and it is disproportionately valuable for low-confidence DEIS students.

- **Claim:** Growth-mindset interventions are real but SMALL and conditional — not the panacea early hype claimed. The correlational link to achievement is tiny (≈1% of variance) and average intervention effects are near-trivial (d≈0.08). Copy that simply chants 'believe you can grow' is not evidence-based.
  - _Evidence:_ Sisk, Burgoyne, Sun, Butler & Macnamara's two meta-analyses (273 studies; N=365,915) found mindset explained ~1% of achievement variance, and the mean intervention effect was d=0.08 (moving the average student from the 50th to ~53rd percentile). Effects were inconsistent and often null.
  - _Source:_ Sisk, V. F., Burgoyne, A. P., Sun, J., Butler, J. L., & Macnamara, B. N. (2018). To What Extent and Under Which Circumstances Are Growth Mind-Sets Important to Academic Achievement? Two Meta-Analyses. Psychological Science, 29(4), 549-571.
  - _Applies to:_ How They Did It, North Star onboarding and any motivational framing across the Launchpad. SKEPTICAL guardrail: do not lean the Launchpad's value proposition on generic growth-mindset slogans — the effect is small and the slogan-only version doesn't replicate. Use mindset language sparingly and concretely (tie 'ability grows' to a specific strategy the student can act on), not as decorative motivational copy.

- **Claim:** WHERE growth-mindset DOES work narrows the design precisely: a brief, well-targeted intervention raised grades specifically for LOWER-ACHIEVING students (+0.10 GPA; ~5pp fewer D/F averages) and lifted advanced-maths take-up — but ONLY when the school/peer climate supported challenge-seeking. The norm context is a make-or-break moderator.
  - _Evidence:_ Yeager et al.'s National Study of Learning Mindsets (nationally representative; ~12,490 9th-graders) raised core-course GPA by 0.10 grade points for lower-achievers (0.15-0.17 where peer norms were supportive), cut D/F averages by >5pp (8pp in supportive medium/low-performing schools), and raised Algebra-II+ enrolment ~3pp. Effects appeared only where peer norms backed pursuing challenging work.
  - _Source:_ Yeager, D. S., Hanselman, P., Walton, G. M., et al. (2019). A national experiment reveals where a growth mindset improves achievement. Nature, 573, 364-369.
  - _Applies to:_ Directly validates NextStepUni's DEIS focus and How They Did It's relatable-peer model: the lift is concentrated in lower-achievers and depends on a supportive PEER NORM. Implication: surface peer/belonging norms ('students like you here are taking on harder work / aiming for college') rather than lone-hero mindset messaging. The tools should normalise challenge-seeking as what one's peers do, because the same intervention is inert without that norm.

- **Claim:** Role models / 'real people who started where you are' only inspire when they are perceived as SIMILAR and their success as ATTAINABLE; models that are too exceptional or dissimilar can BACKFIRE, depressing rather than raising aspiration, especially for minority/first-gen students. Relatability beats prestige.
  - _Evidence:_ Reviews of the motivational theory of role modelling find perceived similarity and attainability of the model's success drive positive effects; aspirational-but-distant models can backfire, and perceived similarity mediates whether minority students are inspired vs. discouraged. Effective models are 'competent but not exceptionally so, similar in demographic/psychological ways, and approachable, with attainable achievements'.
  - _Source:_ Morgenroth, T., Ryan, M. K., & Peters, K. (2015). The Motivational Theory of Role Modeling. Review of General Psychology, 19(4), 465-483. AND Gladstone & Cimpian (2021), 'Which role models are effective for which students?', International Journal of STEM Education, 8, 59.
  - _Applies to:_ How They Did It and Exploring Options. The data files should foreground people demographically/contextually SIMILAR to DEIS students (same kind of school, started behind, not naturally gifted prodigies) and frame their wins as attainable steps, not exceptional talent. A profile of a stellar all-A1 student can actively demotivate the median user — choose 'started where you are' over 'top of the class'.

- **Claim:** Gamification has a genuine but SMALL effect (largest on cognitive learning g≈0.49, smaller on motivation g≈0.36, smallest and least robust on behaviour g≈0.25), and the motivational/behavioural effects are statistically fragile under high methodological rigour. It is not free motivation — it helps modestly and only sometimes.
  - _Evidence:_ Sailer & Homner's meta-analysis found cognitive g=0.49 [0.30,0.69], motivational g=0.36 [0.18,0.54], behavioural g=0.25 [0.04,0.46]; the cognitive effect held under high-rigour subsplits but motivational and behavioural effects were 'less stable' — i.e. shaky.
  - _Source:_ Sailer, M., & Homner, L. (2020). The Gamification of Learning: A Meta-Analysis. Educational Psychology Review, 32, 77-112.
  - _Applies to:_ The platform's journey points, quests, achievements, Island Shop, weekly challenges and kudos. Set expectations honestly: gamification will give a modest cognitive/engagement bump, not transform motivation. Invest in the parts the evidence supports (meaningful feedback on competence/progress) over shallow point-chasing, and don't assume the badge layer is doing motivational work it probably isn't.

- **Claim:** Self-Determination Theory explains WHICH gamification helps vs. harms: elements that feed competence, autonomy and relatedness (progress feedback, meaningful narrative, teammates, choice) support intrinsic motivation; bolt-on Badges/Points/Leaderboards ('BPL') that reward behaviour students would do anyway can SHIFT the locus of motivation from internal to external and crowd out intrinsic interest.
  - _Evidence:_ Sailer et al.'s experimental study isolating game elements found badges/leaderboards/performance-graphs supported competence and perceived task-meaningfulness, while avatars, a meaningful story and teammates supported relatedness — i.e. different elements serve different SDT needs, and indiscriminate BPL does not reliably satisfy competence. SDT holds that controlling external rewards undermine autonomy and intrinsic motivation.
  - _Source:_ Sailer, M., Hense, J. U., Mayr, S. K., & Mandl, H. (2017). How gamification motivates: An experimental study of the effects of specific game design elements on psychological need satisfaction. Computers in Human Behavior, 69, 371-380. (Grounded in Ryan & Deci, 2000, Self-Determination Theory, American Psychologist.)
  - _Applies to:_ Journey points / quests / Island Shop / kudos. Design rule: tie rewards to competence growth and give the student autonomy (choice of quest, self-set goals), use relatedness elements (kudos, shared progress) for belonging — and AVOID rewarding intrinsically interesting exploration (e.g. Future Finder, Your Possible Life) with points, which risks crowding out the very curiosity those tools depend on.

- **Claim:** Leaderboards and competitive ranking are the most dangerous gamification element for this audience: a longitudinal classroom study found students in the gamified (leaderboard + badges) condition declined in motivation, satisfaction and empowerment OVER TIME relative to controls, and macro-leaderboards specifically harm lower performers by accumulating felt failure.
  - _Evidence:_ Hanus & Fox's 16-week longitudinal study (two course sections) found the leaderboard+badge section showed LESS motivation, satisfaction and empowerment over time than the non-gamified section, with social comparison as a mechanism. Subsequent work shows overall-ranking leaderboards harm lower-ranked users while only mid/upper ranks feel success.
  - _Source:_ Hanus, M. D., & Fox, J. (2015). Assessing the effects of gamification in the classroom: A longitudinal study on intrinsic motivation, social comparison, satisfaction, effort, and academic performance. Computers & Education, 80, 152-161.
  - _Applies to:_ Strong WARNING for Points Passport and any platform leaderboard/ranking. For DEIS students who often start behind, a global ranking is a repeated public failure signal that demotivates exactly the students NextStepUni most wants to help. Prefer self-referential progress ('you vs. your last mock', personal-best streaks) over peer ranking; if any leaderboard exists, scope it to small self-selected groups or effort metrics, never global grade rank.

- **Claim:** Learning styles ('I'm a visual/auditory learner, teach me my way') are a debunked neuromyth with no credible supporting evidence — yet belief persists. Designing tools or copy around matching content to a student's 'style' wastes effort and lends false authority.
  - _Evidence:_ Pashler et al.'s commissioned review found that despite the popularity of the meshing hypothesis, virtually no studies used the adequate (crossover-interaction) experimental design, and those that did failed to find the predicted matching benefit; the authors concluded there is no adequate evidence base to justify learning-styles instruction. Belief nonetheless remains widespread among educators.
  - _Source:_ Pashler, H., McDaniel, M., Rohrer, D., & Bjork, R. (2008). Learning Styles: Concepts and Evidence. Psychological Science in the Public Interest, 9(3), 105-119.
  - _Applies to:_ Future Finder / North Star onboarding / any 'how you learn' profiling. SKEPTICAL guardrail: do NOT add a 'learning style' quiz or tailor content delivery to a self-reported style — it has no evidence and signals pseudoscience. Profile genuinely useful, evidence-bearing dimensions instead (RIASEC interests, current attainment, subject set, goals), which Future Finder already does correctly.

  **Design principles:** Always terminate a distal vision in a proximal sub-goal. Bandura & Schunk show distal goals alone behave like no goal; every Launchpad tool that paints a future (Your Possible Life, College Compass, Exploring Options) must end on a concrete this-week / this-topic action the student commits to, the way Catch-Up Lane already does ('one quick topic at a time'). · Treat present bias as the enemy and build a vividness-to-action bridge. Your Possible Life should make the future self vivid, similar and positive (Hershfield) AND immediately convert that feeling into an if-then plan before the student leaves the screen — vividness without an action bridge changes mood, not behaviour. · Add the obstacle and the if-then plan, not just the dream. Pure positive fantasy backfires; WOOP/MCII (mental contrasting + implementation intentions, g≈0.34) and if-then plans (d=0.65) are cheap, higher-evidence additions than the inspirational vision alone. Prompt 'what could get in the way this week, and IF that happens THEN I will…'. · Make students generate the relevance, don't assert it. Utility-value works best self-generated and helps low-confidence students most (Hulleman & Harackiewicz) — add a short 'why this matters to me' free-text/selection step to Future Finder and Career Paths results rather than only ranking options at the student. · Calibrate goal difficulty to just-above-current, never to a demotivating fantasy target. Locke & Latham: hard specific goals help up to the limit of ability, then collapse. In Points Passport / College Compass, let students set targets, show the gap as reachable steps, and pair every target with progress feedback (feedback is a required moderator, not optional polish). · Default to self-referential progress; avoid global leaderboards and grade ranking. Hanus & Fox show competitive gamification erodes motivation over time and macro-leaderboards punish lower performers — for a DEIS audience starting behind this is actively harmful. Use personal-best streaks, 'you vs. your last mock', and small-group or effort-based comparison instead. · Spend gamification budget on competence/autonomy/relatedness, not bolt-on points. Per SDT and Sailer et al., reward genuine competence growth, give real choice (self-set quests/goals), and use relatedness features (kudos, shared progress) for belonging; never gamify intrinsically curious exploration (Future Finder, Your Possible Life) with extrinsic points, which risks crowding out the curiosity those tools rely on. · Choose relatable, attainable role models over high-achieving ones. Models too exceptional or dissimilar backfire (Morgenroth; Gladstone & Cimpian). How They Did It and Exploring Options should foreground people who started behind, attended similar (DEIS) schools, and won through attainable steps — not all-A1 prodigies, who demotivate the median user. · Lead with peer NORMS of challenge-seeking and belonging, not lone-hero mindset slogans. The Yeager National Study shows mindset effects appear only where peers normalise pursuing hard work; frame the Launchpad as 'students like you here aim for college / take on harder subjects' rather than individualistic 'just believe in yourself' copy. · Be honest about effect sizes and resist motivational pseudoscience. Growth-mindset (d≈0.08 average, ~1% variance) and gamification (g≈0.25-0.49) give modest, conditional lifts — design for them, but don't overclaim. Never add a 'learning styles' quiz or style-matched content delivery: it is a debunked neuromyth (Pashler et al.) and signals false authority. Profile evidence-bearing dimensions (interests, attainment, goals) instead.

## Tool-by-tool: current depth + proposed concepts

### `future-finder-revamped`
Current — content depth **4/5**, functionality depth **4/5**. A RIASEC-grounded CAO course-recommendation tool, fully wired into InnovationZone (lazy-loaded, registered, gated on a subject profile). Flow: intro screen lets the student pick "Full" (60 interest + 12 work-value items = 72 taps, ~9 min) or "Quick" (30 interest + 12 value = 42 taps, ~5 min). An activity-card quiz then steps through each item on a 5-point dislike→like / not-important→very-important scale with auto-advance, a back button, a progress bar, and a double-tap/overshoot guard. Likert answers are summed per Holland scale into a six-number interest profile; the top three define the student's RIASEC code. Each of 149 RIASEC-coded courses is scored on three INDEPENDENT axes that are never blended: interest fit (Pearson correlation of the student's six-number profile against the course's, the O*NET method, displayed as a 0-100 Iachan's-M congruence %), work-values congruence, and a points "reach" badge derived from the student's auto-computed best-six points vs the course's typical points. Results render through the shared FutureFinderResults UI (ranked cards, per-course detail, save-to-picks, up-to-3 compare table, region filter, match/points sort) plus a profile read-back (top-3 lean + a six-bar RIASEC chart + top work values), a bespoke RIASEC explainer modal, a "Explore as careers" hand-off to Career Paths, and an honest "snapshot, not a verdict" framing throughout. State persists to its own Firestore namespace (futureFinderRevamped) with a fresh getDoc-on-mount so saved results survive re-entry.
  - _Thinness:_ Hardcoded counts surfaced in UI copy: '72 taps · ~9 min' and '42 taps · ~5 min' are literal strings in the intro; quiz length is fixed at 72/42 items.; Static item bank: RIASEC_ITEMS is a hand-written array of exactly 60 items (10 per scale via R()/I()/A()/S()/E()/C() helpers) + VALUE_ITEMS of exactly 12; `riasecItems(quickOnly)` is just `.filter(i => i.quick)`.; Data-reuse / collision: 149 course records but only 69 distinct riasecProfile objects and 39 distinct riasecCodes (grep uniq counts) — same profile literal repeated up to 12 times.; Course catalogue is a fixed list of 151 entries in futureFinderData.ts (CAO_COURSES); 149 have RIASEC metadata, 2 are unmatched and silently dropped by the `if (!cr) return null` filter.; Results hardcoded-sliced: `.slice(0, 24)` then `.slice(0, 30)` then `.slice(0, 10)` — only 10 cards ever shown.; No TODO/FIXME/placeholder/dummy strings in the revamped files (grep clean) — but the data-file header text self-describes the RIASEC coding as needing a human ratification pass ('recommended final 10%') and the item-bank header defers gender-balance screening to 'once the tool has been live'.; requiredSubjects is defined in the type but set on zero courses, so the eligibility axis is inert (always eligible).; feasibility/reach is a 5-value lookup table (REACH_FEASIBILITY) mapping bucket → fixed 0.15/0.45/0.75/1 score, not a continuous function.; Two scoring systems coexist: futureFinderAlgorithm.ts (539 lines, tag+scenario+slider model) is legacy/unused except for a type import; FutureFinderResults.tsx is a verbatim duplicate of the original tool's UI (per its own header).
  - _Gaps:_ Item bank is fixed and never adapts: every student sees the same 72 (or 42) items in the same hardcoded order — no shuffling, no adaptive/branching item selection, no per-scale early-stopping. The 'quick' form is a static filter on the `quick: true` flag, not a computed short form.; Course RIASEC profiles are coarse and heavily collided: only 69 unique six-number profile shapes and 39 unique 3-letter codes across 149 courses, so many distinct courses are scored identically (e.g. the profile {R:6,I:9,...,C:3} is shared by 12 courses, {R:9,I:6,...} by 11). Two very different courses with the same code get the same interest %, limiting discrimination among the top matches.; The header itself flags that the RIASEC coding is AI-rater-generated and explicitly recommends a human guidance-counsellor ratification pass as 'the final 10%' — that human verification has not happened.; Item bank header concedes O*NET's gender endorsement-balance screening (no male-female mean difference > .30) was NOT done — it requires live response data and is deferred. So the stereotype-avoidance is design-intent, not validated.; The 149-course catalogue is a curated teaching set, not the full CAO list (~1,400+ courses); whole institutions/fields are absent, so a student's true best-fit course may simply not be present.; Results are capped: scored list sliced to 24, then 30, then displayed top 10 — fine for UX but means the long tail of fits is invisible and the region filter operates only on the pre-sliced 30.; Work-values layer is thin relative to interests: only 12 items feeding 6 values (2 each), top-3 taken by raw sum with RIASEC-order tie-breaking; values congruence is a simple set-overlap ratio.; No subject/eligibility gating is actually exercised: the engine supports requiredSubjects but no course in the data defines any (requiredSubjects is absent everywhere), so eligibility is always 'eligible' and the 'Check requirements' warning path for hard gates never fires from real data.; Carries notable dead/legacy weight: the entire tag/scenario-based futureFinderAlgorithm.ts (539 lines, the OLD scoring model) is still imported only for its RecommendationResult type, and FutureFinderResults.tsx is an explicitly VERBATIM copy of the original tool's results layer (884 lines) pending the old Future Finder's retirement.

- **Why It Fits Me — a self-generated relevance + this-week mastery layer on the results** [M] — _verdict: ?, score ?_
  - Future Finder stops being a one-shot quiz that hands back a ranked list and becomes a guided reflection-and-action loop where the student writes, in their own words, why a top-match course could matter to their life, names one concrete step to take this week, and checks it off — turning a 'snapshot' into a small mastery experience.
  - _Content:_ Add an authored, branching set of utility-value writing scaffolds keyed to the student's top-3 RIASEC lean and chosen course, so the prompt is never generic: e.g. for a Social-lean student looking at Nursing, 'Think of a time you helped someone who was struggling — what did that feel like, and how might that connect to this?' (the tool seeds the angle from RIASEC, the STUDENT supplies the content). Author ~3 prompt variants per Holland letter (18 total) plus a small bank of 'this-week step' starter examples grounded in real exploration actions a 15-18yo can actually do without money or a parent's help (look up the course's open-day date, find one person who does this job on a school site, read the first-year module list on the college site, ask your guidance counsellor one named question). Add a short, light 'difficulty is expected' reframe card shown only when a student rates a top-match course as a points stretch — 'finding this hard to picture doesn't mean it's not for you; it means you're exploring something new' — kept deliberately small and non-preachy.
  - _Functionality:_ After results render, surface a free-text 'In your own words: why might this matter to you?' field on a saved course (the student must generate it; the tool never asserts the relevance) plus a single checkable 'my step this week' commitment with a soft, dateless reminder on next entry ('Last time you said you'd look up the open day — did you?'). Persist these to the existing futureFinderRevamped Firestore namespace alongside picks. A tiny 'steps taken' tally accrues as visible incremental progress (not a streak, not a score). The written 'why' becomes a re-readable note attached to each saved pick, and is offered as a prompt the student can bring to a guidance-counsellor conversation (a 'print/show my notes' read-back).
  - _Science:_ Having students write in their OWN words how material connects to their personal life raises interest and grades, with effects LARGEST for low-expectancy/low-performing students — and self-generated relevance outperforms being told the relevance. (Hulleman, C. S., & Harackiewicz, J. M. (2009). 'Promoting interest and performance in high school science classes.' Science, 326(5958), 1410-1412.) | Self-efficacy is the most movable and strongest mediator of realistic career choice, and it is moved most by mastery experiences — small, completed wins — above verbal persuasion. (Bandura, A. (1997). 'Self-Efficacy: The Exercise of Control.' W. H. Freeman; and Lent, Brown & Hackett (1994), Journal of Vocational Behavior, 45(1), 79-122.) | A vivid future self only changes behaviour when paired with a feared self and backed by concrete, plausible strategies that bridge today to that future; aspiration without a this-week pathway is inert. The 'School-to-Jobs' RCT produced sustained gains precisely because it linked possible selves to feasible strategies and reframed difficulty as expected. (Oyserman, D., Bybee, D., & Terry, K. (2006). 'Possible selves and academic outcomes.' Journal of Personality and Social Psychology, 91(1), 188-204.) | Career interventions get larger effects as they stack the five critical ingredients; written exercises and individualised feedback are two of the five, and across both meta-analyses no study contained all five — so adding the written-exercise + feedback ingredients is high-leverage. (Brown, S. D., & Ryan Krane, N. E. (2003). Journal of Vocational Behavior, 62(3), 411-428; Whiston, Li, Mitts & Wright (2017), Journal of Vocational Behavior, 100, 175-184.)
  - _App-in-its-own-right:_ This turns a matcher into a personal career-exploration journal: a re-takeable interest profile PLUS a running set of the student's own written reasons and small completed exploration steps. That is the spine of a standalone 'figure out your future, one small step at a time' app — the written notes and step history are durable personal content a student returns to over a school year, not a one-off result screen.
  - _DEIS framing:_ Directly serves the not-yet-ambitious DEIS student: the gap is knowledge and a believable next move, not aspiration. The student writes their OWN reason (belonging, 'true to me'), the steps are concrete and cost-free, and the 'difficulty is expected' reframe pre-empts the 'this isn't for someone like me' read. Self-efficacy rises through a real completed action, not exhortation.

- **The Other Way In — same-field PLC / apprenticeship / Level 6-7 pathway expansion (anti-circumscription)** [L] — _verdict: maybe, score 0.73_
  - When a high-interest course is a points stretch or 'out of reach', Future Finder refuses to let the points number quietly delete the field — instead it surfaces the SAME-field lower-points routes (PLC-to-degree, apprenticeship, Level 6/7 with progression) so a student sees a real door into the thing that fits them, not a closed one.
  - _Content:_ Author the missing same-field alternative routes that the catalogue currently lacks: the data file already has a pathwayType field ('cao' | 'plc' | 'apprenticeship') that is effectively unused, and whole lower-points routes into the popular fields are absent. Add ~40-60 hand-coded PLC, apprenticeship, and Level 6/7 entries (e.g. Pre-Nursing PLC -> degree, ICT/engineering/electrical apprenticeships via SOLAS/eCollege, Healthcare Assistant -> nursing, business at Level 7 with add-on to Level 8), each tagged with the SAME RIASEC profile/code as its degree counterpart and an honest, sourced 'how you actually get from here to there' note (PLC progression links, apprenticeship earn-while-you-learn framing, typical duration). Group alternatives under each reach/out-of-reach degree by shared RIASEC code so the link is field-true, and write neutral pros/cons (apprenticeship = paid + practical, slower to a degree; PLC = a year, then progression) rather than ranking them below the degree.
  - _Functionality:_ On any result card bucketed 'reach' or 'out-of-reach', add a 'See other ways into this field' expander that pulls same-RIASEC-code, lower-points courses from the expanded catalogue and shows them as a small route map (PLC/apprenticeship -> add-on -> the field) — NOT mixed into the headline interest ranking, preserving the cardinal Fit/Reach separation. The 'open' reach bucket (points<=0, already in the engine) becomes the de-circumscription surface: these routes always render with full interest-fit %, never a demoted score. Add a lightweight 'show me a way in for under [my points]' filter that re-queries the catalogue for the student's own field-of-interest at routes they're eligible for today, and fix the current pre-slice bug so the region/route filter operates on the full scored list, not the first 30.
  - _Science:_ Adolescents prematurely eliminate whole occupational fields by perceived prestige/ability ceiling BEFORE considering interests, and field of interest is sacrificed FIRST in compromise — so a tool that lets a points number delete a high-interest field actively reinforces the most damaging part of the developmental process. Disadvantaged youth are disproportionately prone to this aspiration-attainment misalignment. (Gottfredson, L. S. (1981). 'Circumscription and compromise: A developmental theory of occupational aspirations.' Journal of Counseling Psychology, 28(6), 545-579; Behavioural Insights Team & The Careers & Enterprise Company (2018), 'Moments of Choice'.) | Never blend interest-fit with points into one score; keep Fit/Reach/Eligibility independent and always surface the same-field lower-points/PLC/apprenticeship route beside any 'reach' course — this is the concrete antidote to circumscription for a DEIS audience. (Gottfredson, L. S. (1981), op. cit.; applied via O*NET Interest Profiler matching method, O*NET IP Manual.) | For disadvantaged young people the binding constraint is the knowledge/attainment gap — concrete pathway knowledge, costs, and 'where to get help' — not low aspiration; interventions that just inflate aspiration are ineffective or worse. (Gorard, S., See, B. H., & Davies, P. (2012). 'The impact of attitudes and aspirations on educational attainment and participation.' Joseph Rowntree Foundation.) | Occupational/world-of-work information is one of the five critical career-intervention ingredients; showing the real route in (PLC progression, apprenticeship structure) delivers that ingredient concretely. (Brown, S. D., & Ryan Krane, N. E. (2003). Journal of Vocational Behavior, 62(3), 411-428.)
  - _App-in-its-own-right:_ Expanded with the alternative-routes catalogue and a 'find a way in for my points' query, this becomes a standalone 'every route into the field you want' explorer — the thing no single CAO list gives a DEIS student: not just 'here's the 600-point degree' but the PLC, apprenticeship, and Level 6/7 ladders into the same field, with honest pros/cons. That is a defensible app on its own.
  - _DEIS framing:_ This is the single most DEIS-aligned move: it operationalises de-circumscription. A not-yet-ambitious student whose best-fit field is a 550-point degree is shown a paid apprenticeship or a one-year PLC into the SAME field, framed as a real door, not a consolation prize — directly closing the pathway-knowledge gap the evidence says actually constrains this cohort.
  - _Evidence check:_ All four sources verified REAL and on-point. Gottfredson, L. S. (1981), Circumscription and compromise, Journal of Counseling Psychology 28(6):545-579 — confirmed (udel.edu reprint, ERIC EJ254738); characterization accurate. Gorard, See & Davies (2012), JRF — confirmed (Univ. of Birmingham research portal); the search corroborates its actual thesis that raising aspirations is ineffective and the constraint is structural/knowledge, which is precisely the direction the concept uses it for (strongest anchor, correctly directed). Brown & Ryan Krane (2003), Journal of Vocational Behavior 62(3):411-428 — confirmed (ERIC EJ670780); "critical ingredients" finding real, though the concept's "occupational/world-of-work information" only loosely maps to its actual five ingredients (written occupational exposure being the closest) — minor looseness, not a misuse. BIT / Careers & Enterprise Company "Moments of Choice" — confirmed real, but it is dated 2016, NOT 2018 as the concept cites; minor citation-year error, substance supports the claim. No fabricated or inverted citations. Evidence direction matches the literature throughout. Caveat that is NOT a citation issue but a factual one: the concept's claims about the codebase state (pathwayType unused; routes/RIASEC tags absent) are contradicted by the actual files.

- **Try It On — an interest-EXPANDER that surfaces hexagon-adjacent and counter-stereotypical near-matches with a 'who actually does this' near-peer model** [M] — _verdict: ?, score ?_
  - Instead of confirming a tidy top-3 and the obvious courses, Future Finder deliberately offers a few high-fit near-misses just outside the student's headline code — including counter-stereotypical ones — each paired with a relatable near-peer/DEIS voice saying 'I do this and here's what it's actually like', so the tool widens exploration rather than narrowing it.
  - _Content:_ Author a small bank of short, similar-model micro-profiles (12-20) — near-peer, local, DEIS/ordinary-background voices, NOT distant celebrities — each tagged to a RIASEC code and deliberately including counter-stereotypical pairings (a young woman in an engineering apprenticeship; a fella in early-years/care; a quiet student thriving in an Enterprising role). Each is ~3-4 honest lines: what a typical day is, what surprised them, one hard bit. Also author 'adjacent direction' copy for each hexagon-neighbour relationship (e.g. 'You lean Investigative — Realistic sits right beside it; people who like figuring out WHY also often like building the thing'), so a near-match is explained, not just listed. This reuses the project's existing immersiveDeck 'How They Did It' modelling content language and can cross-link to it.
  - _Functionality:_ Add a 'directions just outside your top 3' strip to the results that computes hexagon-adjacent codes from the student's profile and surfaces 2-3 high-interest-fit courses whose code shares an adjacent letter (rewarding near-matches the way the science argues for), explicitly flagging counter-stereotypical high-fit options. Tapping a near-match opens the matched near-peer micro-profile (vicarious model) and a one-line 'why this is worth a look'. Add a re-take invitation that is meaningful, not cosmetic: store the previous profile and, on re-take, show 'what shifted' so the student SEES that interests move and the result is a snapshot — reinforcing moratorium/exploration over a locked verdict. The adjacency strip is kept visually distinct from the ranked list so it reads as 'explore', not 'ranked below'.
  - _Science:_ These are exploration-stage 15-18s; a tidy single answer risks identity FORECLOSURE (commitment without exploration, the developmentally unsuccessful status). The right posture is moratorium-supporting — try many directions, hold them loosely, re-take — which argues for option-expanding, hexagon-adjacent, re-takeable design. (Marcia, J. E. (1966). 'Development and validation of ego-identity status.' Journal of Personality and Social Psychology, 3(5), 551-558; Super, D. E. (1980), Journal of Vocational Behavior, 16(3), 282-298.) | Adolescent interests are not yet stable (rank-order stability only r ~ .64 by ages 18-22) and congruence is only a weak-to-moderate predictor of satisfaction, so a matcher must present a RANGE and invite re-taking rather than over-claim a verdict. (Low, K. S. D., Yoon, M., Roberts, B. W., & Rounds, J. (2005). 'The stability of vocational interests from early adolescence to middle adulthood.' Psychological Bulletin, 131(5), 713-737; Tinsley, H. E. A. (2000), Journal of Vocational Behavior, 56(2), 147-179.) | Vicarious experience (modelling) raises self-efficacy MOST when the model is perceived as similar to the self — so relatable near-peer/local/DEIS journeys, not distant success stories, are what make 'someone like me does this' land. Modelling is one of the five critical career-intervention ingredients. (Bandura, A. (1997). 'Self-Efficacy: The Exercise of Control.' W. H. Freeman; Brown & Ryan Krane (2003), Journal of Vocational Behavior, 62(3), 411-428.) | There are large sex differences on the Things-People interest dimension (d ~ 0.93), so a naive matcher amplifies gender-stereotyped routing unless it deliberately surfaces relatable counter-stereotypical high-fit options and uses activity-based items. (Su, R., Rounds, J., & Armstrong, P. I. (2009). 'Men and things, women and people: A meta-analysis of sex differences in interests.' Psychological Bulletin, 135(6), 859-884.)
  - _App-in-its-own-right:_ With a model bank, hexagon-adjacency exploration, and a re-take 'what shifted' history, the tool becomes a standalone 'explore who you could be' app: a re-takeable interest map that always opens new doors and shows real people on the other side of them — distinct from a static one-shot quiz, and a natural front door to the wider Launchpad (How They Did It, Career Paths).
  - _DEIS framing:_ Squarely inclusive: counter-stereotypical near-peer models tell a not-yet-ambitious DEIS student 'someone like me does this', the strongest self-efficacy lever for this audience; widening to adjacent directions resists both gendered circumscription and premature foreclosure; and the re-take 'snapshot, interests move' framing protects against a single discouraging result reading as 'this is who you are, full stop'.

### `career-paths`
Current — content depth **5/5**, functionality depth **5/5**. A swipe-card career-exploration deck ("Exploring Options") for Leaving Cert students. The student swipes through real Irish careers Tinder-style (drag-to-save/skip via Framer Motion, with X/Save/Explore buttons and sound cues), then taps any card to open a 4-beat detail walkthrough: (1) front/tagline, (2) "What you actually do" + skills chips, (3) "What it pays in Ireland" with animated CountUp salary bands, a progress bar, and honest pros/cons ("The good" / "The catch"), (4) "How you get there" routes + a runtime-computed "Courses that lead here" list. Each detail card animated salary uses real Irish € data with collapsible source citations. Students save careers to a persisted "shortlist" (Firestore progress/{uid}.careerPaths, additive-merge) framed as "bring these to your guidance counsellor". The tool is wired to the Future Finder: it can be seeded with matchStrings so it opens filtered to "Your matches", and the "courses that lead here" rows are computed live by intersecting each career's matchStrings against the 150 CAO_COURSES in futureFinderData.ts (real catalog, opens real course pages). Registered in InnovationZone.tsx as id 'career-paths' under "Understand / Career discovery".
  - _Thinness:_ Header comment claims '12 careers' but the array holds 27 — a stale hardcoded count, not a thinness signal (content is real and substantial).; studentSubjects prop is declared on the component signature (line 110) and passed from InnovationZone (line 588) but is destructured-out/unused inside CareerPaths.tsx — dead prop.; Only one image asset exists; image is an optional field on 26/27 cards, so most cards render a generic field icon (the single nurse.png is effectively the only bespoke art).
  - _Gaps:_ Header doc comments in BOTH careerPathsData.ts (line 5: 'Career Paths content — 12 careers') and CareerPaths.tsx are stale: the data file actually contains 27 fully-authored careers, not 12. Misleading but harmless to runtime.; Field distribution is uneven: tech (4) and health (4) are well covered but law has only 1 career, design 1, engineering 1 — and 'engineering' collapses 8 distinct branches (mechanical/civil/electrical/biomedical/etc.) into a single generic card.; Only 1 of 27 careers (nurse) has a custom image (career-icons/nurse.png); the other 26 fall back to Lucide line icons (15 have a distinct iconKey, the rest use the field's generic icon), so the deck is visually icon-driven rather than imagery-rich.; matchStrings are hand-maintained per career and must exactly match the free-text careerPaths strings in futureFinderData.ts; any typo or drift silently drops a career from 'courses that lead here' and from Future Finder match-seeding (no validation/test guards this coupling).; coursesFor() caps the 'Courses that lead here' list at 6 and sorts by level then points — popular fields could surface only the highest-points courses, hiding accessible lower-points/Level 6-7 routes for a DEIS audience.; No subject-aware filtering inside the deck: studentSubjects is passed in as a prop but unused in CareerPaths.tsx — the deck cannot down-rank careers a student's subject choices have closed off (e.g. no H5 Chemistry → Vet).; No search or field/category filter on the browse deck beyond the binary All/Matches toggle; with 27 cards a student must swipe linearly.

- **A Day in the Life — the mistake-first decision sim** [L] — _verdict: build, score 4.18_
  - Each career card grows a fourth interactive layer where you make the real, hard calls of that job's day — get one wrong first, see the consequence, then learn the fix — turning a static read-through into a vicarious mastery experience that moves career-decision self-efficacy.
  - _Content:_ For each of the 27 careers, author 3-4 'decision moments' lifted from the real day-to-day already written into the whatYouDo bullets and the honest cons (e.g. nurse: a deteriorating patient + a ringing call bell + a doctor on the phone — what do you do first; electrician: a board that's still live; solicitor: a client who wants you to bend the truth; chef: three tickets, one burnt pan; social worker: a disclosure you're legally obliged to escalate). Each moment ships with a tempting wrong choice (the intuitive-but-mark-losing one), the realistic consequence, and the experienced-practitioner fix, each grounded in a named source (professional body code of conduct / HSE clinical guidance / Safe Electric / Teaching Council Code) so it is decision SUPPORT, not drama. Add a 'what surprised you?' free-text line after the sim. Start with the 8 DEIS-accessible no-points/earn-while-you-learn careers (electrician, chef, healthcare assistant, IT support, accounting technician, youth worker) so the richest new content lands where the audience most needs realistic exposure.
  - _Functionality:_ Add a fifth beat 'try-a-shift' to the existing 4-beat HybridCard walkthrough. It is MISTAKE-FIRST: present the dilemma as 2-3 tappable white option cards; on first tap, if the student picked the tempting-wrong option, animate the consequence — the choice card stays selected (orange = chosen, not 'good') and a NEUTRAL_PANEL consequence strip slides in beneath it with the realistic outcome, then the correct card reveals with a success-green tick (#3A8D5F) and the practitioner's reasoning. Animation encodes meaning: the consequence strip's height-spring shows the size of the misstep (reuse the existing salary growth-bar motion idiom), never decorative confetti on a wrong answer. A small 'how this felt' self-rating (1-5 'could I see myself doing this?') is captured per career and rolls into the shortlist summary so the student leaves with evidence-backed self-knowledge, not just saved cards. No new colours, no header band, no emoji — extends Band + the beat AnimatePresence pattern already in CareerPaths.tsx.
  - _Science:_ Self-efficacy is the most movable and strongest mediator of realistic career choice, and Bandura ranks mastery experiences as the single most powerful source — so letting a student rehearse the real decisions of a job (and recover from a misstep) beats any amount of descriptive text or encouragement. (Bandura, A. (1997). Self-Efficacy: The Exercise of Control. W.H. Freeman; Lent, Brown & Hackett (1994), Journal of Vocational Behavior 45(1), 79-122; Whiston, Li, Mitts & Wright (2017), Journal of Vocational Behavior 100, 175-184 (career-decision self-efficacy = largest single intervention outcome).) | Mistake-first design (lead with the wrong answer, visualise the cost, then the fix) is the project's adopted pedagogy and matches the evidence that struggle reframed as expected — not as 'this isn't for someone like me' — protects engagement for lower-confidence students. (Oyserman, Bybee & Terry (2006), Journal of Personality and Social Psychology 91(1), 188-204 ('difficulty means you're doing it right' reframe).)
  - _App-in-its-own-right:_ This converts a swipe deck into a careers EXPERIENCE app — a library of short, replayable 'try-a-shift' job simulations a student (or a whole DEIS classroom) can work through, each ending with a personal 'could I see myself here?' read-back. That decision-sim format is a standalone product category (think a serious-game careers taster), distinct from any matcher or course finder.
  - _DEIS framing:_ Vicarious 'people-like-me doing the actual job' exposure is exactly the employer-encounter / concrete-knowledge lever the DEIS evidence says works, versus aspiration inflation. Front-loading the no-points trades/apprenticeship careers signals belonging ('these are real, reachable jobs') and the self-rating is framed as autonomy and incremental self-knowledge — never 'are you good enough', and every result is handed to the guidance counsellor.
  - _Evidence check:_ All four cited sources are real, correctly attributed, and on-point (verified via web search). Whiston, Li, Goodrich Mitts & Wright (2017), J. Vocational Behavior 100, 175-184, DOI 10.1016/j.jvb.2017.03.010 — real meta-analysis (57 studies, mean ES 0.352) with career-decision self-efficacy as a named outcome; supports the 'most movable mediator' claim. Oyserman, Bybee & Terry (2006), JPSP 91, 188-204 — verified exact volume/pages; the 'change the meaning associated with difficulty' intervention component for low-income 8th graders with 2-year sustained effects directly grounds the struggle-reframe/mistake-first claim. Bandura (1997), Self-Efficacy: The Exercise of Control — the descending ranking (enactive mastery > vicarious > verbal persuasion > physiological) is accurately stated; mastery 'most potent... especially when the task is viewed as demanding' matches the pitch. Lent, Brown & Hackett (1994) SCCT in JVB 45(1) is the correct canonical citation for self-efficacy as a career-choice mediator. The DEIS 'employer-encounter / concrete-knowledge beats aspiration inflation' framing is independently supported by the OECD 'Challenging Social Inequality Through Career Guidance' (2024) evidence. No shaky or inverted citations found — this is the opposite of buzzword laundering.

- **More than one way in — the de-circumscription pathway map** [L] — _verdict: build, score 4.27_
  - Replace the 6-course capped list with a per-career interactive pathway map that always surfaces the lower-points / PLC / apprenticeship route into the SAME field beside the high-points one, directly fighting the adolescent habit of deleting whole fields as 'not for someone like me'.
  - _Content:_ Two content jobs. (1) Fix the field-coverage gaps the scan found: split the single generic 'engineer' card into its real branches (mechanical, civil, electrical, biomedical) and add law/design depth, each as a proper CareerCard with its own routes and matchStrings — this is the data the map needs to be honest. (2) For every career, author an explicit 'the long way round' note: the PLC-to-degree, apprenticeship-to-chartered, or Level 6/7-then-add-on route to the same destination (much of this already exists in routes[] — make it first-class and visible, not buried as the third route). Each pathway node carries its real entry requirement (points, QQI link, named body like CORU/NMBI/Engineers Ireland) with a source. Crucially, surface the lowest-barrier in-route as prominently as the headline degree, never as a consolation footnote.
  - _Functionality:_ On the existing 'route' beat, render a small SVG pathway map (reuse the deck's cross-tool course-matching, not a new data source): the career sits at the top; below it, parallel vertical routes (CAO L8 / PLC L5→L8 / apprenticeship) drawn as connector lines in muted #d0cdc8 with #F26B1F chevrons — the exact connector-arrow idiom the design system already specifies. Tapping a node expands the live 'courses that lead here' rows for THAT route only, and — fixing the documented gaps — change coursesFor() to guarantee at least one Level 6/7 or apprenticeship option is shown even when high-points L8s dominate (sort by route diversity, not purely level-then-points). Wire the previously-DEAD studentSubjects prop here, softly: if a hard subject gate is unmet (e.g. no H5 Chemistry for Vet), show a non-blocking 'this route usually needs…' note plus the alternative route in — informing, never closing the field. Never collapse fit and points into one number.
  - _Science:_ Adolescents prematurely eliminate whole occupational fields by perceived prestige/ability ceiling (circumscription), sacrificing field of interest FIRST in compromise — so a tool that lets a points number quietly delete a field reinforces the most damaging part of development; always showing the same-field lower-points/PLC/apprenticeship route is the antidote. (Gottfredson, L. S. (1981). Circumscription and compromise. Journal of Counseling Psychology 28(6), 545-579; Behavioural Insights Team & Careers & Enterprise Company (2018), Moments of Choice.) | For DEIS students the barrier is pathway KNOWLEDGE, not aspiration; concrete routes, costs and eligibility (the 'difference-education' framing that not knowing the system is about the system) close attainment gaps where aspiration inflation does not. (Gorard, See & Davies (2012), Joseph Rowntree Foundation; Stephens, Hamedani & Destin (2014), Psychological Science 25(4), 943-949.)
  - _App-in-its-own-right:_ This becomes a standalone 'every route in' pathway-mapping app — an Irish-specific answer to 'I want to do X but think my points/subjects rule it out', spanning CAO, PLC, apprenticeship and professional-body routes with live course links. That is independently valuable to students, parents and guidance counsellors and is the suite's highest-leverage DEIS surface per the evidence.
  - _DEIS framing:_ This is the most directly DEIS-serving concept: it operationalises 'the gap is knowledge and barriers, not aspiration' by making the back-door route as visible and dignified as the front door, and by refusing to let points close a field. Copy frames the alternative route as a normal, common path (belonging), and the unmet-subject note as system information, not a verdict on the student.
  - _Evidence check:_ Both primary sources are REAL and verified via web search. Gottfredson, L. S. (1981), Circumscription and compromise, Journal of Counseling Psychology 28(6), 545-579 — citation exact, mechanism correctly applied (field-of-interest sacrificed first in compromise; the design's always-visible same-field lower-barrier route targets the de-circumscription mechanism itself, not just its name). Stephens, Hamedani & Destin (2014), Closing the Social-Class Achievement Gap, Psychological Science — REAL RCT (N=168); TWO caveats: the concept cites pp.943-949 but the article is pp.943-953 (minor pagination error, not fabrication), and the paper is a college-transition narrative intervention so it supports the 'system-knowledge gap / name outside help' framing rather than directly testing a pathway map — the Gorard/See/Davies (2012, Joseph Rowntree Foundation) aspiration-vs-attainment evidence is the more direct and well-established support. No fabricated or direction-inverted citations. Separately, every code-level claim was independently verified against live files: coursesFor() sorts level-desc then points-desc then slice(0,6), so 118 L8 courses bury the 8 apprenticeship + 32 L5/6/7 options (back-door-invisible claim TRUE); studentSubjects is passed from InnovationZone.tsx:588 but omitted from the CareerPaths destructure at line 110 (DEAD prop, TRUE); single generic 'Engineer' card with 8 branch matchStrings but no per-branch cards (TRUE); matchStrings integrity test already exists at careerPathsData.test.ts line 16 (so that proposed test is partly redundant).

- **Why this could be me — the relevance-writing shortlist** [M] — _verdict: build, score 4.16_
  - Turn the passive saved-careers shortlist into an active reflection surface where the student writes, in their own words, why a path might matter to them and one small step to take this week — adding the two evidence ingredients the deck is missing (written self-generation + support-building) so the saved list becomes a counsellor-ready, self-efficacy-building artefact.
  - _Content:_ Author lightweight scaffolding content (not the student's answer — they self-generate that, which is what makes it work). For each saved career: a one-line relevance prompt tuned to that field ('Where in your life have you already done a bit of this?'), a curated set of 2-3 concrete 'small step this week' suggestions per field drawn from the real routes (e.g. 'email the apprenticeship coordinator at your local ETB', 'ask your guidance counsellor about HEAR/DARE for this course', 'watch one day-in-the-life clip'), and a difference-education micro-line normalising that not knowing the next step is about the system, not about you. Add a short 'who could help you with this?' nudge listing the guidance counsellor, the named professional body, and SUSI/HEAR/DARE where relevant — the support-building ingredient currently absent across the suite.
  - _Functionality:_ Extend the existing 'Your shortlist' view (already in CareerPaths.tsx, persisted to progress/{uid}.careerPaths). Each saved card gains: (1) a free-text 'why might this be for you?' field (utility-value writing) saved alongside savedIds in the additive-merge state; (2) a single checkable 'this week I'll…' step the student picks or types — a manufactured small mastery win that ticks to success-green when done, with a gentle animation encoding completion (not celebration-on-save); (3) an 'export / share with my counsellor' action that renders the shortlist + their own words as a clean printable summary. Keep the deck re-takeable and add a soft 'snapshot, not a decision — bring it to your counsellor' framing so it supports moratorium-style exploration rather than locking in a choice. The 'why' text and checked step persist via the existing useFreshProgress getDoc-on-mount pattern, sidestepping the known staleness bug.
  - _Science:_ Utility-value writing — students articulating in their OWN words how something connects to their life/future — raises interest and grades, with effects LARGEST for low-expectancy students (the DEIS profile); student-self-generated relevance beats being told the relevance. (Hulleman & Harackiewicz (2009), Science 326(5958), 1410-1412; Harackiewicz, Rozek, Hulleman & Hyde (2012), Psychological Science 23(8), 899-906.) | Career-intervention effect size rises near-linearly with how many of Brown & Ryan Krane's five ingredients are present; the two most absent across the Launchpad are written exercises and support-building — exactly what a self-written 'why' plus a 'who can help / what step this week' adds. (Brown & Ryan Krane (2003), Journal of Vocational Behavior 62(3), 411-428; reframing not-knowing as a system issue: Stephens, Hamedani & Destin (2014), Psychological Science 25(4), 943-949.)
  - _App-in-its-own-right:_ This is the seed of a personal 'career reflection journal' app: a persistent, re-visitable record of which paths a student explored, in their own words why, the concrete next step they committed to, and who to ask — a living document for the student and a structured input for guidance-counsellor conversations, valuable independent of the swipe deck.
  - _DEIS framing:_ Centres the student's OWN voice and a single achievable step over aspiration inflation, and the difference-education 'not knowing the next step is about the system' line directly de-stigmatises the knowledge gap. Naming concrete helpers (counsellor, ETB, SUSI/HEAR/DARE) is the support-building the evidence says works for disadvantaged students, and the snapshot/counsellor framing respects the project's minors-consent posture and autonomy-first tone.
  - _Evidence check:_ All four citations verified real and on-point via WebSearch. (1) Hulleman & Harackiewicz (2009), Science 326(5958):1410-1412 — confirmed; 262 students, students wrote how content related to their lives, effect LARGEST for low-expectancy students, direction matches the claim. (2) Harackiewicz, Rozek, Hulleman & Hyde (2012), Psych Science 23(8):899-906 — confirmed real. (3) Brown & Ryan Krane (2003), JVB 62(3):411-428 — confirmed; the five critical ingredients are correctly named (written exercises + support-building are two of them) and the near-linear escalation of effect sizes (.45/.61/.99 for 1/2/3 ingredients) is real, supporting the dose-response claim. (4) Stephens, Hamedani & Destin (2014), Psych Science 25 — confirmed (difference-education eliminated the social-class GPA gap by increasing resource-seeking); concept cites pages 943-949, actual is 943-953 — a trivial endpoint typo, not a misattribution. No shaky or invented citations. One mild caveat, not a falsity: utility-value evidence is established for academic course-content relevance; applying it to career-shortlist relevance is a reasonable but not directly-tested transfer.

### `future-finder`
Current — content depth **4/5**, functionality depth **4/5**. A 4-file career/subject-discovery tool. For senior-cycle (LC) users it runs a 10-question intent quiz (interests, work scenarios, 3 value sliders, work-style, team preference, study duration, relocation, regions) and scores all 149 hand-curated CAO/PLC/apprenticeship courses with a weighted algorithm (45% interest + 30% values + 25% feasibility, plus a geographic multiplier), then renders ranked result cards with match % and "reasons" pills, a sortable/region-filterable top-10 list, a per-course detail view (points gap vs the student's auto-computed best-six points, employability bars, career-path/subject-bonus chips, score breakdown bars), a save-to-picks feature, a 2-3 course comparison table, a "how this works" scoring-explainer modal, and a handoff button into the Career Paths tool. For junior-cycle users the SAME quiz is rebranded "Subject Explorer" (Q8-Q11 skipped) and matched to 11 LC-subject clusters with a separate normalised scorer, showing best-fit + 2 alternates + "things to try" + a soft career hint. All state persists to Firestore (progress/{uid}.futureFinder) with answer-format migration. IMPORTANT: this is the LEGACY tool — InnovationZone titles it "Future Finder Old" for senior users (line 493) and the RIASEC-based FutureFinderRevamped is now the live "Future Finder". The legacy file remains live ONLY as the JC "Subject Explorer".
  - _Thinness:_ No TODO/FIXME/placeholder/dummy markers anywhere in the 4 files (grep clean) — this is a finished, not stubbed, tool; Course database is a 149-entry static literal array (every code/points/tag typed by hand), not fetched from any API or generated — points are frozen at '2025 CAO Round 1'; 11 SUBJECT_CLUSTERS and 10 quiz questions are fully static literal definitions; Off-token colours throughout the legacy senior UI (emerald-600/emerald-500/emerald-400 success states, #FAF7F4 warm-cream card backgrounds) contradict the project design memory (success token #3A8D5F; no warm cream in module surfaces) — a sign it predates the current design system and hasn't been migrated; Senior detail/results/compare views (~700+ lines) are effectively unreachable for the only live audience (JC), since senior users are routed to FutureFinderRevamped
  - _Gaps:_ Superseded: titled 'Future Finder Old' for senior users (InnovationZone.tsx line 493); FutureFinderRevamped (RIASEC) is the live senior 'Future Finder'. The legacy component now justifies its existence only as the JC 'Subject Explorer' path — the entire senior-facing 1000+ lines of ResultsPhase/DetailPhase/ComparePhase are dead weight for the only audience still routed here; All course data is a static hand-maintained array (2025 Round-1 points baked in as literals) — points/salary/employability will drift year-on-year with no live CAO/HEA data source; no update mechanism; typicalPoints, salaryBand, and employability are author-asserted reference values, not sourced/cited (the modal admits this) — defensible but unverifiable, and the codebase has a citation discipline elsewhere (examiner-reports) that this tool doesn't follow; Subject-fit is computed (_subjectFitScore) but deliberately NOT scored — it only colours an informational badge/reason; a student's actual subjects don't move the ranking, which undersells the 'matched to who you are' framing; Course coverage is breadth-thin per field: e.g. only one veterinary degree, mostly one flagship course per discipline per institution — a student matched to a niche interest may see the same handful of courses; JC cluster thingsToTry / careerHint / blurb are static per-cluster prose (11 clusters); good quality but fixed — no personalisation beyond which cluster surfaces; Hardcoded display limits: top-30 sliced then top-10 shown; compare capped at 3; reasons capped at 5-6 — reasonable but inflexible

- **Subject Lab — the JC subject-choice companion (write-it-yourself, mistake-first)** [L] — _verdict: build, score 0.82_
  - Grow the one-shot JC cluster matcher into a standalone subject-choice app that makes the student write the link between subjects and their own life, shows the costly choosing-mistakes first, and ends with an individualised, counsellor-shareable plan — deliberately stacking three of the four Brown & Ryan Krane ingredients the current tool is missing.
  - _Content:_ Replace the 11 static-prose clusters with a structured JC subject-decision knowledge base authored from primary Irish sources (NCCA senior-cycle subject specifications, schools' actual option-band structures, the SEC subject list). For each of the ~26 real LC subjects add: a plain-English 'what you actually DO in this subject' card (activity-based, not 'good prospects'), a 'common choosing-mistake' card (e.g. 'picked Biology expecting animals, it's mostly cell processes and exam essays'; 'dropped HL Maths and later needed it for the course I wanted'; 'chose a subject because my friend did'), the one-or-two downstream doors it tends to open AND the honest note that almost nothing is closed by NOT taking it at JC except a short list of true gatekeepers (HL Maths, a lab science for some health courses). Add a 'de-circumscription' content layer: for every aspiration a student types, surface BOTH the high-points CAO route and the PLC/apprenticeship/access route into the same field, so a points ceiling never silently deletes a field. Keep the cluster scorer as the entry read, but the content the student then explores is per-subject and per-mistake, not per-cluster boilerplate.
  - _Functionality:_ Three new functional layers on top of the existing 7-question scorer. (1) Mistake-first 'Choosing Trap' interactive: before any recommendation, the student is shown 3-4 realistic JC subject-choice scenarios and predicts the outcome; the animation then plays the mark-loss/door-closing consequence (a subject tile the student wrongly eliminated lights back up as 'still open', or a friend-following choice visibly diverges from the student's own interest signal) — animation encodes the actual cost, then the fix. (2) Utility-value writing step (load-bearing): after the cluster read, the student writes 1-2 sentences in their OWN words on 'why might these subjects matter to a life you'd actually want' — self-generated, never tool-asserted; the text is saved and echoed back in the plan. (3) Individualised feedback + support-building output: a one-page 'My Subject Thinking' summary (their signal, their own words, their named open doors, 2-3 concrete this-week 'things to try', and an explicit 'who to talk to: your guidance counsellor / this subject's teacher — bring this page' prompt with a share/print action). Re-takeable, with a visible 'this is a snapshot, you'll change' banner.
