  - _Science:_ Career-intervention effect size rises near-linearly as you stack five active ingredients (written exercises, individualised feedback, world-of-work info, modelling, support-building): ~.22 with none → .45 (1) → .61 (2) → .99 (3). The current JC tool delivers only partial world-of-work info; adding the written exercise, individualised feedback and support-building is exactly the documented lever. (Brown, S. D., & Ryan Krane, N. E. (2003). 'Critical Ingredients of Career Choice Interventions: More Analyses and New Hypotheses.' Journal of Vocational Behavior, 62(3), 411-428; replicated by Whiston, Li, Mitts & Wright (2017), Journal of Vocational Behavior, 100, 175-184 (weighted mean d = 0.352, career-decision self-efficacy the largest outcome).) | Having students write in their OWN words how material connects to their personal life raises interest and performance, with the largest gains for low-expectancy/low-confidence students (the DEIS profile); self-generated relevance beats being told the relevance. (Hulleman, C. S., & Harackiewicz, J. M. (2009). 'Promoting Interest and Performance in High School Science Classes.' Science, 326(5958), 1410-1412 (262 students, 30 classrooms; effects concentrated among students with low success expectations).) | Adolescents prematurely eliminate whole fields (circumscription) by perceived prestige/ability ceiling before considering interests, and field of interest is sacrificed first in compromise — so a tool must always surface the same-field lower-points/PLC/apprenticeship route and never let a points number delete a field. (Gottfredson, L. S. (1981). 'Circumscription and compromise: A developmental theory of occupational aspirations.' Journal of Counseling Psychology, 28(6), 545-579.)
  - _App-in-its-own-right:_ This is the natural standalone product: a JC (and TY) subject-choice companion an entire year group could run at the option-form moment. It owns a real recurring decision (which LC subjects to pick) the CAO/Future Finder-RIASEC tools don't touch, produces a counsellor-shareable artefact, and is re-takeable across JC→TY→senior cycle. It absorbs the legacy file's only live job and turns it into a much deeper tool, while letting the ~700 lines of dead senior results/detail/compare code be retired.
  - _DEIS framing:_ Frames subject choice as autonomy and keeping-doors-open, not high-achievement. Stephens-style difference-education tone throughout: 'not knowing how subject choice works is about the system, not about you — here's how it actually works, and here's who to ask.' The mistake-first scenarios normalise that nearly everyone mis-picks at 14 and it's recoverable; the de-circumscription layer guarantees a not-yet-ambitious student always sees a real route in (PLC/apprenticeship) beside the high-points one, so no field reads as 'not for someone like me.' Belonging and incremental 'this-week things to try', never 'lock in your future'.
  - _Evidence check:_ All three citations verified real and on-point via web search. (1) Brown, S. D. & Ryan Krane, N. E. (2003), Journal of Vocational Behavior 62(3):411-428 — CONFIRMED, and the exact staircase figures (.22 none -> .45 one -> .61 two -> .99 three) are verbatim from the source, which itself notes "no study employed more than three of the critical ingredients" (so the concept's plan to add three is the honest ceiling, not an overreach). Whiston, Li, Mitts & Wright (2017) replication (JVB 100) also confirmed. The five ingredients are correctly named (written exercises, individualised feedback, world-of-work info, modelling, support-building). (2) Hulleman & Harackiewicz (2009), Science 326(5958):1410-1412 — CONFIRMED, including the load-bearing detail that effects concentrated among students with LOW success expectations (the DEIS profile) and that writing-own-relevance beat being-told. (3) Gottfredson (1981), Journal of Counseling Psychology 28(6):545-579 — CONFIRMED. One nuance: the concept's claim "field of interest is sacrificed first in compromise" matches Gottfredson's original first principle, but empirical tests give the compromise-order MIXED support (some find sex-type most readily sacrificed). This does not undermine the design, which rests on the robust circumscription mechanism (premature elimination of whole fields by perceived prestige/ability ceiling), not on the contested compromise-ordering. No fabricated or mis-applied sources; effect directions match the literature.

- **People Like Me — near-peer subject & path stories woven into the matcher** [M] — _verdict: build, score 4.21_
  - Add a modelling layer of short, relatable near-peer/DEIS-local journeys keyed to each subject cluster, so a JC student who matches 'Practical and applied' or 'Sport and health' immediately reads 'someone like me chose these subjects and here's where it went' — supplying Brown & Ryan Krane's modelling ingredient via Bandura's similar-model mechanism.
  - _Content:_ Author a library of ~30-40 short journey cards (120-180 words each), 2-4 per existing subject cluster, deliberately featuring relatable, near-peer and DEIS/regional figures and non-glamorous, honest pathways (apprentice electrician via Construction Studies + DCG; community-nurse via PE/Biology and a PLC bridge; first-in-family Arts student via History + a foreign language and the HEAR route). Each card: the JC subjects they picked, the honest middle (a setback, a points miss, a change of direction — NOT a clean success story), the door it opened, and one 'what I'd tell 14-year-old me' line. Content is source-grounded against real Irish pathway structures (CAO/PLC/apprenticeship routes, HEAR/DARE/SUSI existence) and written to be similar to the audience, not aspirational celebrity. Deliberately include counter-stereotypical pairings (a girl into Engineering, a boy into Home Economics/care) to widen horizons rather than confirm the People–Things stereotype.
  - _Functionality:_ (1) Cluster-keyed surfacing: when the scorer returns a student's best-fit cluster + alternates, 1-2 matching journey cards appear inline, drawn from the shared components/immersiveDeck/ HybridCard + SwipeDeck primitives (white card, white header with a pastel-blob initials, ink serif title, orange CTA — exactly like How They Did It / Career Paths). (2) A 'similarity filter' the student sets in two taps (region, and 'first in my family to consider this?' / 'into something people don't expect?') that re-ranks which journeys surface, maximising perceived model similarity — the variable Bandura says drives vicarious self-efficacy. (3) A light 'save this person's path' action that drops the journey into the student's 'My Subject Thinking' page from Concept 1, linking modelling to the written plan. No quiz, no scoring of the stories — they are exploration, not assessment, to support moratorium-style breadth.
  - _Science:_ Self-efficacy is the most movable and most pivotal career-tool outcome, and Bandura's vicarious-experience source works strongest when the model is perceived as SIMILAR to the self — so relatable near-peer/local/DEIS journeys ('someone like me did this') beat distant success stories. (Bandura, A. (1997). 'Self-Efficacy: The Exercise of Control.' W. H. Freeman; Lent, Brown & Hackett (1994) Social Cognitive Career Theory, Journal of Vocational Behavior, 45(1), 79-122; Whiston et al. (2017) — career-decision self-efficacy the largest intervention effect.) | Modelling (exposure to role models) is one of the five critical career-intervention ingredients whose presence raises effect size; it is currently absent from the JC Subject Explorer. (Brown, S. D., & Ryan Krane, N. E. (2003). 'Critical Ingredients of Career Choice Interventions.' Journal of Vocational Behavior, 62(3), 411-428.) | For disadvantaged young people the gap is knowledge and concrete contact, not aspiration; effective interventions cluster around relatable models, employer encounters and personalised transition support, and large Things–People sex differences mean a tool should deliberately surface counter-stereotypical models to widen rather than narrow horizons. (Behavioural Insights Team & The Careers & Enterprise Company (2018), 'Effective Careers Interventions for Disadvantaged Young People'; Gorard, See & Davies (2012), Joseph Rowntree Foundation; Su, Rounds & Armstrong (2009), Psychological Bulletin, 135(6), 859-884 (Things–People d ≈ 0.93).)
  - _App-in-its-own-right:_ The modelling content is reusable across the whole Launchpad (it shares the How They Did It / Career Paths deck primitives) but, keyed to JC subject clusters, it makes the Subject Explorer a destination students re-open to browse 'people like me' rather than a one-shot quiz. Combined with Concept 1 it is a complete standalone JC app: read your signal → see people like you who chose these → write your own why → walk out with a plan.
  - _DEIS framing:_ This is the most directly DEIS-shaped concept: similarity is the active ingredient, so the library leads with near-peer, local, first-in-family and non-glamorous routes, and explicitly includes PLC/apprenticeship journeys as equal-status, not consolation. The 'messy middle' framing (setbacks, course changes, points misses that worked out) protects not-yet-ambitious students from reading success as 'not for someone like me.' Counter-stereotypical models are deliberate, framed around belonging and 'you'd be welcome here', never exceptionalism.
  - _Evidence check:_ All three primary sources verified real and on-point via WebSearch. Brown and Ryan Krane (2003), Journal of Vocational Behavior 62(3), 411-428: confirmed exact; modelling is genuinely one of the five critical ingredients. Su, Rounds and Armstrong (2009), Psychological Bulletin 135, 859-884, d=0.93 Things-People: exact match on volume, pages, effect size. BIT and Careers and Enterprise Company (2018) report is real and its findings support the framing (peer networks, repeated-over-one-shot, knowledge/contact gap for non-academic routes not aspiration). SCCT (1994) and Whiston (2017) are standard supporting cites, correctly characterised. No shaky or inverted citations.

### `your-possible-life`
Current — content depth **5/5**, functionality depth **4/5**. A 5-step WOOP-structured flow (pick → life → reality → plan → steer) that bridges a student's current Leaving Cert grades to the realistic adult life a chosen career could open up. Step 1 (PICK) seeds from the student's Future Finder matches (computed by joining their saved course picks to CAREERS via matchStrings) or lets them pick any of 27 careers from a grid. Step 2 (LIFE) translates the career's salary band, region (Dublin/city/regional) and horizon (starting out / a few years in) into Irish 2026 take-home pay (real PAYE+USC+PRSI maths) and then into a tangible, qualitative life picture (housing, transport, saving, travel, a tier label, a one-line vignette) with an animated take-home counter and honesty caveat. Step 3 (REALITY) shows the honest points gap between the student's best-6 projected points (current grades) and the lowest CAO entry route for that career, with branch logic for no-grades/no-points-race/already-reachable/real-gap cases. Step 4 (PLAN) lists the highest-yield grade bargains (computeBargains, sorted by points-per-grade-up) and computes how many of the top bargains close the gap, plus a self-transcendence reflection textarea ("Who, beyond you, would this matter to?"). Step 5 (STEER) reframes it all as malleable possibilities with live region/horizon dials and quick-swap chips to other matched careers, plus a nudge to bring it to a guidance counsellor. State (careerId, region, horizon, why) persists to Firestore via useEffortLifeSim and re-seeds on return.
  - _Thinness:_ Tax engine constants are hardcoded for a single 2026 single-employee scenario: TAX_CREDITS=4000, STANDARD_BAND=44000, PRSI_RATE=0.042, ESSENTIALS=750, CAR_COST=500 — no profile inputs feed them.; effortHint in computeBargains is one of exactly 3 static strings selected by a grade-digit if/else (subjectData.ts:318-323) — not computed from any real effort/time model.; Horizon is a 2-value union ('start' | 'experienced') mapped directly to two salary fields; no salary curve.; Region is 3 fixed buckets (LIFE_REGIONS = ['dublin','city','regional']) with one {share, alone} rent pair each.; Career grid is a static 27-item .map over CAREERS with no search/filter/sort affordance in manual mode; data file header comment claims '12 careers' (stale by 15).; Lifestyle tier is a 4-step hardcoded net-monthly threshold ladder (2200/2900/3600 in lifestyleFromNet; tests at effortLifeModel.test.ts:55-60).; All lifestyle copy (housing/transport/saving/travel/vignette/caveat) is template-string interpolation over the computed numbers — varied but rule-generated, not authored per career.; PLAN gap-closing shows at most the top 4 bargains (bargains.slice(0,4)) and matched-career swap chips cap at 5-6.; Component is 431 lines but ~all five step screens are presentational; the only net-new computation lives in effortLifeModel.ts (303 lines, ~90 of which are the two real functions) — the rest is reuse of existing engines (bestSixPoints, careerReach, computeBargains, CAREERS).; No GenAI/dynamic generation anywhere; everything is deterministic from grades + fixed tables.
  - _Gaps:_ The PLAN step's effort-to-points mapping is shallow vs the rest of the tool: computeBargains (subjectData.ts:298-339) only ever models a SINGLE grade step up per subject (nextGrade = grades[currentIdx-1]) and reports a per-subject effortHint chosen from exactly three hardcoded static strings ('2-4 focused study sessions' / '4-6 focused study sessions' / '6-10 focused study sessions') keyed only on the grade band ending digit — it does not account for the student's exam date, subject difficulty, current study habits, or how many grades up are actually needed, so the 'one term's focus, not a miracle' framing rests on a coarse heuristic.; The lifestyle picture is single-and-childless only: irishNetPay hardcodes a single PAYE employee with €4,000 personal+PAYE credits and no reliefs (effortLifeModel.ts:51,77), and lifestyleFromNet assumes one person's ESSENTIALS=750 and a young-driver CAR_COST=500 (flat constants). There is no couple/dependents/student-debt/pension lens, so 'a few years in' only moves the salary band, never the life stage.; Only two horizon points exist (start vs experienced) drawn straight from career.salary.startK/experiencedK — there is no mid-career or trajectory curve, and no per-career salary growth shape; the dial is effectively a 2-state toggle, not a timeline.; The career roster is capped at the 27 entries in the shared careerPathsData.ts (the file header still says '12 careers', now stale). There is no search/filter on the 27-career grid in manual-pick mode — it is a flat 2-column scroll — and several common LC destinations (medicine/doctor, civil/public service, defence, hospitality beyond chef, apprenticeship trades beyond electrician) are absent.; Region cost-of-living is modelled from three fixed buckets with hardcoded rent/essentials constants (REGION_RENT dublin/city/regional, effortLifeModel.ts:129-133) derived from Daft Q4 2025; there is no per-county granularity and the figures will silently drift out of date with no refresh mechanism.; The 'reach' uses only the LOWEST typical-points route via careerReach (min of CAO routes), so it always frames the most accessible door; it does not let the student explore the specific course they actually picked in Future Finder or the spread of routes, and 'entryPoints' can mislead for careers whose realistic route is far above the floor (e.g. one cheap Level 7 dragging the number down).

- **The Open Door — affordability & eligibility layer (Can I actually get in, and pay for it?)** [L] — _verdict: ?, score ?_
  - Your Possible Life grows a sixth step that turns the abstract 'route in' number into a concrete, visibly OPEN door — showing SUSI/grant eligibility, the HEAR/DARE access door, and the real cost of the actual course the student picked — so 'I can get there and afford it' becomes a checked-off fact, not a hope.
  - _Content:_ Author a source-grounded affordability/eligibility dataset (new affordabilityData.ts): (1) SUSI 2026 maintenance + fee-grant bands keyed to a simple household-circumstances input (reckonable income brackets, distance >30km vs ≤30km for adjacent/non-adjacent rates, number of dependants), drawn verbatim-rate from susi.ie; (2) the Student Contribution charge and how the SUSI fee grant / partial grant offsets it, plus the €1,000 fee reduction context; (3) a plain-English HEAR (socio-economic, DEIS-school flagged) and DARE (disability) eligibility explainer with the indicator checklist and the 'reduced-points route into the SAME course' mechanic; (4) per-pathway cost-of-getting-there copy for PLC→degree (PLC year is largely free/low-cost, then advanced entry), apprenticeship (you EARN from day one — net pay during training, reusing irishNetPay), and CAO degree (SUSI-adjusted). Every figure carries a source comment and a 'rates change yearly — check susi.ie' freshness caveat like the existing tax-engine header. Content is authored, not invented: rates from susi.ie, accesscollege.ie (HEAR/DARE), citizensinformation.ie.
  - _Functionality:_ Insert an 'Open the door' step between REALITY and PLAN (or as an expandable panel inside REALITY) with a 3-tap, optional, non-stored circumstances mini-form (income band as a coarse 4-bucket Segment, distance toggle, 'is your school DEIS?' toggle that can pre-fill from the school profile). It computes an eligibility verdict and renders the door as an animation-encodes-meaning interactive: a literal closed door (the bare Student Contribution + full living cost) that swings OPEN as each support is applied, each support sliding in as a stacked deduction bar that visibly shrinks the 'what you actually pay' number with an animated CountUp — the mistake-first framing being the scary sticker price first, then watching it fall. Output is a deduction ledger (sticker cost → minus SUSI maintenance → minus fee grant → your real cost) plus a HEAR/DARE 'reduced-points door' callout that lowers the REALITY entry-points number when the student flags eligibility, recomputing the gap live. Nothing is stored beyond a boolean 'explored affordability' flag (privacy: no income data persisted — important for minors).
  - _Science:_ Making the path to college look OPEN and affordable (financial aid exists, points achievable, route exists) increases current school effort in low-income adolescents; making it look closed/expensive suppresses effort. Belief that effort pays off is contingent on a visible open pathway. (Destin, M. & Oyserman, D. (2009), 'From Assets to School Outcomes: How Finances Shape Children's Perceived Possibilities and Intentions', Psychological Science, 20(4), 414–418; Destin & Oyserman (2010), 'Incentivizing Education', Journal of Experimental Social Psychology, 46(5), 846–849.) | Aspiration must always terminate in a concrete navigational next step (a CAO route, application step, affordability/eligibility fact); pure aspiration-raising shows little-to-no effect on attainment, while effective programmes attach a concrete academic/navigational component. (Kintrea, St Clair & Houston (2011, JRF); Education Endowment Foundation, 'Aspiration interventions', Teaching & Learning Toolkit; Carter-Wall & Whitfield (2012, JRF).)
  - _App-in-its-own-right:_ Stands alone as 'The Open Door' — a SUSI/HEAR/DARE eligibility-and-affordability calculator that almost no DEIS student currently has in plain English. It answers the single most suppressing question ('can people like me actually afford this?') with a personalised, animated yes-and-here's-how, and is genuinely useful even detached from the life-simulation — a guidance-counsellor-grade tool a school could open on its own.
  - _DEIS framing:_ This is the single highest-leverage inclusion move in the whole aspiration cluster. DEIS students disproportionately self-select out on a false belief that college is unaffordable and the door is shut to them; HEAR exists precisely for them. Surfacing 'the door is open, here is your grant, here is the reduced-points access route into the same course' directly counters the closed-path effort suppression the evidence describes, and reframes effort as an investment toward an attainable identity rather than a chore.

- **Someone Like Me — the near-peer on-ramp that proves the path is walkable** [L] — _verdict: build, score 4.25_
  - Every life and every route in Your Possible Life gains a matched near-peer who started where the student is — first-in-family, a DEIS school, the 'long way round' via repeat/PLC/apprenticeship/access — telling the relatable on-ramp and the early wobble in their own voice, so the impressive outcome reads as attainable rather than deflating.
  - _Content:_ Author a near-peer profile bank (new nearPeerData.ts) of 18–24 short, first-person, source-grounded-or-clearly-composite profiles, each tagged to one or more of the 27 careers and to relatability flags (first-in-family, DEIS/non-grind-school, rural, repeated the LC, came via PLC→degree, came via apprenticeship, HEAR/access route, changed direction late). Each profile leads with the START ('I sat where you're sitting — I was sure university wasn't for people like me'), names the specific path mechanic ('I missed it by 20 points, did a PLC in [field], advanced-entered into year 2'), normalises a transient struggle in the student's own register ('first term I felt completely behind — it passed'), and only then lands the outcome as a quiet payoff, never a salary boast. Content is researched from real Irish access/PLC/apprenticeship destination patterns and published first-gen testimony (accesscollege.ie, SOLAS apprenticeship.ie graduate stories, gradireland, HEA access reports) and written to the Lockwood 'you still have time + ability grows' frame; profiles foreground the on-ramp, never the polished end-state. Keep any paraphrase of a real testimony below the verbatim threshold and mark composites as composites.
  - _Functionality:_ Add a 'Someone like me' surface that appears at two points: (a) on PICK/REALITY, a matched profile card chosen by overlapping the student's situation (DEIS flag, current-points gap, chosen pathway type) with the profile's relatability tags — explicitly defaulting to the relatable on-ramp profile first, with a 'show me a different route in' control so it never implies the matched route is the only one; (b) on the LIFE step, a one-line 'how someone got from your grades to here' that links to the full profile. The interactive is a self-generated belonging beat (Walton/Cohen format): after reading the on-ramp, the student gets an optional reflection prompt in their OWN words ('What's the part of this that feels out of reach right now?') and the matched peer's struggle line is revealed as a response, with a gentle 'a lot of people feel this — and it shifts' framing. A small filter lets the student surface peers by the relatability tag that matters to them (first-in-family / repeated / PLC / apprenticeship), which also doubles as the missing search/filter affordance the current flat 27-career grid lacks.
  - _Science:_ Outstanding role models inspire only when success seems ATTAINABLE and the domain is self-relevant; when success looks unattainable the same star DEFLATES self-view. Inspiration requires the viewer to still have time or believe ability grows. (Lockwood, P. & Kunda, Z. (1997), 'Superstars and Me: Predicting the Impact of Role Models on the Self', Journal of Personality and Social Psychology, 73(1), 91–103.) | Relatability/similarity of the role model matters most for negatively-stereotyped or under-represented groups: a competent SAME-group / first-gen near-peer raises self-appraised ability and buffers performance, while non-stereotyped groups depend far less on matched models. (Marx, D. M. & Roman, J. S. (2002), 'Female Role Models: Protecting Women's Math Test Performance', PSPB, 28(9), 1183–1193; Lockwood, P. (2006), '"Someone Like Me Can Be Successful"', Psychology of Women Quarterly, 30(1), 36–46; Plaskett, Bali, Nakkula & Harris (2018), Phi Delta Kappan.) | A brief social-belonging intervention — framing early struggle as common and TEMPORARY, voiced by senior/near-peer students, with the student self-generating the message — durably raises stigmatized students' GPA and closes achievement gaps. (Walton, G. M. & Cohen, G. L. (2011), 'A Brief Social-Belonging Intervention Improves Academic and Health Outcomes of Minority Students', Science, 331(6023), 1447–1451; Stephens, Hamedani & Destin (2014), 'Closing the Social-Class Achievement Gap', Psychological Science, 25(4), 943–953.)
  - _App-in-its-own-right:_ Stands alone as a 'Someone Like Me' near-peer belonging gallery for the Launchpad — a filterable wall of first-gen / DEIS / non-traditional-route Irish stories tagged to careers and routes, with the self-generated reflection beat. It is the same evidence-backed format as the strongest belonging interventions and shares the bank cleanly with How They Did It and Career Paths (which already use the immersiveDeck primitives), so the content is reusable across three tools.
  - _DEIS framing:_ This is the most cohort-specific concept: the evidence says similarity matters most precisely for the under-represented, and DEIS students are exactly who deflate when shown a polished surgeon-earning-€120k with no on-ramp. Leading with 'someone from a DEIS school who repeated and got there' and letting the student name their own out-of-reach feeling (rather than being told to feel confident) is the inclusive, non-exclusionary version — belonging and autonomy over high-achievement boasting.
  - _Evidence check:_ All four citations verified REAL and on-point via web search. Lockwood & Kunda 1997, JPSP 73(1):91-103 — confirmed; matches the attainable-inspires / unattainable-deflates + "still have time OR ability grows" claim verbatim. Walton & Cohen 2011, Science 331(6023):1447-1451, doi:10.1126/science.1198364 — confirmed; "framing social adversity as common and transient" + "self-generate the intervention message" + durable benefit to stigmatized students is exactly as cited, and the concept's interactive loop is a faithful instantiation. Marx & Roman 2002, PSPB 28(9):1183-1193 — confirmed; competent same-group role model buffers self-appraised ability/performance under threat. Stephens, Hamedani & Destin 2014, Psychological Science 25(4):943-953 — confirmed; difference-education using senior students' REAL-LIFE STORIES raised first-gen GPA (RCT N=168), directly supporting the near-peer-story format for first-gen students. Effect directions all match the literature; no inverted or mis-transferred mechanism found. The Plaskett et al. 2018 Phi Delta Kappan cite was not independently verified but is secondary, not load-bearing. No shaky citations.

- **More Than One Way In — the Plan-B pathway map that makes a setback survivable** [M] — _verdict: build, score 3.91_
  - Your Possible Life stops collapsing each career to a single lowest-points 'route in' and instead lays out the full spread of flexible pathways to it — direct CAO, a higher-points dream route, PLC→advanced entry, apprenticeship, repeat year, access route — as a branching map where 'if this blocks, here's the other way' is a first-class, recorded next action.
  - _Content:_ Author per-career pathway maps (extending careerPathsData / a new pathwaysData.ts) that enumerate, for each of the 27 careers, the realistic ROUTE SPREAD rather than just the floor: the typical/competitive CAO route, at least one lower-threshold or non-points route (PLC→degree advanced entry, apprenticeship, Level 6/7 stepping-stone, access/HEAR), and an explicit 'if you miss your first choice' Plan-B (repeat, deferred entry, related course that bridges in). Each route carries its real points/entry mechanic (reusing CAO_COURSES + the existing pathwayType field and the apprenticeship/PLC rows already in futureFinderData) and a one-line honesty note on time/cost trade-off. This directly fixes the documented gap where careerReach uses ONLY the lowest route and can mislead; here the spread is shown, with the cheap-Level-7-dragging-the-floor case made explicit rather than hidden. Add a small bank of 'a single blocked route is not the end' framing lines authored to hope-theory's pathways component.
  - _Functionality:_ Replace the single REALITY entry-points stat with a branching pathway map (animation-encodes-meaning): one node = the student's current points, fanning to multiple route nodes, each labelled reachable-now / a-stretch / open (reusing reachBucket). The mistake-first beat: it opens showing the all-or-nothing fear — ONE narrow door at the competitive points, framed as 'if this is the only way you see it, one bad day ends it' — then animates the other doors fanning IN, each becoming tappable to reveal its mechanic, visibly defusing the single-point-of-failure. The student picks a primary route AND nominates a Plan B (the agency component), and records one concrete next action ('I'll look up the PLC in X', 'I'll ask my counsellor about HEAR') that persists via the existing useEffortLifeSim Firestore hook alongside careerId/why. PLAN then sequences both the chosen route's grade-bargains and the Plan-B's lower threshold so closing the gap never reads as pass/fail. The map nodes also serve as the missing route-explorer the current tool lacks (it presently exposes only the floor number).
  - _Science:_ Hope is two trainable cognitions — agency ('I can') and pathways ('I see routes, including around obstacles'); hopeful thinkers imagine multiple workable pathways and persevere through blocks. Academic-specific hope correlates with GPA (~r=.39). (Snyder, C. R. (2002), 'Hope Theory: Rainbows in the Mind', Psychological Inquiry, 13(4), 249–275; Rand, Shanahan, Fischer & Fortney meta-analytic summaries of hope–GPA associations.) | Possible-selves interventions produce durable academic gains only when the future image is bound to concrete current strategies AND named obstacles (mental contrasting / WOOP) — the effect comes from the action-linkage and a recorded next step, not the daydream. (Oyserman, D., Bybee, D. & Terry, K. (2006), 'Possible Selves and Academic Outcomes: How and When Possible Selves Impel Action', Journal of Personality and Social Psychology, 91(1), 188–204.) | SKEPTICAL ANCHOR: belief-change nudges have small, heterogeneous effects and fail unless paired with real scaffolding (actual routes, study skills, supportive context); never sell mindset/belonging as a substitute for real pathways. (Sisk et al. (2018), Psychological Science, 29(4), 549–571; Yeager et al. (2019), 'A national experiment reveals where a growth mindset improves achievement', Nature, 573, 364–369.)
  - _App-in-its-own-right:_ Stands alone as a 'More Than One Way In' CAO Plan-B / pathway-spread explorer — a tool that takes any career and shows every realistic door (CAO, PLC, apprenticeship, access, repeat) with live reachability against the student's points and a recorded Plan B. It is directly useful in CAO season for the all-or-nothing points anxiety that crushes DEIS students, and could anchor College Compass / the CAO planning surface on its own.
  - _DEIS framing:_ This is the antidote to the single most damaging dynamic for DEIS students: treating the CAO as one narrow all-or-nothing door, where one missed grade reads as the end. Making Plan B a first-class, recorded choice — PLC→degree, apprenticeship, repeat, access route — turns a setback into a detour, supports autonomy and incremental progress, and pairs the hope/possible-selves nudge with the REAL scaffolding the skeptical evidence demands, rather than empty 'just believe in yourself' copy.
  - _Evidence check:_ All four citations verified real and accurately cited. Snyder, C. R. (2002), 'Hope Theory: Rainbows in the Mind', Psychological Inquiry 13(4):249-275 — confirmed (DOI 10.1207/S15327965PLI1304_01); agency+pathways two-component definition correct. Oyserman, Bybee & Terry (2006), JPSP 91(1):188-204 — confirmed (DOI 10.1037/0022-3514.91.1.188); the claimed conditional effect (possible-selves impel action only when linked to plausible strategies + social identity, in low-income/minority adolescents) matches the abstract exactly. Yeager et al. (2019), Nature 573:364-369 — confirmed (s41586-019-1466-y); the cited direction (gains concentrated in lower achievers, sustained only where peer norms align) is accurate and is used correctly as a scaffolding caveat, not oversold. Sisk et al. (2018), Psych Science 29(4):549-571 — well-known small/heterogeneous-effects meta-analysis, used legitimately as a skeptical anchor. No shaky or inverted citations; effect directions all match the literature. Separately verified against the codebase: the careerReach 'floor-only' behaviour the concept claims to fix is real (Math.min over CAO routes, components/effortLifeModel.ts:297), pathwayType plc/apprenticeship rows exist (futureFinderData.ts), and useEffortLifeSim already uses the getDoc-on-mount pattern (no staleness bug). The one inaccuracy is internal, not in the science: the pitch's '27 careers' should be 28, and the route-spread content it proposes to author already largely exists in careerPathsData.ts.

### `how-they-did-it`
Current — content depth **4/5**, functionality depth **3/5**. A swipeable role-model card deck. Student picks one of 4 barrier filters (Money was tight / I learn differently / New to Ireland-English / First in my family) or "Everyone", then swipes a Tinder-style 3-card stack (drag right = save, left = skip, tap = open) through 11 real people. Opening a card runs a 4-beat struggle-led reveal: front (hook or pull-quote) → "The hard part" (2-3 struggle beats, staggered in) → "What they did" (2-3 tap-to-flip 3D move tiles + a "Who helped" panel) → "Where they are now" (outcome + optional strengths reframe + a "Steal this move" action the student can bookmark, plus an expandable cited-sources disclosure). Saved moves accrue into a "Your playbook" view; finishing a filtered group shows a celebration screen with a saved-count and start-over. Progress (seenIds, savedIds) persists to Firestore via useHowTheyDidIt with sound effects and confetti on save/complete. Component index.tsx is 356 lines; it leans on shared immersiveDeck primitives (SwipeDeck, HybridCard, Celebration, useDeckSound) also used by Career Paths and Your Possible Life.
  - _Thinness:_ Hardcoded deck of exactly 11 entries in howTheyDidItData.ts (PEOPLE array, lines 16-329); peopleForBarrier() is a one-line static .filter on the `barrier` field.; Dead data field: every card defines an `emoji` (🥊, ✍️, 🎬…) and the type documents it as 'shown on the colour-world face until a real PNG is supplied', but the current component never renders emoji anywhere — it uses initials/portrait blobs. The field is now unused legacy data.; `portraitKey` is set on all 11 cards but 10 of the 11 referenced PNGs do not exist on disk (only kellie-harrington.png present), so the documented portrait feature is effectively a placeholder that degrades to initials.; Several cards carry empty-string fields the schema treats as optional: `quote: ''` (Roddy Doyle) and `strengthsLine: ''` (Kellie, Roddy, Brian, Tim) — content authored unevenly across the set.; Single barrier with only 2 people (financial), so that filtered deck is finished after 2 swipes — visibly thin compared to the other three groups.; Persistence model is minimal: HowTheyDidItState is just { seenIds: string[]; savedIds: string[]; updatedAt } with two mutators (markSeen, toggleSaved) in a 59-line hook.; Only test coverage is a single render-smoke test (test/howTheyDidIt.smoke.test.tsx, 29 lines) that just asserts the barrier picker mounts; no interaction/flow coverage.; studentSubjects prop is accepted in the type but unused — a vestige of an intended personalisation that was never wired up.
  - _Gaps:_ Only 11 people total, unevenly split across the 4 barriers: financial=2 (Kellie Harrington, Roddy Doyle), dyslexia=3, eal=3, first-gen=3. The 'Money was tight' filter yields just 2 cards, so that whole barrier group feels thin and finishes in two swipes.; The component signature accepts `studentSubjects?: string[]` but immediately discards it (only `uid` is destructured at index.tsx:121). Despite the header comment claiming it 'shares the engine with Career Paths' (which IS subject-matched via matchStrings per the project memory), there is NO subject/interest matching here — the deck cannot be personalised to a student's chosen subjects or career interest. Matching is barrier-only and manual.; Portrait assets are almost entirely missing: every PersonCard sets a `portraitKey` and the UI requests `/portraits/{key}.png`, but only 1 of 11 PNGs exists (public/portraits/kellie-harrington.png). The other 10 (Roddy Doyle, Keira Knightley, Steven Spielberg, etc.) silently fall back to serif initials via BlobIcon's onError handler — so 10/11 cards show monogram blobs, not faces.; No search, no sort, no 'show me only unseen', and no cross-barrier discovery beyond the single 'Everyone' bucket. The barrier filter is the only navigation axis.; No reflection/commitment loop: 'Steal this move' only toggles a bookmark (savedIds). There is no scheduling, reminder, write-your-own-plan, mark-as-done, or connection to the rest of the app's progress/guidance dashboard — the playbook is a static read-only list.; No analytics or pattern surfacing — unlike the Necessary Knowledge tools which write cross-module pattern signals, this tool records only seenIds/savedIds and exposes nothing about which barrier or move-types a student gravitates to.; Content scales only by hand-authoring more PersonCard entries; there is no data-driven ingestion, tagging beyond the single `barrier` enum, or relating people to one another.

- **Mirror — the matched-stories engine that finds 'someone like me'** [L] — _verdict: build, score 3.97_
  - Becomes a personalised role-model library that matches a student to people who share their *specific* barrier AND their subject/career interest, so the first card is always 'someone like me, who got where I want to go'.
  - _Content:_ Grow the corpus from 11 hand-authored people to 40-60, and add three new tag axes to every PersonCard alongside the existing single `barrier` enum: (1) `interestTags` (the career/field families already enumerated in careerPathsData's `matchStrings` — health, engineering, trades, media, education, etc.), (2) `routeTags` ('repeated the LC', 'PLC→degree', 'access/HEAR/DARE', 'apprenticeship', 'mature student', 'direct-entry scheme'), and (3) optional secondary barriers (a person can be both first-gen AND financial — the current model forces exactly one). Backfill the thin 'Money was tight' group (currently only 2 people) to parity with the others, and author multi-barrier and multi-interest people drawn from the same Irish-leaning, source-cited research discipline already used (every card keeps its 1-5 real URLs and the expandable 'Real story · N sources' disclosure). Each card also gains an explicit 'where they started vs where you are' line so the on-ramp, not the trophy, leads. Retire the dead `emoji` field (never rendered) and either supply the missing 10 portraits or formally make initials the canonical visual so the data stops documenting a feature that doesn't exist.
  - _Functionality:_ Wire up the `studentSubjects` prop that the component currently destructures and discards, and read the student's North Star driving-motivation + subject choices (already persisted in useNorthStar / progress). On entry, instead of the manual barrier picker being the ONLY axis, compute a ranked deck: a person scores higher when their barrier matches the student's self-identified barrier, their `interestTags` intersect the student's subjects/career interest (reuse the exact `matchStrings` intersection logic Career Paths already uses against CAO_COURSES), and they share a route the student is eligible for. The very first card surfaced is the best 'someone like me' match, with a small honest 'Matched to you because: first-in-family + interested in health' chip on the card face. Crucially — to honour Lockwood & Kunda — never hard-narrow to one route: below the matched deck sits a 'Other ways in' shelf so the student always sees multiple pathways, not a single deterministic answer. Keep the manual barrier filter as an override. Add a lightweight 'show me only people I haven't met' toggle (the seenIds data already exists but is unused for navigation) and a search-by-field box.
  - _Science:_ Relatability/similarity of the role model matters most for members of negatively-stereotyped or under-represented groups: a competent same-group model raises self-appraised ability, while members of non-stereotyped groups depend far less on matched models — so DEIS students meeting matched near-peers first will out-perform generic celebrity exemplars. (Marx, D. M. & Roman, J. S. (2002), 'Female Role Models: Protecting Women's Math Test Performance', PSPB, 28(9), 1183-1193; Lockwood, P. (2006), '"Someone Like Me Can Be Successful"', Psychology of Women Quarterly, 30(1), 36-46.) | Outstanding role models inspire only when success seems ATTAINABLE and the domain is self-relevant; when it looks unattainable the same star deflates the viewer — so the matched card must foreground the relatable on-ramp and the matching must surface, not narrow away, multiple routes. (Lockwood, P. & Kunda, Z. (1997), 'Superstars and Me: Predicting the Impact of Role Models on the Self', Journal of Personality and Social Psychology, 73(1), 91-103.)
  - _App-in-its-own-right:_ A standalone 'someone like me' discovery app: a searchable, multi-tag library of dozens of cited real-people stories that any DEIS student can filter by their own barrier(s), subjects and intended route — the kind of representation library guidance counsellors currently assemble by hand. It stops being a finite 11-card swipe and becomes a living, navigable corpus, which is exactly the gap (no search/sort/cross-barrier discovery) flagged in the current build.
  - _DEIS framing:_ Matching is framed as 'people who started where you are', never 'high achievers like you' — the chip names the shared *barrier and interest*, not an achievement tier. Because matching never collapses to one route and always shows 'Other ways in', a not-yet-ambitious student isn't told there's a single correct path; they're shown that several doors exist and at least one fits their situation. The honest source disclosure stays, so nothing reads as aspirational marketing.
  - _Evidence check:_ All three cited sources are real and correctly cited, and — crucially — the effect DIRECTIONS match the literature, so this is not buzzword laundering. (1) Lockwood & Kunda (1997), JPSP 73, 91-103 — VERIFIED verbatim; the paper's actual finding is that relevant superstars inspire when success seems ATTAINABLE but DEFLATE when unattainable. The concept does the rare correct thing: it designs to this boundary condition (foreground the on-ramp, never collapse to one route, keep an 'Other ways in' shelf) rather than just name-dropping the citation. (2) Marx & Roman (2002), PSPB 28(9), 1183-1193 — VERIFIED; a competent SAME-GROUP model buffered women's self-appraised math ability, and non-stereotyped members depend far less on matched models — exactly the asymmetry the pitch claims, and the match-on-shared-barrier mechanism instantiates the active ingredient. (3) The inherited Lin-Siegler et al. (2016) struggle-first claim (in the existing data header) also VERIFIED: struggle stories raised science grades with LOW-achievers benefiting most; achievement-only control did not — the correct direction for a DEIS cohort. ONE overclaim flagged: the pitch says it will read 'North Star driving-motivation' as an interest signal, but the NorthStar type is a category enum (~10 values like 'career-craft') + free-text statement, NOT a clean interestTags signal. The genuinely usable matching signal is subjectProfile.subjects[].subjectName — which is precisely the studentSubjects prop the component currently discards. So the interest-matching is feasible via subjects, but the North Star contribution is thinner than claimed.

- **Steal the Move — a commitment + belonging loop, not a read-only list** [M] — _verdict: build, score 4.46_
  - Turns the static 'Your playbook' bookmark list into an active loop where the student adapts each stolen move into their own this-week plan with a named obstacle, writes a one-line 'I felt behind too' belonging note in their own words, and ticks moves done.
  - _Content:_ Every PersonCard's single `stealThisMove` string grows into a small structured 'move kit': the original verb-led action, an editable template the student fills in ('My version: I'll ____ on ____ this week'), one or two named obstacle prompts pulled from that person's own struggle beats ('What gets in the way for you — like the showers Kellie didn't have?'), and an explicit Plan-B line so a blocked move never reads as failure ('If you can't get the script early, record yourself reading it cold instead'). Add a short 'belonging seed' to each person — a one-line, first-person normalisation of transient struggle ('Loads of people feel they don't belong in the room at first — it fades') that the student is then prompted to rewrite in their own words rather than just read. New copyable-action content is authored to the same evidence discipline; nothing becomes pure motivation — every saved item terminates in a concrete dated step.
  - _Functionality:_ Extend the deliberately-minimal HowTheyDidItState (currently just seenIds/savedIds/updatedAt) to store per-saved-move objects: { personId, myPlan, myObstacle, planB, doneAt, myBelongingNote }. The playbook becomes a working surface: each saved card flips between 'their move' and 'my version', has a checkbox to mark done (with a quiet success-green tick, animated as a single satisfying fill — meaning, not decoration), and shows a gentle 'you turned 3 of 5 saved moves into a plan' progress line. A short self-authored reflection step appears the first time the student saves anything ('When have you felt behind and then it got better? One line.') — the student generates the belonging message themselves rather than being told to feel confident. Optionally surface a 'share with your guidance counsellor' summary that writes a pattern signal (mirroring the Necessary Knowledge knowledgePatterns store) so the guidance dashboard can see which barriers/move-types a student is engaging with — closing the analytics gap flagged in the current build.
  - _Science:_ Possible-selves and aspiration effects come from binding a future image to a present strategy AND a named obstacle (mental contrasting / WOOP), not from imagery or inspiration alone — the action-linkage is what produces durable academic gains in low-income adolescents; pure aspiration-raising shows little-to-no effect. (Oyserman, D., Bybee, D. & Terry, K. (2006), 'Possible Selves and Academic Outcomes', JPSP, 91(1), 188-204; Education Endowment Foundation, 'Aspiration interventions', Teaching & Learning Toolkit ('raising aspirations in isolation will not be effective').) | Brief social-belonging interventions that frame early struggle as common and TEMPORARY, voiced in the first person and with the student self-generating the message, durably raise stigmatised students' GPA and close achievement gaps — distinct from, and stronger than, generic motivational copy. (Walton, G. M. & Cohen, G. L. (2011), 'A Brief Social-Belonging Intervention Improves Academic and Health Outcomes of Minority Students', Science, 331(6023), 1447-1451; Stephens, Hamedani & Destin (2014), 'Closing the Social-Class Achievement Gap', Psychological Science, 25(4), 943-953.)
  - _App-in-its-own-right:_ A personal 'borrowed-strategies' planner: a place where a student collects real moves from people who overcame their exact barrier, adapts each into a dated this-week action with an obstacle and a Plan B, marks them done, and keeps a private running note of 'times I felt behind and it passed'. That is a complete self-coaching app — the role-model deck becomes its front-end discovery layer rather than the whole product.
  - _DEIS framing:_ The loop is built around autonomy and incremental progress: the student writes their own plan, names their own obstacle, and authors their own belonging line — they are never told to 'dream big' or 'believe in yourself'. Plan B on every move defuses the all-or-nothing anxiety that crushes DEIS students. 'Turned 3 of 5 into a plan' celebrates effort and progress, not outcomes or points. The optional counsellor summary supports, rather than surveils, and is opt-in.
  - _Evidence check:_ All four citations verified as real and correctly attributed, and — the part most concepts fail — each cited source SUPPORTS the claim AND its effect direction. Oyserman, Bybee & Terry 2006 confirmed at JPSP 91(1):188-204 (PubMed 16834488); its finding is action-linkage, exactly what the design instantiates. Walton & Cohen 2011 confirmed at Science 331(6023):1447-1451 (doi:10.1126/science.1198364); abstract explicitly names 'framing social adversity as common and transient' and self-generating the message — the precise ingredients the belonging-note step copies. EEF Aspiration-interventions toolkit confirmed; the quoted line 'raising aspirations in isolation will not be effective' is accurate and the source adds 'effective approaches almost always have a significant academic component' — strengthens, not weakens, the concept. Stephens, Hamedani & Destin 2014 confirmed at Psych Sci 25(4):943-953 (PubMed 24553359). No shaky or inverted citations. One minor copy nit (not an evidence problem): the concept's designNotes say 'no coloured backgrounds' while the live component legitimately uses a per-barrier colour Band/tints — the concept stays inside that existing register, so this is an under-description, not a banned-pattern violation.

- **The Mistake They Almost Made — a mistake-first branching path through a real story** [L] — _verdict: build, score 4.18_
  - Adds an interactive 'at this fork, what would you do?' layer to each story where the student first commits to the tempting wrong move, sees the mark-loss / dead-end it leads to, then unlocks what the person actually did — turning passive swiping into mistake-first decision practice.
  - _Content:_ Author 2-3 real decision forks per person, sourced from their actual story, each as { situation, theTemptingWrongMove, whatThatCostsYou, whatTheyActuallyDid, theOpenDoorTheySaw }. Examples grounded in the existing data: Tim Sheridan — 'You're nowhere near the points for any Level 8. Do you (a) give up on a medical career, or (b)…?' → the tempting wrong move ('assume points are the only door') visibly closes the path, then his real move (work backwards from the job's *actual* entry rule — six ordinary passes) opens it. Suaad Alshleh — 'Your school doesn't offer the chemistry medicine needs' → wrong move 'pick a different career' vs her real move 'switch schools to chase the subject'. Each fork explicitly visualises an OPEN affordable/eligible path (SUSI, HEAR/DARE, PLC→degree, direct-entry) versus the closed-and-expensive assumption. Every fork is source-cited like the rest of the content.
  - _Functionality:_ Insert a new beat between 'struggle' and 'moves' in the existing 4-beat reveal (front → struggle → FORK → moves → now), so the engine and ProgressDots extend from 4 to 5 cleanly. At the fork the student taps the move they'd be tempted to make FIRST; an animation encodes the consequence — the chosen wrong path's route literally narrows/greys and a 'door' closes (a simple SVG path collapse, meaning-bearing not decorative), then the real move animates a second door opening. Store the student's fork choices so the closing screen can surface a gentle pattern ('You tended to assume the front door was the only way in — here's the Plan-B habit to build'), and write that as a pattern signal for the guidance dashboard. This converts the deck from a read/skip swipe into an active, mistake-first interactive consistent with the rest of the Learning Lab.
  - _Science:_ Making the path to college look OPEN and affordable raises current effort in low-income adolescents; making it look closed/expensive suppresses effort — and hope is operationally pathways ('I see routes around obstacles') + agency, with academic-specific hope correlating ~.39 with GPA, so a single blocked route must never read as the end. (Destin, M. & Oyserman, D. (2009), 'From Assets to School Outcomes', Psychological Science, 20(4), 414-418; Snyder, C. R. (2002), 'Hope Theory: Rainbows in the Mind', Psychological Inquiry, 13(4), 249-275.) | Effective role-model and aspiration interventions attribute success to copyable PROCESS at the decision points (struggle-led, the specific steps), not to genius or the polished end-state — leading with the move someone almost got wrong makes the process, not the trophy, the lesson. (Lin-Siegler, X. et al. (2016), 'Even Einstein Struggled: Effects of Learning About Great Scientists' Struggles on High School Students' Motivation to Learn Science', Journal of Educational Psychology, 108(3), 314-328; Lockwood & Kunda (1997), JPSP, 73(1), 91-103 (attainable on-ramp).)
  - _App-in-its-own-right:_ A decision-practice app built on real lives: a student plays through the actual forks faced by people who shared their barrier, makes the wrong call safely, watches a door close, then learns the move that opened a different one — accumulating a personal 'pathways instinct' (always look for the second door). That is a self-contained interactive-narrative learning product, not just a story library.
  - _DEIS framing:_ The whole device is anti all-or-nothing: it teaches that the obvious closed door (e.g. 'not enough points') is rarely the only one, which is precisely the hope/pathways antidote DEIS students need. It never frames the student as having failed for picking the tempting move — picking it is the point, and the reveal is supportive. Forks always centre an OPEN, affordable, eligible route (SUSI/HEAR/DARE/PLC/apprenticeship) so 'I can actually get there and pay for it' is concrete, not aspirational.
  - _Evidence check:_ All four citations verified real and on-point via web search. (1) Destin & Oyserman (2009), From Assets to School Outcomes, Psychological Science 20(4):414-418 — confirmed (PMID 19298260, DOI 10.1111/j.1467-9280.2009.02309.x); supports fewer-assets → lower expectations/effort, the open-vs-closed-door active ingredient. (2) Snyder (2002), Hope Theory: Rainbows in the Mind, Psychological Inquiry 13(4):249-275 — confirmed (DOI 10.1207/S15327965PLI1304_01); hope = pathways + agency, reorienting around barriers; the second-door reveal IS the pathways component. (3) Lin-Siegler et al. (2016), Even Einstein Struggled, J. Educational Psychology 108(3):314-328 — confirmed (ERIC EJ1096062); and the LOAD-BEARING direction checks out: struggle stories raised grades MOST for low-achieving/low-income 9th-10th graders, while achievement-ONLY stories LOWERED grades — exactly the lead-with-the-near-miss design. (4) Lockwood & Kunda (1997), Superstars and Me, JPSP 73:91-103 — confirmed; attainable > unattainable role models, domain-relevance matters. One minor flag: the specific "academic hope ~.39 with GPA" figure was not surfaced verbatim by search; the directional claim is well-supported by Snyder's review but the exact coefficient should be treated as non-load-bearing colour, not a hard cite. No fabricated or mis-applied sources found.

### `college-compass`
Current — content depth **5/5**, functionality depth **4/5**. College Compass is a year-aware roadmap of the Irish senior-cycle-to-college path. It renders a 6-stop vertical "trail" (Doors Open, Lock-In Day, The Access Window, Money Stops, Change Your Mind, Results & Offers) covering the CAO calendar plus HEAR/DARE/scholarship deadlines. Two modes driven by yearGroup: 6th years get the LIVE layer (live countdowns, a status-marked checklist persisted to Firestore, a sticky "Your next step" banner, past/now/future rail dots); TY/5th years get an ORIENTATION preview (no countdowns/pressure, calm "year ahead" framing). Dates are stored cycle-relative (month/day, no year) and resolved at runtime to whichever entry cycle is still ahead, so a passed deadline is never shown as upcoming and the trail auto-rolls each year. Each stop expands to a "why it matters" callout, tap-to-reveal myth-buster flip cards, and cross-links to sibling tools (Future Finder, CAO Simulator, Points Passport). Three stops embed genuinely interactive mini-tools: a HEAR eligibility meter (income hard-gate + 4 scored indicators summing to a 7-pt scale with Eligible/Priority thresholds), a two-gate DARE self-check (12 diagnosis categories x impact-indicator logic), a Money Sorter (8 funding items bucketed into means/merit/automatic/admission), and a live DocumentChecklist that builds a personalised post-list from the HEAR/DARE selections. Also: a target-college picker (persisted), a Lock-In restricted-course quick check, and a "How dates work" verify modal listing official sources. The completion model (computeCompassProgress/itemStatus) is shared verbatim with the GC dashboard so what the student marks is exactly what the counsellor sees (no copy, no drift), and is regression-tested.
  - _Thinness:_ Exactly 6 trail stops, hardcoded as a static JOURNEY_STOPS array (collegeCompassData.ts lines 99-297).; 13-16 total checklist items across the whole trail, hardcoded (e.g. Doors Open has 2, Lock-In 3, Access Window 4, Money Stops 3, Change of Mind 1, Results 2).; HEAR self-check = 5 fixed indicators (HEAR_INDICATORS) with hardcoded point weights (2.5/2/1.5/1) and 2 hardcoded thresholds (2.5 eligible, 5.5 priority).; DARE self-check = 12 hardcoded categories and 6 hardcoded impact indicators; the 'meets impact' rule is a hardcoded count (>=2, or attainment + >=1 for literacy/numeracy).; MoneySorter = 8 hardcoded MONEY_ITEMS across 4 buckets; RestrictedCheck = 5 hardcoded course types.; Myth flip cards are static claim/verdict/explainer triples (1-3 per stop, ~9 total), purely tap-to-reveal text, no scoring or interaction beyond the flip.; Approximate '~' date labels for non-CAO deadlines (SUSI ~Apr, Bursary ~Oct, forms ~1 Mar) signal placeholder precision.; DareGate impact answers held in component-local useState and discarded on unmount — not part of the persisted state shape.; targetInstitutionCodes is persisted but read by no downstream feature (collected then unused).; Repeated hardcoded 'estimate only / confirm on the official source' disclaimers acknowledge the figures are non-authoritative static reference data.
  - _Gaps:_ DareGate 'impact' indicators are local useState (DareGate.tsx line 26), NOT persisted via the hook — only dareCategoryId is saved. So on tool re-entry the second DARE gate resets to empty even though the category restores, and the DocumentChecklist (which only consumes hearIndicators + dareCategoryId) never reflects the impact answers at all. This is an asymmetry vs HearMeter, whose selections ARE persisted (setHearIndicators).; The 6 trail stops and all eligibility logic are entirely hardcoded in collegeCompassData.ts; there is no live data feed from cao.ie/accesscollege.ie/susi.ie. Every date, points value, threshold and euro figure is a static 2026-cycle reference value gated behind COMPASS_LAST_VERIFIED='2026-05-29' and a prominent 'must be re-checked each year' warning — accuracy depends entirely on manual annual maintenance.; Cycle-date resolution uses calendar dates only with descriptive labels like '~1 Mar (forms)', '~10 Mar (documents)', 'SUSI ~Apr' — the HEAR/DARE/SUSI dates are approximate placeholders ('~'), not exact published deadlines, so the countdowns for the access/money stops are intentionally fuzzy.; toggleDismissStop exists in the hook (and the GC progress model honours dismissedStops in the denominator) but is NOT wired into the CollegeCompass UI — there is no button for a student to hide a stop they're not applying for (e.g. DARE), so the dismissal feature is dead from the student side.; No notifications/calendar export — despite being a 'deadline roadmap', there is no .ics export, no reminder scheduling, no email/push; the countdown only updates when the app is open and recomputes from new Date() at mount.; Target colleges are collected (persisted to targetInstitutionCodes) and the copy says 'we'll keep them in mind for the money stop', but the selection is never actually USED anywhere downstream — MoneySorter shows the same 8 generic items regardless of target colleges. It's a stored-but-inert input.; The checklist is a flat status toggle (not-started/in-progress/done) with no due-date binding per item, no per-item reminders, and no link from a checklist item to the relevant external action page.

- **The Open Door — a 'can I actually afford and get into this?' affordability engine** [L] — _verdict: ?, score ?_
  - College Compass grows from a deadline roadmap into a personalised affordability-and-access calculator that shows a DEIS student, in real euro and real points, that the door to their chosen course is open — with grants, the reduced-points HEAR/DARE route, and a PLC fallback all stacked onto one concrete plan.
  - _Content:_ Author a cost-and-funding content layer the tool currently lacks. (1) A 'real cost of first year' breakdown per institution type (university / TU / IoT / living-at-home vs away), built from HEA Student Contribution + SUSI maintenance bands + a researched accommodation/transport estimate, every figure source-stamped to susi.ie/hea.ie and gated behind the existing COMPASS_LAST_VERIFIED discipline. (2) Wire the already-collected-but-inert `targetInstitutionCodes` into MoneySorter so the funding items shown are the ones that actually exist at the colleges the student picked (entrance scholarships, college hardship funds, the relevant access bursary), closing the 'stored-but-unused' gap noted in the scan. (3) A SUSI band self-estimate (household income + family size -> 'likely full grant / part grant / fees-only / above threshold' as an estimate, not a decision) reusing the HearMeter income-gate pattern. (4) Short, source-cited 'how someone in your situation actually paid for it' worked examples (e.g. 'lived at home, full SUSI maintenance, Free Fees covered tuition, Student Assistance Fund for the laptop').
  - _Functionality:_ A single 'Your way in' summary screen that fuses three engineered outputs: (a) the points gap to the target course pulled in from the existing CAO Simulator / Points Passport cross-links (so this stays a navigator, not a re-implementation of points maths); (b) the HEAR/DARE reduced-points route from the existing HearMeter/DareGate outputs, shown as a SECOND, lower door beside the standard points door; (c) a personalised funding stack from the income self-estimate. Mistake-first framing: the default first view is the 'closed door' a student fears — full sticker price, top-line points — then an animation pulls the real numbers down (Free Fees removes tuition, SUSI removes maintenance, HEAR lowers the points bar) to reveal the actual, lower door. Persist the income-estimate and target-college selections through the existing useCollegeCompass hook (additive merge, same pattern as setHearIndicators), and surface the resulting 'door is open / partly open / check these three things' status on the GC dashboard via the shared computeCompassProgress-style model so counsellors see who still believes the door is shut.
  - _Science:_ Making the path to college look OPEN and affordable (need-based aid available, points achievable, route exists) raises current school effort in low-income adolescents, while a closed/expensive-looking path suppresses it; belief that effort pays off is contingent on a visible open pathway. (Destin, M. & Oyserman, D. (2009), 'From Assets to School Outcomes: How Finances Shape Children's Perceived Possibilities and Intentions', Psychological Science, 20(4), 414-418; Destin & Oyserman (2010), 'Incentivizing Education', Journal of Experimental Social Psychology, 46(5), 846-849.) | Aspiration-raising in isolation does not move attainment; effective approaches almost always attach a concrete academic/navigational component — every aspirational surface must terminate in a concrete next step (a route, a points target, an application step), never a daydream. (Education Endowment Foundation, 'Aspiration interventions', Teaching & Learning Toolkit; Kintrea, St Clair & Houston (2011), 'The influence of parents, places and poverty on educational attitudes and aspirations' (JRF).) | Hope is two trainable cognitions — agency and pathways ('I see routes, including around obstacles'); academic-specific hope correlates with GPA at ~r=.39, far above generalized hope, so designs should always show multiple workable routes. (Snyder, C. R. (2002), 'Hope Theory: Rainbows in the Mind', Psychological Inquiry, 13(4), 249-275.)
  - _App-in-its-own-right:_ A standalone 'Can I afford college?' / 'What's my real way in?' app is a genuine product gap in the Irish market — most affordability information is scattered across susi.ie, hea.ie and individual college pages. A DEIS student (or parent) could open it cold, enter household situation + a target course, and get one honest, source-cited 'here is your actual cost and your actual route in (standard points, HEAR/DARE door, or PLC ladder)' answer. That is a reason-to-exist independent of the rest of the platform.
  - _DEIS framing:_ This is the single highest-leverage inclusive move for the cohort: it directly attacks the 'college isn't for people like me / we can't afford it' belief that suppresses effort, using the exact Destin & Oyserman mechanism. Frame every output around 'here's how someone in YOUR situation gets there', never around high points or prestige. The HEAR/DARE 'second, lower door' and the PLC ladder are shown as equally valid routes (not consolation prizes), and the income self-estimate is explicitly an estimate that never gatekeeps or judges. No high-achiever framing anywhere — the win state is 'the door is open', not 'you scored highly'.

- **Route Map — multiple pathways, a built-in Plan B, and 'someone like me took this exact road'** [L] — _verdict: build, score 4.15_
  - The tool stops modelling a single linear deadline trail and becomes a branching route map to a chosen course, where every route (direct CAO, HEAR/DARE reduced-points, PLC/QQI ladder, apprenticeship, repeat year) is a first-class path with its own deadlines, paired with a short near-peer story of someone who actually took that exact road.
  - _Content:_ Author a routes-to-a-destination content set that the tool currently pushes entirely out to cross-links. For a starter set of common DEIS-relevant destinations (nursing, computing, engineering, primary teaching, trades/apprenticeship, business) author 3-4 named routes each — direct CAO, PLC/QQI L5 ladder, apprenticeship, access/reduced-points, repeat — reusing the researched `routes` data already authored in careerPathsData.ts (e.g. 'Pre-Nursing PLC (QQI L5) -> degree', 'apprenticeship, earn while you learn, no CAO points') so this is curation, not invention. Each route carries its own deadline anchors fed through the existing cycle-relative date engine. Pair each route with one source-cited near-peer 'someone like me' micro-story drawn from / in the voice of the howTheyDidIt evidence model (struggle-led, copyable process, 'started where you are', attainable framing, Irish-leaning) — explicitly tagged for relatability (first-in-family, DEIS school, took the long way round, repeated/PLC).
  - _Functionality:_ A branching SVG route map (reusing the existing vertical-trail + rail-dot idiom, but forking into parallel lanes) where the student picks a destination and sees every viable lane, not one. Core interaction is mistake-first and Plan-B-led: the tool first shows the all-or-nothing trap students fear ('I need 480 points or it's over'), then animates the branch revealing 'if that's blocked, here's the PLC->degree door and here's who walked through it'. A persisted 'My route + my Plan B' selection (additive merge via the hook) records the student's chosen primary route AND a named backup, plus a single concrete next action they type themselves (the agency component). The dismissedStops hook function — currently dead in the UI per the scan — gets wired here so a student can collapse routes they're not taking. GC dashboard reads, via the shared progress model, whether each student has a route AND a Plan B recorded (a counsellor-actionable 'no backup yet' flag).
  - _Science:_ Hope's 'pathways' component plus the ~.39 academic-hope/GPA link mean a single blocked route must never read as the end; multiple flexible pathways and an explicit Plan B (alternative courses, PLC/access/repeat routes) defuse all-or-nothing points anxiety, paired with a recorded next action for the 'agency' component. (Snyder, C. R. (2002), 'Hope Theory: Rainbows in the Mind', Psychological Inquiry, 13(4), 249-275.) | Outstanding models inspire only when success looks ATTAINABLE and the domain is self-relevant; lead with the relatable on-ramp and specific path ('she repeated, did a PLC, then got in'), not the polished end-state, or the exemplar deflates rather than inspires. (Lockwood, P. & Kunda, Z. (1997), 'Superstars and Me: Predicting the Impact of Role Models on the Self', Journal of Personality and Social Psychology, 73(1), 91-103.) | Relatability/similarity of the model matters most for under-represented groups: a competent SAME-group / first-gen near-peer who took the access/PLC route buffers self-efficacy and outperforms generic celebrity exemplars for first-gen/low-income students. (Marx, D. M. & Roman, J. S. (2002), 'Female Role Models: Protecting Women's Math Test Performance', PSPB, 28(9), 1183-1193; Plaskett, Bali, Nakkula & Harris (2018), 'Peer mentoring to support first-generation low-income college students', Phi Delta Kappan.)
  - _App-in-its-own-right:_ A standalone 'every road to the course you want' app fills a real gap — Irish students overwhelmingly believe the CAO points race is the only route and don't know the PLC/QQI ladder, apprenticeship, or access doors lead to the same destinations. A branching route map with deadlines and a named backup is a self-contained planning product a student could use without any other tool, and it is exactly the 'pathways' artefact hope theory prescribes.
  - _DEIS framing:_ Directly built for 'not-yet-ambitious' and first-gen students: it normalises the non-linear route (PLC, repeat, apprenticeship) as a real and respected way in, not a fallback for failures, which is precisely what removes the all-or-nothing points dread that crushes DEIS students at the cut-off. The matched near-peer is the default-first card and is chosen for similarity (DEIS, first-in-family, took the long way), per Marx & Roman / Lockwood. Avoid implying the matched route is the only route — always show the full fork.
  - _Evidence check:_ All four citations verified real and on-point via web search. (1) Snyder 2002 'Hope Theory: Rainbows in the Mind', Psychological Inquiry 13(4) — confirmed; pathways + agency decomposition and academic-outcome link are accurately represented. The specific ~.39 hope/GPA figure is cited a bit loosely (literature gives a range) but the direction is correct — minor, not disqualifying. (2) Lockwood & Kunda 1997, JPSP 73(1), 91-103 — confirmed; the attainable-inspires / unattainable-deflates finding is applied in the correct direction. (3) Marx & Roman 2002, PSPB 28(9), 1183-1193 — confirmed real, correct pages/DOI; supports competent same-group role model buffering performance (cited for under-represented similarity — fair extension, though the original is a stereotype-threat/women-in-math study, so the first-gen/low-income generalisation leans partly on citation 4). (4) Plaskett, Bali, Nakkula & Harris 2018, Phi Delta Kappan 99(7), 47-51 — confirmed real; explicitly about relatable peer mentors for first-generation low-income students, directly on-point. No shaky or invented citations. Bonus: the existing howTheyDidIt content the concept reuses is already written to this evidence model and already cites Lin-Siegler and Lockwood & Kunda, so the struggle-first framing is not retrofitted — it is the platform's established, verified pattern.

- **Runway — a self-updating deadline companion with reminders, a co-pilot for parents, and a near-peer 'I felt behind too' belonging layer** [M] — _verdict: build, score 3.98_
  - The roadmap becomes an active companion that actually keeps time for you — exporting deadlines to the student's calendar, sending reminders, sharing a read-only view a first-gen parent can follow, and opening each application stage with a short near-peer 'I felt out of my depth too, and here's what I did' note so the process feels survivable, not just scheduled.
  - _Content:_ Author the missing per-deadline 'this is normal and survivable' belonging content. For each application stage (CAO open, Lock-In, the Access Window, SUSI, Change of Mind, results) write a short first-person near-peer reflection in the validated difference-education format — 'I felt behind / out of place / convinced I'd messed it up, and here's the specific thing I did and that it passed' — sourced to / written in the howTheyDidIt evidence voice, plus a one-line self-reflection prompt the student answers themselves rather than being told to feel confident. Add a 'parent / supporter' explainer set: plain-language, jargon-free one-liners on what each deadline means and what the family actually needs to do (the navigational knowledge first-gen families are documented to lack), with the same source-cited, estimate-only discipline.
  - _Functionality:_ Close the 'no notifications/calendar export' gap the scan flags as the core contradiction of a 'deadline roadmap'. (1) Generate an .ics export from the existing resolveCycleDate / computeStopStates engine so every resolved deadline drops into the student's own calendar — this is a pure transform of data the engine already produces, with reminder lead-times baked into the events. (2) Optional reminders via the platform's push capability for the next live deadline. (3) A read-only shareable 'family view' link/summary so a parent who isn't a platform user can follow the same trail (mirrors the existing GC read-only progress model). (4) Persist each stage's self-reflection answer through the hook (additive merge), and finally PERSIST the DARE impact indicators — the scan identifies these as held in component-local useState and discarded on unmount, so the DocumentChecklist never reflects them; lift them into the hook the same way hearIndicators are, fixing a real bug. (5) Surface 'reflection done / felt behind here' signals to the GC dashboard via the shared progress model so a counsellor can reach out at the exact stage a student flagged.
  - _Science:_ A brief belonging intervention — framing early struggle as common and TEMPORARY, voiced by senior students who felt the same and then belonged, with the student self-generating the message — durably raises stigmatised students' GPA and closes achievement gaps; difference-education using seniors' real stories eliminated the social-class gap by increasing help-seeking. (Walton, G. M. & Cohen, G. L. (2011), 'A Brief Social-Belonging Intervention Improves Academic and Health Outcomes of Minority Students', Science, 331(6023), 1447-1451; Stephens, Hamedani & Destin (2014), 'Closing the Social-Class Achievement Gap', Psychological Science, 25(4), 943-953.) | For disadvantaged students the real gap is not aspiration but the navigational knowledge/expectation of how to fulfil it; effective support is concrete day-to-day 'how to do this' help (careers advice, knowing the steps), not aspiration-raising. (Kintrea, St Clair & Houston (2011), JRF; Carter-Wall & Whitfield (2012), 'The role of aspirations, attitudes and behaviour in closing the educational attainment gap' (JRF).) | Psychological nudges help disadvantaged/at-risk students more than others but only when the surrounding context and real opportunity support the message; belief-change must be paired with concrete scaffolding (real routes, real reminders), never sold as a substitute. (Sisk, V. F. et al. (2018), 'To What Extent and Under Which Circumstances Are Growth Mind-Sets Important to Academic Achievement?', Psychological Science, 29(4), 549-571; Yeager, D. S. et al. (2019), 'A national experiment reveals where a growth mindset improves achievement', Nature, 573, 364-369.)
  - _App-in-its-own-right:_ A standalone 'CAO deadline companion' that lives in your calendar, nudges you before each gate, lets a parent follow along, and reassures you that the wobble you're feeling is normal would be a genuinely useful, self-contained app — it is the thing a 'deadline roadmap' implicitly promises but the current tool can't deliver because it only recomputes when open. The parent-view and belonging layer make it more than a calendar: it's the navigational + emotional companion the JRF evidence says first-gen families actually need.
  - _DEIS framing:_ Squarely inclusive: the JRF finding is that DEIS families' gap is navigational, not motivational — so the parent/supporter view and the calendar reminders ARE the intervention, handing first-gen households the day-to-day 'what do we do and when' knowledge they're documented to lack. The belonging layer normalises the 'am I even university material / I feel behind' wobble as common and transient in near-peers' own voices and lets the student articulate it themselves (Walton & Cohen / Stephens), rather than any 'just believe in yourself' copy the Sisk/Yeager evidence warns against. Nothing here is gated on ambition or achievement; it meets a not-yet-ambitious student exactly where they are.
  - _Evidence check:_ All four academic citations verified real, correctly attributed, and on-point — exact authors/journals/volumes/pages confirmed via web search: Walton & Cohen (2011) Science 331:1447-1451 (belonging framing of adversity as common/transient + self-generated message raised minority GPA and halved the gap); Stephens, Hamedani & Destin (2014) Psychological Science 25(4):943-953 (difference-education with seniors' real stories closed the first-gen gap via help-seeking); Kintrea, St Clair & Houston (2011) JRF (aspirations high across social groups, disadvantaged students lack belief/navigational capacity — supports navigational-not-motivational); Sisk et al. (2018) Psych Science + Yeager et al. (2019) Nature 573:364-369 (belief-change effects weak overall but stronger for low-SES/at-risk students and only when context supports — concept uses this correctly as a brake against bootstraps copy). Effect directions all match. No shaky or inverted citations. Technical claims partially verified against code: the DARE-impact persistence gap is REAL (DareGate local useState, lost on unmount) but its stated DocumentChecklist payoff is INACCURATE (buildDocumentPack ignores impact indicators); the 'push capability' is OVERSTATED (no FCM/web-push exists — in-app feed only); the 'read-only family link' is NOT the authenticated GC model it claims and carries an unaddressed minors'-privacy concern; the .ics-from-engine claim is accurate and sound.

### `planner`
