Current — content depth **4/5**, functionality depth **5/5**. The Spaced Repetition Timetable ("planner") is a genuinely data-driven study planner, not a static schedule. Given a student's subject profile (current/target grades or JC bands, rest days, block duration, exam date), it computes a per-subject priority score, allocates a weekly session budget across subjects, and lays the sessions out across the 7 days with spacing logic, daily caps, and weekend loading. The UI (1260-line component) offers a Day view (horizontal day-tab pills, today summary card with an animated SVG progress ring + "Next up", per-block cards), a Week view (7-column grid), week navigation, inline rest-day toggling, a "Priority Breakdown" bar chart, and an expandable "How is this calculated?" explainer that shows per-subject score math, a projected CAO Best-6 total out of 625, and a deprioritise-suggestion panel for subjects outside the top 6. Tapping a block opens a modal with Study Now (launches a real StudySessionView with timer + teach-back + debrief), "Already Studied (+5 pts)", and a 20-point "Skip Session" gamification option. It integrates live with: topic mastery (shaky topics raise priority and surface as per-block "Focus:" suggestions), study-debrief strategy hints (per-subject "Try: <strategy>"), school events (GC key events render on the relevant day), a study streak, and a points economy. It is curriculum-aware (separate senior CAO-points-deficit vs junior band-deficit priority paths). The algorithm file (820 lines) implements a real SM-2 (SuperMemo) spaced-repetition engine, sustainability caps (max weekly hours scaling with exam proximity), a log-scaled proportional allocator with max-ratio clamping, and an SM-2-aware day-assignment placer that maximises same-subject spacing.
  - _Thinness:_ studiedMinutes = totalBlocks * 45 and totalMinutes = totalSessions * 45 — duration hardcoded to 45 (lines 395, 419) despite configurable blockDuration; weeksUntilExam = 22 magic-number placeholder for pre-exam juniors (line 350); sm2States argument passed as `undefined` at the live call site (line 410: generateWeeklyTimetable(allocations, weeksUntilExam, weekOffset, restDaysArray, blockDuration, undefined, topicMastery)); SM-2 engine never receives state -> session-type chosen by seededRandom fallback; longestStreak: streak returned equal to currentStreak in computeStreak (line 795) — not independently tracked; 'Already Studied (+5 pts)' and 'Skip Session (20 pts)' point values hardcoded inline in JSX (lines 1234, 1242); deprioritise panel uses raw #F59E0B amber + boxShadow (lines 1127-1135) — off the documented design-token palette (amber is a banned primary UI colour per CLAUDE.md); _PRIORITY_BAR_COLORS / _PRIORITY_BAR_INLINE are dead/empty maps left in source (lines 245-255); several underscore-prefixed unused vars (_bargainPts, _onBlockDurationChange, _daysUntilExam, _totalHours); syllabus efficiency data exists for only 29 of 54 selectable subjects; SUBJECT_COLORS / SUBJECT_HEX are large static literal maps (~30 entries each) duplicated as Tailwind-class and hex variants
  - _Gaps:_ SM-2 read-back is broken: the centerpiece SM-2 engine (~130 lines: easeFactor/interval/repetitions, updateSM2, qualityFromDebrief) is fully implemented AND persisted to Firestore via hooks/useDebriefSideEffects.ts on every debrief — but SpacedRepetitionTimetable.tsx calls allocateSessions(priorities, ...) and generateWeeklyTimetable(allocations, ..., undefined, topicMastery) with the sm2States argument left undefined (line 403 + 410). So the persisted per-subject ease factor / due-interval is never fed back into session counts OR session-type selection. The tool is named 'Spaced Repetition Timetable' but the actual SM-2 spacing it accumulates is dead-ended — every call site in the repo (InnovationZone, WarRoom, StudySessionView, KnowledgeTree) also passes no sm2States. The visible spacing is only the geometric 'spread sessions evenly across available days' placer, not memory-model-driven.; Session-type selection (Learn/Practice/Revise) falls back to a SEEDED-RANDOM coin flip (seededRandom(hashString(subjectName)+i+weekOffset*1000), lines 569-578 of timetableAlgorithm.ts) because sm2 is always undefined. The intended SM-2-driven branch (repetitions===0 -> new-learning, etc., lines 558-566) never executes in the live app. So whether a given block is 'Learn' vs 'Practice' is effectively pseudo-random, not pedagogically derived.; Hardcoded 45-minute assumption for studied-time accounting: studiedMinutes = totalBlocks * 45 (line 395) and totalMinutes = totalSessions * 45 (line 419) are hardcoded to 45 even though profile.defaultBlockDuration is configurable and onBlockDurationChange exists — a student who sets 25- or 60-min blocks gets a wrong 'Xh Ym this week' figure.; Pre-exam Junior (1st/2nd year) hardcodes weeksUntilExam = 22 (line 350) as a magic number to force the intensity factor to ~0; meaningful but a placeholder cadence, not a real schedule horizon.; Syllabus efficiency multiplier only covers 29 subjects (subject: entries in syllabusData.ts) out of 54 selectable LC_SUBJECTS — the remaining ~25 minority subjects silently fall back to efficiencyMultiplier = 1.0 with no boost signal.; computeStreak returns longestStreak = currentStreak (line 795 of timetableAlgorithm.ts) — longest streak is not actually tracked across history in the algorithm; the real longest is reconstructed only in InnovationZone via Math.max with the stored value, so the function's own longestStreak output is misleading.; The 'Already Studied (+5 pts)' button label hardcodes +5 but the Quick Debrief path uses a separate QUICK_DEBRIEF_POINTS constant — point values are scattered and not centrally defined.; onBlockDurationChange is wired through props and InnovationZone but the component itself never calls it (destructured as _onBlockDurationChange) — there is no in-tool UI to change block duration; it only changes via the settings/onboarding flow.; Priority labels (High/Medium/Low) for junior students are computed off priorityScore thirds, but the explainer panel that documents the formula is senior-only (gated on !isJunior, line 942/964), so JC users get a bar chart with High/Medium/Low badges and no explanation of how bands drive them.

- **The Intention Engine — from a timetable you read to a plan you actually do** [M] — _verdict: build, score 4.31_
  - Every scheduled block grows a bound if-then cue and an outside-view completion check, turning a stated intention into an enacted one and surfacing the plan→do→reflect→re-plan loop as one designed cycle.
  - _Content:_ Author a library of ~120 subject-and-context-specific implementation-intention templates (the editable cue half of an 'when [specific situation], then I will [this block, here]' line). Source them from the cue grammar that ALREADY exists and works in components/CatchUpLane/Comeback.tsx (lines 253-258: a fixed trigger + an editable '…then I'll…' textarea). Promote that primitive out of the comeback-only flow into the mainstream planner, but expand the trigger bank from the handful of comeback triggers into a DEIS-grounded set keyed on the real anchor moments of an after-school evening: 'When I get home and drop my bag', 'When the dinner plates are cleared', 'When my phone hits 8pm', 'When I get off the bus', 'When my younger siblings are settled'. Add a per-subject 'obstacle bank' (also already modelled in Comeback's obstacleIds step) — concrete, non-judgemental anticipated obstacles ('phone keeps pinging', 'too tired after training', 'nowhere quiet at home') each paired with a coping line. Write a short, plain-language 'why this works' card citing the d≈0.65 evidence, framed for autonomy ('this is your plan, in your words'), never as a productivity lecture. Add a weekly 'You-versus-You' recalibration copy bank that reports completion honestly ('You usually finish about 6 of 9 — here's a 6-block week you'll actually close') drawn from the student's own stored completions rather than an idealised target.
  - _Functionality:_ 1) Bind a cue to a block: tapping a block in the existing block-action modal (SpacedRepetitionTimetable.tsx line ~1194) adds an 'Add your if-then' affordance — a fixed trigger chip the student picks plus a free-text 'then I will' field, persisted per blockId to the timetableCompletions sibling store. The bound cue then renders on the block card itself (a small quoted line under the subject), so the plan the student reads back is concrete and theirs. 2) Outside-view recalibration: read the completions data the tool ALREADY stores (completions[dateKey]) to compute a rolling completion rate, then offer next week at the student's real historic rate instead of the idealised allocator output — keep the existing sustainability caps as a ceiling, but lower the FLOOR to what they actually finish. This fixes the planning-fallacy gap by using data the repo already has. 3) Make the self-regulation loop visible: a slim, persistent 'Plan → Do → Reflect → Re-plan' status strip that lights the stage you're in (plan = blocks set; do = StudySessionView; reflect = debrief saved; re-plan = next week recalibrated), so the student experiences it as one cycle, not a one-shot schedule. 4) Fix the hardcoded-45 bug as part of the recalibration math: studied-minutes accounting reads profile.defaultBlockDuration instead of the literal 45 (timetableAlgorithm.ts lines 395, 419), so the honest 'a plan you'll finish' number is actually correct.
  - _Science:_ Implementation intentions ('when situation X, then I will do Y') have a medium-to-large effect (d≈0.65 across 94 studies, >8,000 participants) on actually enacting goals — far more than forming a goal intention alone. A timetable is a stated intention; without a cue-binding layer it predictably under-delivers. (Gollwitzer, P. M., & Sheeran, P. (2006). Implementation Intentions and Goal Achievement: A Meta-Analysis of Effects and Processes. Advances in Experimental Social Psychology, 38, 69–119.) | Intentions explain under ~30% of variance in action; short-term desires, habits and norms routinely override them. Concrete, autonomy-framed, personally-held goals translate to action better than abstract or socially-pressured ones — converging with the DEIS guidance to avoid exclusionary high-achievement copy. (Sheeran, P., & Webb, T. L. (2016). The Intention–Behavior Gap. Social and Personality Psychology Compass, 10(9), 503–518.) | Students underestimate task completion time (the planning fallacy): predicted 33.9 days, took 55.5 (~64% longer); only 30% finished by their '99%-certain' date. The remedy is reference-class / outside-view estimation from the student's own past completions. (Buehler, R., Griffin, D., & Ross, M. (1994). Exploring the 'Planning Fallacy': Why People Underestimate Their Task Completion Times. Journal of Personality and Social Psychology, 67(3), 366–381.) | Self-regulated learning is a cycle (forethought → performance → self-reflection) where reflection feeds the next plan; a tool doing only forethought (generate a plan) and skipping monitoring and reflection is missing two-thirds of the loop. (Zimmerman, B. J. (2002). Becoming a Self-Regulated Learner: An Overview. Theory Into Practice, 41(2), 64–70.)
  - _App-in-its-own-right:_ This is the 'why doesn't my timetable work?' app — a standalone study-follow-through coach. Most timetable apps stop at generating a grid; this one closes the intention-behaviour gap with cue-binding and outside-view recalibration, which no Irish-market study planner does. It stands alone as 'the planner that's honest about what you'll actually finish, and helps you finish it' — a distinct product wedge versus generic scheduling tools.
  - _DEIS framing:_ Directly serves not-yet-ambitious DEIS students: the cue bank is built around real home obstacles (no quiet room, caring for siblings, after-training tiredness) and the copy is autonomy/mastery framed ('your plan, in your words') with zero high-achievement pressure. The outside-view recalibration is explicitly anti-shame — it offers a plan you'll actually close rather than an aspirational one you'll fail, protecting the at-risk cohort from the demotivation of a broken plan. It promotes the supportive Comeback/Catch-Up Lane mechanism into everyday use rather than reserving it for students already in crisis.
  - _Evidence check:_ All four cited sources are real, correctly cited, and support their claims; effect directions match the literature. VERIFIED via web search: Gollwitzer & Sheeran (2006), Advances in Experimental Social Psychology 38, 69-119 — d=.65 across 94 independent tests confirmed verbatim; the if-then construct is the paper's actual mechanism. Buehler, Griffin & Ross (1994), JPSP 67(3), 366-381 — predicted 33.9 days confirmed; outside-view/reference-class is the genuine remedy from this exact paper (the 55.5-actual and 30%-by-99%-certain figures are well-established findings of it). Zimmerman (2002), Theory Into Practice 41(2), 64-70 — forethought/performance/self-reflection cycle confirmed exactly. Sheeran & Webb (2016), Social and Personality Psychology Compass 10(9), 503-518 — real, the intention-behaviour gap paper; the '<30% of variance' framing is consistent with this literature. CODE CLAIMS: verified the Comeback if-then primitive (Comeback.tsx 253-258), obstacle bank (54-61), CYAN=#0E9AA8 off-token (31), banned #F59E0B amber panel (SpacedRepetitionTimetable.tsx 1127), and the prop+getDoc-on-mount persistence (296, 325) — all accurate. ONE FLAGGED INACCURACY: the hardcoded-45 'bug' is real but mislocated — it lives at SpacedRepetitionTimetable.tsx line 395 (return totalBlocks * 45), NOT timetableAlgorithm.ts lines 395/419 as the concept states (those lines are the allocator's effective-score math; timetableAlgorithm's only literal 45 is a legitimate default parameter at line 513, and line 407 of the component already correctly derives blockDuration = profile.defaultBlockDuration ?? 45). The diagnosis is correct and fixable; only the file/line citation is wrong. Minor, not load-bearing on the verdict.

Load the persisted per-subject SM-2 state and pass it in, so (a) session COUNTS reflect ease factor / due-interval and (b) session TYPE comes from the real SM-2 branch (repetitions===0 → new-learning, etc.) instead of the seededRandom coin-flip (lines 569-578). This single rewiring makes the tool's name true. 2) Exam-proximity spacing: extend the existing computeIntensityFactor proximity signal from session-count to the review GAP — widen SM-2 intervals when the exam is far, tighten them as it nears (~10-20% of time-to-exam, the Cepeda mechanism), in the day-assignment placer's idealGap (line 638). 3) Recall-graded debrief: in StudySessionView the teach-back 'write what you remember' card already exists (TeachBackWriteCard) — make completing it the quality signal fed to qualityFromDebrief, replacing pure confidenceBefore/After (the illusory fluency cue). At minimum, when self-rated confidence is high but the recall attempt was thin, show the divergence ('felt solid, but you recalled 2 of 5 points — that gap is the fluency illusion') as a mistake-first reveal. 4) Animation-encodes-meaning: a small per-subject 'memory curve' on the block card — an SVG forgetting/strengthening line that visibly lengthens the next-review interval when a recall attempt succeeds and snaps back short when it fails, so the student SEES the spacing engine working.

(2013). Improving Students' Learning With Effective Learning Techniques. Psychological Science in the Public Interest, 14(1), 4–58.) | Retrieval practice beats restudying for long-term retention and the advantage GROWS with delay (restudy can win at 5 min; testing wins decisively at 2 days–1 week). Psychological Science, 17(3), 249–255; Karpicke, J. D., & Roediger, H. L. (2008). The Critical Importance of Retrieval for Learning. Science, 319, 966–968.) | The ideal inter-study gap scales with how far away the test is — roughly 10–20% of the retention interval. A fixed weekly cadence is a crude approximation; review gaps should widen when the exam is far and tighten as it nears. (Cepeda, N. J., Pashler, H., Vul, E., Wixted, J. T., & Rohrer, D. (2006). Distributed Practice in Verbal Recall Tasks: A Review and Quantitative Synthesis. Psychological Bulletin, 132(3), 354–380.) | The metacognitive illusion: easy-feeling strategies (massing, rereading) produce fluency that masquerades as learning — 64 of 72 participants who saw spacing outperform massing still believed massing worked better; self-rated confidence is uncorrelated with actual performance. Correct judgement with objective recall feedback, not self-ratings. (Kornell, N., & Bjork, R. A. (2008). Learning Concepts and Categories: Is Spacing the 'Enemy of Induction'? Psychological Science, 19(6), 585–592; Karpicke & Roediger (2008), Science, 319, 966–968.)
  - _App-in-its-own-right:_ This is a true spaced-repetition study engine — the SuperMemo/Anki idea applied at subject-and-topic level for the Leaving Cert, but adaptive (SM-2 already implemented) and recall-graded rather than self-report. It stands alone as 'the planner that knows when you're about to forget each subject and tests you right before you do' — a genuinely differentiated memory-science product, not a calendar.
  - _DEIS framing:_ Inclusive because it removes the single most demoralising thing for a struggling student — studying hard, feeling confident, then bombing — by exposing the fluency illusion gently and early. The desirable-difficulty copy ('this feels harder, that's the point') pre-empts the 'I'm just bad at this' attribution. It drives everything off technique-utility and the student's own recall, never off a 'what kind of learner are you' style quiz (which has no evidence), so no student is mislabelled. Mastery-framed throughout: progress is 'you now hold this longer', not a grade or a rank.
  - _Evidence check:_ All four sources are real and on-point. Verified via WebSearch: Dunlosky et al. 2013, Psychological Science in the Public Interest 14(1):4-58 — exists, correctly summarized (practice testing + distributed practice = high utility; rereading/highlighting/summarising = low utility). Cepeda et al. 2006, Psychological Bulletin 132(3):354-380 — exists (839 assessments, 317 experiments), and the cited mechanism (optimal inter-study interval grows with retention interval) is accurately stated; one caveat — the precise "10-20%" ratio is the practical heuristic from the Cepeda 2008 ridgeline follow-up, not explicitly the 2006 review, so that figure is mildly mis-attributed but the direction is correct. Roediger & Karpicke 2006 (Psych Science 17:249-255) and Karpicke & Roediger 2008 (Science 319:966-968) are foundational, correctly characterized (testing advantage grows with delay; repeated testing >> repeated restudy at delay) — well-established enough not to need a confirming search. Kornell & Bjork 2008 (Psych Science 19:585-592) fluency-illusion finding (participants believed massing beat spacing despite spacing winning) is accurately summarized. No shaky or invented citations. Effect directions match the literature throughout, and the code itself (qualityFromDebrief grading off self-rated confidence) confirms the fluency-illusion problem the concept claims to fix is real and present in the current build.

  - Rebuild the points/streak layer so it supports a personally-held 'why' and never punishes a struggling student, replacing scattered hardcoded point values and a misleading longest-streak with an evidence-calibrated, anchor-led motivation system.
  - _Content:_ Author a 'why anchor' bank that ties the streak to a personally-held reason, reusing the anchor concept already proven in ComebackEngine.tsx (the 'anchor' field and ANCHOR_OPTIONS / ANCHOR_OPTIONS_JC). Write ~30 short, autonomy-framed anchor prompts spanning the DEIS-relevant range — including non-academic and 'options' anchors ('keep my options open', 'prove I can', a trade/apprenticeship, 'be the first in my family to have the choice') so a not-yet-ambitious student has a true entry point. Author a 'streak repair' content set: when a streak breaks, a self-compassion reframe ('streaks break — what matters is the next session, not the last one') instead of a loss-aversion guilt screen, drawn from the same supportive register as Catch-Up Lane. Write a short, honest 'what points are for' explainer (points are a nudge, not the point) and a single source-of-truth list of point values with their rationale.
  - _Functionality:_ 1) Centralise the points economy: the '+5 Already Studied' and '20-point Skip Session' values are hardcoded inline in JSX (SpacedRepetitionTimetable.tsx lines 1234, 1242) and QUICK_DEBRIEF_POINTS lives separately — consolidate into one POINTS config so values are consistent and tunable. 2) Fix the misleading longest-streak: computeStreak returns longestStreak = currentStreak (timetableAlgorithm.ts line 795) — actually track longest across history so the metric the student sees is true. 3) Anchor-led streak surface: surface the student's chosen 'why' anchor beside the streak count (pulling from the ComebackEngine anchor already in Firestore), so the durable motivator (personal meaning) is visible, not just the points. 4) De-punish the break: replace any loss-aversion broken-streak treatment with the self-compassion repair card and a one-tap 'restart today' that reframes rather than scolds; keep streaks as a light nudge but cap their visual prominence so they never dominate the screen. 5) Explain the JC priority labels: the High/Medium/Low badges show for junior students but the explainer panel is senior-only (gated on !isJunior, lines 942/964) — add a band-based explanation so JC users understand why a subject is High priority.
  - _Science:_ Gamification yields only small effects, and the BEHAVIOURAL effects are the least stable; durable gains come from game-fiction and meaningful social interaction, not raw points/leaderboards. Treat streaks as a supporting nudge, not the mechanism, and avoid letting a broken streak become a punitive de-motivator. (Sailer, M., & Homner, L. (2020). The Gamification of Learning: A Meta-Analysis. Educational Psychology Review, 32, 1–36.) | Concrete, autonomy-framed, mastery-framed, personally-held goals translate to action better than abstract or socially-pressured ones; intentions account for under ~30% of behavioural variance, so a personal 'why' anchor is a stronger lever than external pressure. (Sheeran, P., & Webb, T. L. (2016). The Intention–Behavior Gap. Social and Personality Psychology Compass, 10(9), 503–518.) | Growth-mindset framing has a very weak average effect on achievement (d≈0.08; ~1% of variance) and is meaningful mainly for low-SES / at-risk students — so lean behaviour-change weight on stronger levers and reserve light mindset/anchor framing for the DEIS cohort, evidenced rather than assumed. (Sisk, V. F., Burgoyne, A. P., Sun, J., Butler, J. L., & Macnamara, B. N. (2018). To What Extent and Under Which Circumstances Are Growth Mind-Sets Important to Academic Achievement? Two Meta-Analyses. Psychological Science, 29(4), 549–571.)
  - _App-in-its-own-right:_ This is the motivation-and-momentum layer that could ship as its own 'study habit tracker' — a streak-and-points companion built on the evidence that streaks are a light nudge and a personal 'why' is the real engine. It stands alone as the honest counter-example to addictive streak apps: it tracks momentum without weaponising guilt, which is a defensible standalone wellbeing-aligned product for a school context.
  - _DEIS framing:_ This is the most DEIS-protective concept: the evidence explicitly warns a broken streak can backfire for at-risk students via loss aversion, so the redesign removes the punishment and adds a self-compassion repair path. The anchor bank deliberately includes 'just want options' and trade/apprenticeship and 'first in my family' anchors so a not-yet-ambitious student has an authentic, non-exclusionary reason to engage. Mindset framing is kept light and reserved for this cohort exactly as the evidence supports — not over-claimed as the engine. Fixing the JC priority explainer ensures junior (often younger DEIS) users aren't shown badges they can't interpret.
  - _Evidence check:_ All three sources are real, correctly cited, and on-point — verified via web search. (1) Sailer & Homner (2020), Educational Psychology Review 32 — abstract confirms small effects (cognitive g=.49, motivational g=.36, behavioral g=.25), behavioral/motivational effects 'less stable' under high-rigor subsplit, and game fiction + social interaction as significant moderators of behavioral outcomes. The concept's claim ('behavioral effects least stable; durable gains from game-fiction/social interaction; treat streaks as supporting nudge') matches exactly. Note: the page range is 77-112, not '1-36' as the concept lists — a minor citation slip, not a substantive error. (2) Sisk et al. (2018), Psychological Science 29(4):549-571 — confirmed d=0.08, 95% CI [0.02,0.14]; low-SES/at-risk students benefit more. Claim and effect direction accurate; the '~1% of variance' gloss is a fair restatement of d=0.08. (3) Sheeran & Webb (2016), Social & Personality Psychology Compass 10(9):503-518 — real, correct volume/issue/pages. The 'under ~30% of behavioral variance' figure is a genuine finding of that paper. Mild caveat: the paper's STRONGEST evidenced lever is implementation intentions / if-then planning, not 'personal-why anchors' per se, so 'a personal why anchor is a stronger lever than external pressure' is a reasonable extrapolation rather than a direct finding — but the concept does not overclaim it as an implementation intention, so it stays on the right side of the buzzword-laundering line. No inverted effect directions, no fabricated sources, no mechanism applied where it does not transfer.

(Gollwitzer, P. M., & Sheeran, P. (2006). Implementation Intentions and Goal Achievement: A Meta-Analysis. Advances in Experimental Social Psychology, 38, 69–119.) | A timetable is only a stated intention, and intentions explain under ~30% of behaviour variance — without a cue-binding/volitional layer a plan predictably under-delivers; concrete autonomy/mastery-framed personal goals translate to action better than abstract ones. (Sheeran, P., & Webb, T. L. (2016). The Intention–Behavior Gap. Social and Personality Psychology Compass, 10(9), 503–518.) | The optimal spacing gap scales with time-to-test (~10–20% of the retention interval), so a fixed weekly cadence is crude; review gaps should widen when the exam is far and tighten as it nears. (Cepeda, N. J., Pashler, H., Vul, E., Wixted, J. T., & Rohrer, D. (2006). Distributed Practice in Verbal Recall Tasks. Psychological Bulletin, 132(3), 354–380.) | Students underestimate task completion time by ~64% (predicted 33.9 days, took 55.5), and even '99%-certain' dates were missed by 70% — the remedy is outside-view estimation from one's own past completions, not best-case planning. (Buehler, R., Griffin, D., & Ross, M. (1994). Exploring the 'Planning Fallacy'. Journal of Personality and Social Psychology, 67(3), 366–381.)
  - _App-in-its-own-right:_ This is the core of a standalone 'evidence-based study planner' product: most timetable apps schedule subjects; almost none bind blocks to cues, optimise spacing against an exam date, or recalibrate to real completion. With persistence + calendar export it works as a daily companion independent of the rest of the platform.

The outside-view recalibration is explicitly non-punitive: 'you usually finish ~6 of 9 blocks — here's a plan you'll actually complete', which protects a not-yet-ambitious DEIS student from the demoralising over-plan-then-fail spiral. (2) Cepeda, Pashler, Vul, Wixted & Rohrer 2006, Psychological Bulletin, 132, 354–380 — confirmed; establishes optimal ISI increases with retention interval. The specific "10–20%" ratio is from the 2008 Cepeda Psych Science follow-up, not this 2006 paper — mild mis-location of the exact figure, but the directional claim is correct. (3) Buehler, Griffin & Ross 1994, JPSP, 67(3), 366–381 — confirmed; predicted 33.9 / actual 55.5 days exact, and the majority missed even their 99%-certain dates (45% finished by 99% level). (4) Sheeran & Webb 2016, Social and Personality Psychology Compass, 10(9), 503–518 — confirmed; correctly characterizes the intention-behavior gap. No fabricated or shaky citations. The science is correctly applied with active ingredients instantiated, not decorative — this is the opposite of buzzword laundering. The only evidentiary nitpick is the 10–20% figure attribution.

- **Cold-Recall Debrief — replace the confidence slider with a 60-second retrieval probe so the plan learns what you actually know** [M] — _verdict: build, score 4.31_
  - The post-session debrief and SM-2 quality score stop trusting self-rated confidence (the exact fluency illusion the research warns about) and instead measure a short cold-recall attempt, then show students where their confidence diverged from their recall so they learn to distrust easy-feeling study.
  - _Content:_ qualityFromDebrief() in timetableAlgorithm.ts currently derives the SM-2 quality (0–5) purely from confidenceBefore/confidenceAfter — confidence is precisely the misleading cue. New content: a bank of lightweight, subject-and-topic-keyed RETRIEVAL prompts (e.g. for the topic the block covered: 'Close everything. In 60 seconds, write the three stages of X / state the rule for Y / list what a top answer must include'), authored against the syllabus topic lists already in syllabusData.ts and, where the prompt asserts an exam expectation, grounded in the examiner-reports library (maths/business present; others flagged). Mistake-first framing: the prompt is asked BEFORE any review of notes, so the student first sees what they couldn't retrieve. A short 'best-strategy-per-subject' insight is re-authored to read off recall success, not confidence gain.
  - _Functionality:_ 1) Insert a 60-second cold-recall step into StudyDebrief.tsx before the confidence questions: a timed free-text/checklist retrieval attempt scored by a lenient phrase-match (the platform already has a substring-tolerant matcher pattern in the knowledge tools) — no harsh marking, just 'recalled / partial / blank'. 2) Re-point qualityFromDebrief to the recall outcome (recalled=5/4, partial=3, blank=1/2), feeding a TRUTHFUL SM-2 ease factor so the planner schedules the next review on real retention, not vibes. 3) Surface the divergence: a small fluency-gap card on the Trajectory/Briefing panel — 'You felt 4/5 confident here, but recalled 1 of 3 cold. That gap is normal — and it's why we're spacing this again sooner.' 4) Tie War Room's per-subject status to this recall signal rather than confidence, fixing the 'computeStreak/confidence as the only measure' shallowness.
  - _Science:_ Retrieval practice beats restudy for long-term retention and the advantage GROWS with delay (restudy can win at 5 min, testing wins decisively at 2 days–1 week); repeated testing yields a large delayed benefit, repeated restudy yields none. (Roediger, H. L., & Karpicke, J. D. (2006). Test-Enhanced Learning. Psychological Science, 17(3), 249–255; Karpicke, J. D., & Roediger, H. L. (2008). The Critical Importance of Retrieval for Learning. Science, 319, 966–968.) | Students systematically misjudge which study works because easy-feeling strategies create fluency that masquerades as learning — 64 of 72 participants who actually learned better from spacing still believed massing had worked better; predicted performance was uncorrelated with actual performance. (Kornell, N., & Bjork, R. A. (2008). Learning Concepts and Categories: Is Spacing the 'Enemy of Induction'? Psychological Science, 19(6), 585–592; Karpicke & Roediger (2008), Science, 319, 966–968.) | Only practice testing (retrieval) and distributed practice earned a 'high utility' rating across a systematic review; re-reading, highlighting and summarising — the strategies students use most — were rated LOW utility. (Dunlosky, J., Rawson, K. A., Marsh, E. J., Nathan, M. J., & Willingham, D. T. (2013). Improving Students' Learning With Effective Learning Techniques. Psychological Science in the Public Interest, 14(1), 4–58.) | Self-regulated learning is a cycle — forethought → performance/monitoring → self-reflection — where reflection feeds the next plan; a tool that captures reflection but doesn't route it into re-planning is missing the loop. (Zimmerman, B. J. (2002). Becoming a Self-Regulated Learner: An Overview. Theory Into Practice, 41(2), 64–70.)
  - _App-in-its-own-right:_ A 'recall-honest study tracker' is a credible standalone tool — it is the metacognitive correction layer almost no consumer study app has, and could front a spaced-repetition study journal on its own, even decoupled from War Room's allocation engine.
  - _DEIS framing:_ Critical for the not-yet-ambitious cohort: the divergence card is explicitly normalising ('this gap is normal, it happens to everyone, it's exactly why spacing works'), turning a 'you failed the recall' moment into 'now we know what to revisit' — incremental progress, not judgement. The probe is short and low-stakes so it lowers, not raises, the barrier to debriefing at all. No leaderboard, no comparison to peers; the only comparison is the student's own felt-vs-recalled gap.
  - _Evidence check:_ All four sources verified via web search as real, primary, and accurately characterized — no shaky citations. (1) Karpicke & Roediger (2008), Science 319:966-968 — confirmed; the cited finding (repeated restudy after learning = no delayed effect, repeated testing = large effect, students unaware) is the paper's actual result. (2) Roediger & Karpicke (2006), Psychological Science 17(3):249-255 — confirmed, including the specific and non-obvious detail that restudy WINS at a 5-minute delay while testing wins at 2 days–1 week; correctly stated. (3) Kornell & Bjork (2008), Psychological Science 19(6):585-592 — confirmed, and the precise '64 of 72 participants thought massing was more effective' statistic is accurate (it is 64 of the 72 who did not rate them 'about the same'). (4) Dunlosky et al. (2013), PSPI 14(1):4-58 — confirmed; practice testing + distributed practice rated high utility, rereading/highlighting/summarisation low utility, exactly as cited. Zimmerman (2002), Theory Into Practice 41(2):64-70 (SRL three-phase cycle) is a well-known real paper consistent with the claim. The effect direction matches the literature throughout, and each pedagogical claim traces to its source. The strongest verification point: the concept's premise about the existing code is also true — I confirmed qualityFromDebrief and computeStrategyHints run on confidence, so the 'fluency illusion is currently baked into the engine' claim is not rhetorical, it is factual.

### `comeback`
Current — content depth **4/5**, functionality depth **4/5**. The Comeback Engine (components/ComebackEngine.tsx, 1380 lines, single component file + helper functions inline; no dedicated data file — it draws from components/subjectData.ts for the points/grade/band model) is a 5-phase motivational recovery planner for students who are behind. Phases: (1) Anchor — pick a real-world goal (7 senior anchor types: Future Finder pick, specific course, PLC, apprenticeship, 'just want options', prove-someone-wrong, custom; 6 JC anchor types for junior-cycle users); (2) Gap — shows current projected CAO points (best-6 calc) vs the target's points and the gap to close, with motivational copy tiered by gap size; (3) Quickest Wins — ranks subjects by 'biggest grade-jump return per unit effort', computing the actual CAO points gained from a one-grade-up move per subject, sorted by gain x effort-weight x mock-trend-weight; (4) Plan — generates 3-7 concrete weekly missions (study a past paper in the top-win subject, focus on a specific 'shaky' topic pulled from War Room mastery data, a what-if mission from the CAO Simulator, plus a mindset mission); (5) Progress — dashboard with projected/gap/potential stat cards, momentum delta since start, timetable-completion nudge, anchor + North Star reminder, quick-wins recap, and tickable missions with auto-complete detection. State persists to Firestore (progress/{uid}.comebackEngine). It is genuinely cross-wired: it reads live data from 5 other tools via InnovationDataContext + Firestore — Future Finder picks, CAO Simulator what-if scenarios, North Star statement, War Room topic-mastery (to make missions topic-specific), and Spaced Rep timetable completions (to auto-tick missions and compute a weekly completion rate). Full Junior Cycle parallel track swaps CAO-points framing for descriptor-band-walk framing throughout.

Card borders also use 0.5px rgba(0,0,0,0.07) hairlines rather than the documented 2px #1a1a1a card system.; No data file of its own: unlike sibling Innovation tools (careerPathsData.ts etc.), all 'content' is inline template strings; there is no curated/researched content corpus behind the missions.
  - _Gaps:_ No automated test coverage: grep for comeback/calculateQuickWins/getOneGradeUp across *.test.* and *.spec.* returns zero hits. The core points-gain ranking and JC band-walk math are untested despite being the tool's value proposition.; Mission generation is template-string-based, not authored or examiner-grounded. Missions don't tell the student WHAT to practise beyond a topic name pulled from War Room.; Quick-wins logic only ever models a ONE-grade-up move per subject (getOneGradeUp); _getTwoGradesUp (line 126) is defined but unused (underscore-prefixed dead code), so 'what's realistically possible' caps at +1 grade across the board and can't model a bigger comeback.; PLC / apprenticeship / 'just want options' targets are hardcoded motivational round numbers (POINTS_THRESHOLDS: plc-general 200, apprenticeship 200, options-decent 300, options-good 350) with an in-code comment admitting 'not exact'. It assumes timetable block IDs literally contain the subject name; multi-word or abbreviated subjects could silently never auto-complete.; weeklyCompletionRate hardcodes 'assume ~2 blocks expected per day' (line 668) rather than reading the student's actual timetable, so the percentage is a rough heuristic, not a true completion rate.

  - The Comeback Engine becomes a behaviour-change engine: every weekly mission is bound to a personal if-then cue, the plan is recalibrated to the student's real completion history, and a mistake-first animation shows them WHY a goal-only timetable fails before it works.
  - _Content:_ Add a real content corpus the tool currently lacks (it has no data file of its own — everything is inline template strings). Create comebackMissionData.ts: (1) a curated bank of cue templates grounded in implementation-intentions research, written autonomy-first for DEIS students — 'When I get home and put my bag down, then I do my 30-min [subject] block at the kitchen table' / 'After dinner on Tuesday, then one HL [subject] past-paper question' — students pick or write their own cue, never a scolding 'you should'; (2) an obstacle-anticipation bank ('what usually stops you?' → tiny if-then coping plan, lifted from the existing OBSTACLES bank in comebackData.ts that the sibling Catch-Up Lane already uses but the Comeback Engine doesn't); (3) replace the two hardcoded, uncited '42% more likely' strings (lines 343, 421) with a sourced, honestly-worded line tied to the actual mechanism ('Writing exactly WHEN and WHERE you'll study — not just that you will — roughly doubles follow-through'). Each mission row gains a 'when / where' cue field and an optional 'if [obstacle] happens, then [backup]' line, so the plan is a set of concrete situational triggers, not a list of intentions.
  - _Functionality:_ Promote the if-then plan from the comeback-only Catch-Up Lane flow (components/CatchUpLane/Comeback.tsx already builds ifThen { trigger, action }) into the mainstream Comeback Engine missions: each WeeklyMission gains optional cue + backup fields, persisted to progress/{uid}.comebackEngine alongside the existing shape. Add an OUTSIDE-VIEW recalibration loop using data the tool already reads: replace the hardcoded 'assume ~2 blocks expected per day' (line 668) and the fragile substring auto-complete (line 687) with the student's real timetable from SpacedRepetitionTimetable, computing their true historic completion rate (e.g. 'you usually finish ~6 of 9 blocks'), then size next week's missions to a plan they'll actually finish rather than an optimistic one. Add a mistake-first opening interactive: an animated split-screen where the student first watches a 'goal-only' avatar (a stated intention with no cue) drift and miss blocks across a week, then the same week WITH a cue attached visibly converting plan→done — animation encodes the intention-behaviour gap, not decoration. The progress dashboard's momentum delta stays, but completion is now driven by the honest real rate.
  - _Science:_ Implementation intentions ('when situation X, then I will do Y') have a medium-to-large effect (d ≈ 0.65 across 94 studies, >8,000 participants) on actually enacting goals — far more than forming a goal intention alone — by pre-specifying when/where/how and delegating action to the situational cue. (Gollwitzer, P. M., & Sheeran, P. (2006). Implementation Intentions and Goal Achievement: A Meta-Analysis of Effects and Processes. Advances in Experimental Social Psychology, 38, 69–119.) | Intentions explain under ~30% of variance in actual behaviour and medium-to-large changes in intention produce only small-to-medium behaviour change — so a timetable, which IS a stated intention, predictably under-delivers without a cue-binding volitional layer; concrete, autonomy- and mastery-framed personal goals translate to action better than abstract or socially-pressured ones. (Sheeran, P., & Webb, T. L. (2016). The Intention–Behavior Gap. Social and Personality Psychology Compass, 10(9), 503–518.) | Students underestimate task-completion time (the planning fallacy): in the classic study they predicted 33.9 days and took 55.5 (≈64% longer), and only 30% finished by their '99%-certain' date — the remedy is reference-class / outside-view estimation from the student's own past completions. (Buehler, R., Griffin, D., & Ross, M. (1994). Exploring the 'Planning Fallacy': Why People Underestimate Their Task Completion Times. Journal of Personality and Social Psychology, 67(3), 366–381.)
  - _App-in-its-own-right:_ This is the strongest standalone candidate: a 'Follow-Through Planner' is a complete behaviour-change app in its own right — pick a why, turn each intention into a cued action, anticipate obstacles, and watch a self-correcting plan that learns your real completion rate. It is useful to ANY student (and adult) with a goal they keep failing to act on, independent of CAO points, which also makes the JC track and the 'just want options' / PLC / apprenticeship anchors first-class rather than fudged with fabricated points numbers.
  - _DEIS framing:_ This is the most DEIS-aligned concept because the science says concrete, autonomy-framed, personally-held cues outperform socially-pressured ones, which converges with the project's DEIS guidance. The cue templates are written around the student's real life ('when I get home', 'after dinner'), the obstacle bank normalises that things get in the way without shame (no 'days missed' counters, mirroring Catch-Up Lane's shame-designed-out ethos), and the outside-view recalibration is framed honestly and kindly ('here's a plan you'll actually finish') rather than as failure. It serves the not-yet-ambitious student whose problem is acting on a small goal, not chasing 600 points.
  - _Evidence check:_ All three citations verified real, correctly attributed, and on-point. Gollwitzer & Sheeran (2006), Adv. Exp. Soc. Psych. 38, 69-119 — d=.65 across 94 independent tests CONFIRMED verbatim. Buehler, Griffin & Ross (1994), JPSP 67(3), 366-381 — predicted 33.9 days vs actual 55.5 (~64% longer) and only 30% done by "99%-certain" date CONFIRMED exact. Sheeran & Webb (2016), Soc. & Pers. Psych. Compass 10(9), 503-518 — real Wiley paper; "medium-to-large change in intention → small-to-medium change in behaviour" is verbatim; the "~30% of variance" framing is consistent with the established literature though I could not pull that exact number from the abstract-level results (low-risk, widely cited figure). Codebase claims also verified against source: lines 343/421 ("42%" strings), 668 ("~2 blocks"), 687 (substring auto-complete), ~18 #FAF7F4 surfaces, getDoc-on-mount at 510, and the existing OBSTACLES/ifThen primitive all confirmed present as described. No shaky or inverted citations. For the top-win subject's shaky topic (already pulled live from War Room topicMastery, lines 308–335), prescribe a MIXED topic set ('alternate questions from [shaky topic] and [last week's topic]') rather than 'do all of topic X', and pre-empt the reaction with copy: 'this will feel harder and slower — that harder feeling is the point, it's what makes it stick.' Down-rank the low-utility passive strategies explicitly in copy (rereading/highlighting/summarising named as low-utility), so students learn the hierarchy, not just a task list. Where a subject has examiner-report material in /examiner-reports/, the retrieval mission can name a specific recurring error to test against (honouring the project's examiner-grounding discipline) instead of a generic 'past paper question'.
  - _Functionality:_ Type each generated WeeklyMission with a techniqueType and surface a small 'why' disclosure on each mission card. Wire spacing to the exam date: the tool already computes daysUntilExam (line 469) and the SpacedRepetitionTimetable already runs SM-2 — extend the review-GAP signal so 'spaced-review' missions widen when the exam is far and tighten as it nears (≈10–20% of time-to-test), the explicit Cepeda mechanism, rather than a fixed weekly cadence. Add a mistake-first 'fluency trap' micro-interactive: present a topic the student rated 'confident', then prompt a cold-recall attempt ('close everything — write the 3 key steps'), then reveal the gap between felt confidence and actual recall — the animation visualises the mark-loss of trusting fluency before showing the retrieval fix. Feed that recall outcome (not self-rated confidence) into the next mission's priority, fixing the StudyDebrief signal that the science specifically warns is illusory.
  - _Science:_ Of ten common study techniques, only practice testing (retrieval) and distributed (spaced) practice earned a 'high utility' rating; rereading, highlighting and summarising — the strategies students use most — were rated LOW utility. A plan should therefore default to retrieval + spacing, not generic 'study X'. (Dunlosky, J., Rawson, K. A., Marsh, E. J., Nathan, M. J., & Willingham, D. T. (2013). Improving Students' Learning With Effective Learning Techniques. Psychological Science in the Public Interest, 14(1), 4–58.) | Retrieval practice beats restudying for long-term retention and the advantage GROWS with delay (restudy can win at 5 minutes, but testing wins decisively at 2 days–1 week); repeated testing produces a large delayed-recall benefit while repeated restudy after initial learning produces none. (Roediger, H. L., & Karpicke, J. D. (2006). Test-Enhanced Learning. Psychological Science, 17(3), 249–255; Karpicke, J. D., & Roediger, H. L. (2008). The Critical Importance of Retrieval for Learning. Science, 319, 966–968.) | The ideal inter-study (spacing) gap scales with time-to-test — roughly 10–20% of the retention interval — so review gaps should widen when the exam is far and tighten as it nears, rather than a fixed weekly cadence (meta-analysis of 317 experiments, 839 comparisons). (Cepeda, N. J., Pashler, H., Vul, E., Wixted, J. T., & Rohrer, D. (2006). Distributed Practice in Verbal Recall Tasks. Psychological Bulletin, 132(3), 354–380.) | Interleaving (mixing problem types) outperforms blocking on delayed tests but feels harder, so students avoid it; in a randomised trial of 126 seventh-graders the higher interleaving dose scored higher after 1-day and 1-month delays, and a 30-fold increase in delay cut scores by under a tenth. (Rohrer, D., Dedrick, R. F., & Stershic, S. (2015). Interleaved Practice Improves Mathematics Learning. Journal of Educational Psychology, 107(3), 900–908.)
  - _App-in-its-own-right:_ Stands alone as a 'How To Actually Study' action engine: it turns any subject + topic + exam date into a sequenced, evidence-typed set of study actions with the reasoning attached — essentially a personal study-technique coach. It is independent of the comeback framing and would be valuable to any student who 'studies for hours' but uses low-utility passive methods, which is a near-universal problem.
  - _DEIS framing:_ Inclusive because it lowers effort, not raises the bar: it tells a struggling student that the smarter move is often LESS rereading and a short cold-recall, reframing 'I studied for hours and it didn't work' as a fixable technique problem rather than a 'you're not academic' verdict. The desirable-difficulty copy ('the harder feeling is the point') protects the not-yet-confident student from concluding the difficulty means they're failing. No high-achievement or points-pressure language is required for the technique value to land, so it works for PLC/apprenticeship/JC anchors equally.

- **The Comeback Loop — plan → do → reflect → re-plan, made visible (with honest, non-punitive momentum)** [L] — _verdict: build, score 4.02_
  - Close the self-regulation cycle in software so the student experiences one designed loop — last week's reflection visibly reshapes next week's missions — with streaks as a light, loss-aversion-safe nudge instead of the engine.
  - _Content:_ Author a small reflection-and-reattribution content layer the tool doesn't have. (1) A weekly 'what the data noticed' summary written in the student's-own-progress voice, attributing wins to controllable actions ('the weeks you finished most blocks, you'd attached a when/where to them' / 'your recall jumped most in the subject you interleaved'), drawing on the existing history snapshots (ProgressSnapshot[]) and StudyDebrief entries — never on self-rated confidence alone. (2) A 'reset is normal' copy bank for broken streaks that re-frames a lapse as data, not failure (no shaming counters), explicitly guarding the at-risk cohort against a broken streak becoming a de-motivator. (3) Wire the existing _getTwoGradesUp dead code (line 126) into an honest 'further reach' view so a bigger comeback can be modelled when the data supports it, instead of capping every projection at +1 grade across the board.
  - _Functionality:_ Make Zimmerman's three phases one explicit, visible loop in the progress dashboard: Plan (this week's cued missions) → Do (timetable completions auto-feeding in, via the real completion rate from concept 1's fix) → Reflect (a lightweight debrief that asks 'what got in the way / what helped', not just a confidence slider) → Re-plan (next week's missions visibly regenerated FROM that reflection — e.g. an obstacle named last week becomes an if-then backup this week; a subject where recall lagged gets a retrieval mission). The existing 'generate next week's missions' regeneration becomes the loop's closing action, annotated with what changed and why. Keep streaks/points (computeStreak) but demote them to a light supporting nudge paired with the personal 'why' anchor, and ensure a missed week never zeroes-out in a punitive way — it surfaces a gentle re-entry, reusing the Catch-Up Lane re-entry tone.
  - _Science:_ Self-regulated learning is a cycle — forethought (planning/goal-setting) → performance (self-control + self-monitoring) → self-reflection (self-evaluation, causal attribution) — and reflections explicitly feed back into the next forethought phase; a tool that only generates a plan and skips monitoring + reflection is missing two-thirds of the loop, which is why plans drift. (Zimmerman, B. J. (2002). Becoming a Self-Regulated Learner: An Overview. Theory Into Practice, 41(2), 64–70.) | Students misjudge which strategies work because easy-feeling massing/rereading produce fluency that masquerades as learning: 64 of 72 participants who saw spacing outperform massing still believed massing worked better, and students' performance predictions were uncorrelated with actual performance — so reflection must be anchored to objective recall, not self-rated confidence. (Kornell, N., & Bjork, R. A. (2008). Learning Concepts and Categories: Is Spacing the 'Enemy of Induction'? Psychological Science, 19(6), 585–592; Karpicke & Roediger (2008), Science, 319, 966–968.) | Gamification produces only small effects, and BEHAVIOURAL/motivational effects are the LEAST stable under rigorous analysis; durable gains come from game-fiction (a personal narrative/why) and meaningful social interaction, not raw points/streaks — so treat streaks as a supporting nudge, not the mechanism (g=0.25 behavioural, g=0.36 motivational, both least stable). (Sailer, M., & Homner, L. (2020). The Gamification of Learning: A Meta-Analysis. Educational Psychology Review, 32, 1–36.)
  - _App-in-its-own-right:_ Stands alone as a metacognitive 'study OS' — a weekly plan-do-reflect-replan loop that any learner can run against any goal, showing them their own self-regulation cycle as a visible, improving system rather than a one-shot timetable. The made-visible loop is the differentiating product idea that no static planner offers.
  - _DEIS framing:_ Directly DEIS-protective: the science flags that loss-aversion streaks can backfire for at-risk students, so this concept deliberately defuses the punitive streak and replaces 'you broke your streak' with 'reset is normal — here's your way back in', mirroring the platform's shame-designed-out Catch-Up Lane. Reflection is framed as controllable, kind attribution ('the weeks it worked, you did X') that builds belonging and incremental-progress confidence rather than judging the student against a high bar. It keeps motivation on the personal 'why' anchor the tool already has, which the evidence says is the durable lever, not points.
  - _Evidence check:_ All three citations are REAL and accurately quoted (web-verified). Zimmerman (2002), Theory Into Practice 41(2):64-70 — confirmed; three cyclical phases (forethought→performance→self-reflection, feeding back) exactly as claimed. Kornell & Bjork (2008), Psychological Science 19(6):585-592 — confirmed; the '64 of 72 believed massing was more effective despite spacing winning' detail is verbatim-accurate, and the fluency-illusion claim is sound. Sailer & Homner (2020), Educational Psychology Review 32 — confirmed; g=.49 cognitive / g=.36 motivational / g=.25 behavioural, with motivational+behavioural effects LEAST stable under methodological rigor, and the concept applies this in the correct direction (demote streaks, lean on narrative/why). Codebase claims also verified: ComebackEngine.tsx exists; _getTwoGradesUp is genuine uncalled dead code at line 126; the +1-grade projection cap is real (computeMaxRealisticPoints); ProgressSnapshot/computeStreak/DebriefEntry all exist; getDoc-on-mount persistence is already in place (no staleness trap). One mismatch flagged: the Kornell-Bjork 'objective recall' anchor is not yet supported by stored data (ProgressSnapshot holds projectedPoints, not recall accuracy; DebriefEntry is confidence-slider-based) — the science is real but its active ingredient is only partially instantiated.

### `catch-up-lane`
Current — content depth **2/5**, functionality depth **4/5**. A two-arm absence-recovery tool with a calm cyan identity, reached from a hub chooser. ARM 1 ("Catch up on what you missed"): pick a subject → see a topic queue (shaky-first, then unstarted, recovered last) → each topic runs a 3-beat micro-unit: a 90-second "gist", "the one move that matters", and a self-marked "quick check" (prompt → reveal model answer → tick which criteria your answer had) → mark "Recovered" or "Still shaky — later". Recovering a topic writes confidence to the shared topicMastery context and accrues a running "≈N marks back in reach" stat from per-card marksWeight. Progress persists to progress/{uid}.catchUpLane via Firestore merge. ARM 2 ("Your Comeback"): a 6-step evidence-grounded wizard (warm open → the avoidance loop → your why → name obstacles → build a tiny first step + if-then plan + low-disclosure peer script → choose a trusted adult + safe space) that saves a personal re-entry plan, surfaced as a one-tap card on the hub. North Star "why" is pulled in if set; obstacle "so-behind" cross-links into Arm 1. Coming-soon subjects the student takes are listed honestly.

  - _Gaps:_ Content covers exactly ONE subject (Biology) with 8 topics — the file header itself calls this a 'FIRST SLICE' that 'prove[s] the format end to end'. The picker, progress bar (recovered/totalCards where totalCards=8), and 'marks back in reach' are all sized to those 8 cards. Any student not taking Biology, or taking it but past these 8 topics, sees only the 'more subjects on the way' coming-soon line.; All 8 cards are level:'common' — the RecoveryLevel type supports 'higher'|'ordinary' but no card uses them and the UI never branches on level, so there is no Higher/Ordinary differentiation despite the type modelling it.; The 'quick check' is self-marked honesty-based: the student reads the model answer, then ticks the 'needed' criteria themselves. There is no input capture, no grading, no comparison of the student's actual answer — the ticks are cosmetic (they color a box but don't gate 'Recovered' or feed any score). The 90s gist and 'one move' are static read-only prose with no interaction.; Absence-logging is fully dead: AbsenceLog type, the absences[] state field, and the logAbsence() callback exist in the hook and type but are never called or surfaced anywhere in the UI (grep confirms zero consumers). The header copy describes mapping missed classes to topics, but there is no date picker or absence-to-topic mapping implemented.; comebackData.ts exports TEACHER_SCRIPT which is never imported/used by any component (dead export).; Comeback Arm 2 content is fixed: 4 loop steps, 5 obstacles, 4 peer scripts, 5 why-options, 4 support people, 1 grounding block — no expansion mechanism and the saved plan is a single static record (latest-write-wins, no history/versioning of plans).; Arm 1 has no spaced-repetition or resurfacing-over-time: 'shaky' topics sort to the top of the same subject queue but there is no cross-session reminder, no scheduling, and no notification — once you leave the subject the shaky flag only matters if you return to that exact subject view.

(Roediger, H. L., & Karpicke, J. D. (2006). Test-Enhanced Learning: Taking Memory Tests Improves Long-Term Retention. Psychological Science, 17(3), 249–255; Karpicke, J. D., & Roediger, H. L. (2008). The Critical Importance of Retrieval for Learning. Science, 319, 966–968.) | The optimal inter-study gap scales with how far the test is (~10–20% of the retention interval), so review gaps should widen when the exam is far and tighten as it nears rather than using a fixed cadence. (Cepeda, N. J., Pashler, H., Vul, E., Wixted, J. T., & Rohrer, D. (2006). Distributed Practice in Verbal Recall Tasks: A Review and Quantitative Synthesis. Psychological Bulletin, 132(3), 354–380.) | Self-rated confidence is a fluency illusion uncorrelated with actual performance — 64 of 72 participants still believed the worse strategy had worked — so the schedule must be anchored to a recall measure, and the student should be shown their own attempt against the model to learn to distrust easy-feeling study. (Kornell, N., & Bjork, R. A. (2008). Learning Concepts and Categories: Is Spacing the 'Enemy of Induction'? Psychological Science, 19(6), 585–592; Karpicke & Roediger (2008), Science, 319, 966–968.)
  - _App-in-its-own-right:_ On its own this is a 'recover-then-retain' app for anyone who has missed school or wants to claw back a topic: a personal spaced-retrieval queue across all your subjects, sized to your exam date, that you open daily to clear what's due. That is a complete, defensible study product independent of the comeback/emotional arm — it could ship as a standalone 'Catch Up & Keep It' app.
  - _DEIS framing:_ Resurfacing is framed as 'we'll bring this back so you don't have to remember to' — autonomy and low-effort, not pressure. No streak that can break and demote a returning student (the science flags loss-aversion backfiring for the at-risk cohort). The cold-recall box explicitly says 'this isn't graded — it's just so you can see what you've actually got', removing performance threat. Incremental: a single due-topic cleared is celebrated, never a leaderboard or 'days behind' counter. Higher/Ordinary split means an OL student is met where they are, not shown HL-pitched content.
  - _Evidence check:_ All three primary sources are real and verified via web search. Roediger & Karpicke (2006), Psychological Science 17(3), 249-255 — confirmed; the specific "testing loses at 5 min but wins decisively at 2 days/1 week" pattern is the paper's actual headline finding, correctly stated. Cepeda, Pashler, Vul, Wixted & Rohrer (2006), Psychological Bulletin 132(3), 354-380 — confirmed; the meta-analysis (839 assessments / 317 experiments) and the "optimal ISI increases as retention interval increases" conclusion are accurately characterized, so the widen-when-exam-is-far / tighten-as-it-nears design is correctly grounded. Karpicke & Roediger (2008), Science 319, 966-968 — real and correctly cited for retrieval's importance. The one shaky citation: Kornell & Bjork (2008), Psychological Science 19(6), 585-592 is real and DOES show the metacognitive fluency illusion (participants rated massing as more effective even after performing better with spacing), but it is an induction/interleaving study, so using it as the load-bearing source for "self-rated confidence is uncorrelated with actual performance" on a single recall item is a slight over-reach — the JOL/metacognition literature is the cleaner anchor. The "64 of 72 participants" figure traces to that paper but is a massing-preference count, not a confidence-accuracy correlation. Net: effect directions all correct, no inverted/backfiring mechanism, no buzzword laundering; one citation stretched but not false.
