Completing a day advances the timeline and logs a private (never punitive) record so the student sees the loop close: plan → do the tiny thing → reflect → tomorrow's step adapts (if a day is skipped, the next day softens rather than scolds — the obstacle's reframe re-shows). Cross-link days into Arm 1 ('today also clear one Catch-Up topic — here's the smallest one'), making the 'so-behind' obstacle a scheduled action not just a one-off button. The reflection writes back so the timeline gently recalibrates the next step's size to what the student is actually managing.
  - _Science:_ Implementation intentions ('when situation X, then I will do Y') have a medium-to-large effect (d≈0.65 across 94 studies, >8,000 participants) on actually enacting goals — far more than forming an intention alone — by delegating action to a situational cue. (Gollwitzer, P. M., & Sheeran, P. (2006). Implementation Intentions and Goal Achievement: A Meta-Analysis of Effects and Processes. Advances in Experimental Social Psychology, 38, 69–119.) | Intentions explain under ~30% of behavioural variance — a stated plan without a cue-binding/volitional layer predictably under-delivers, and concrete, autonomy-framed, personally-held goals translate to action better than abstract or socially-pressured ones. (Sheeran, P., & Webb, T. L. (2016). The Intention–Behavior Gap. Social and Personality Psychology Compass, 10(9), 503–518.) | Self-regulated learning is a cycle (forethought → performance → self-reflection) where reflection feeds the next plan; a tool that only generates a plan and skips monitoring and reflection is missing two-thirds of the loop, which is why plans drift. (Zimmerman, B. J. (2002). Becoming a Self-Regulated Learner: An Overview. Theory Into Practice, 41(2), 64–70.)
  - _App-in-its-own-right:_ This is a standalone re-entry / school-attendance support app — a guided First Week Back companion a guidance counsellor, HSCL coordinator or School Completion keyworker could point any returning student to, independent of the schoolwork side. It is a complete EBSA-aligned product: name the loop, set the cue-bound tiny steps, walk the week with you, with the right adult surfaced on the right day.
  - _DEIS framing:_ This is the most DEIS-load-bearing concept: shame stays designed out (no 'why were you out?', no days-missed counters — preserved from the current arm), and a skipped day is met with the obstacle reframe, not a penalty. It leans the behaviour-change weight on if-then cues and the trusted-adult relationship (strong evidence) rather than mindset messaging (weak/conditional). Steps are deliberately tiny and autonomy-framed ('today's ONE thing'), meeting a not-yet-ambitious returning student exactly where they are; the DEIS-specific supports (HSCL, School Completion, EWO-as-help) are surfaced as allies, reinforcing belonging.
  - _Evidence check:_ All three citations verified as real, correctly attributed, and on-point. Gollwitzer, P. M. & Sheeran, P. (2006), Advances in Experimental Social Psychology, 38, 69-119 — confirmed: medium-to-large effect d=.65 across 94 independent tests; the if-then/situational-cue mechanism is exactly what the design instantiates. Sheeran, P. & Webb, T. L. (2016), Social and Personality Psychology Compass, 10(9), 503-518 (doi 10.1111/spc3.12265) — confirmed real; the "intentions under-predict behaviour, a stated plan without a volitional layer under-delivers" claim matches the paper's thesis. Zimmerman, B. J. (2002), Theory Into Practice, 41(2), 64-70 — confirmed; the three cyclical phases (forethought → performance → self-reflection) are quoted accurately and the "a tool that only generates a plan skips two-thirds of the loop" reading is faithful to the source. No shaky or inverted citations. Codebase claims (dead TEACHER_SCRIPT export, exact content inventory, getDoc-on-mount persistence via useFreshProgress) all independently verified.

- **Map Your Missed Week — turn real absence dates into an honest, right-sized recovery plan you'll actually finish** [L] — _verdict: maybe, score 2.84_
  - Activate the tool's dead absence-logging machinery into a front door: tell it the days and classes you missed, and it maps them to the exact topics taught then, then builds a recovery plan sized to what you've historically actually completed — not an optimistic pile.
  - _Content:_ Build the absence→topic bridge content the header already promises but never implements: a lightweight per-subject 'what's typically covered around now' mapping (teaching-sequence ordering already implicit in the syllabus/curriculum.ts subtopic order) so a logged absence for, say, Biology on a date maps to the likely topicIds in play, surfaced as 'classes you likely missed'. Extend recovery cards beyond Biology (same authored, source-cited discipline) so the mapping has somewhere to land across the student's real subjects. Author honest empty-states for subjects without content yet, preserving the tool's existing 'coming soon, subject by subject' honesty.
  - _Functionality:_ Wire up the fully-dead AbsenceLog type, absences[] state field and logAbsence() callback (grep-confirmed zero UI consumers today) into a real, optional opening step: a no-shame date+subject picker ('which days were you out? — just so we can find the right topics, not to count anything'). The log maps to a prioritised recovery queue across Arm 1. Then add the outside-view layer the science demands: the tool already stores attempts and recovered/shaky data, so compute the student's real completion rate and right-size the suggested plan ('you usually finish about 2 of 3 topics you start — here's a 4-topic week you'll actually clear', not a guilt-inducing 12-topic backlog). The 'marks back in reach' stat becomes grounded in the actually-missed topics rather than all 8 cards. This closes the planning-fallacy gap: the plan is calibrated to history, presented honestly, and the backlog is shown as 'closeable one at a time' to defuse the 'so-behind' overwhelm the comeback arm already names.
  - _Science:_ Students reliably underestimate task time (predicted 33.9 days, took 55.5 ≈64% longer; only 30% finished by their 99%-certain date), so a recovery plan that asks 'how much can you do?' will be optimistic — the remedy is reference-class estimation from the student's own past completions. (Buehler, R., Griffin, D., & Ross, M. (1994). Exploring the 'Planning Fallacy': Why People Underestimate Their Task Completion Times. Journal of Personality and Social Psychology, 67(3), 366–381.) | Self-regulated learning is a cycle where reflection/monitoring feeds the next forethought phase; using stored completion history to recalibrate the next plan is the explicit feedback loop a one-shot plan lacks. (Zimmerman, B. J. (2002). Becoming a Self-Regulated Learner: An Overview. Theory Into Practice, 41(2), 64–70.) | Of ten common study techniques only practice testing and distributed practice earned 'high utility', so the recovery queue mapped from absences should default each missed-topic to a retrieval+spacing micro-unit rather than a generic 're-read what you missed' slot. (Dunlosky, J., Rawson, K. A., Marsh, E. J., Nathan, M. J., & Willingham, D. T. (2013). Improving Students' Learning With Effective Learning Techniques. Psychological Science in the Public Interest, 14(1), 4–58.)
  - _App-in-its-own-right:_ Standalone, this is an 'absence-to-recovery' app: log the school you missed, get back exactly the topics taught while you were out, in a plan calibrated to what you genuinely finish. That honest, history-grounded right-sizing is a distinct product from a generic study planner — its whole premise is recovering from real, logged gaps without overwhelm.
  - _DEIS framing:_ Directly answers the DEIS reality that chronic absence is markedly higher in DEIS schools (the tool's own data-file rationale): the date picker is explicitly de-shamed ('just to find the right topics, not to count anything') — no totals, no judgement. The outside-view right-sizing is the inclusive move: instead of confronting a not-yet-confident student with an impossible backlog, it offers a plan scaled to what they actually complete, framed as fit and autonomy. Honest coming-soon states avoid faking content for subjects the student takes. It deliberately leans on retrieval/spacing scheduling and realistic completion (strong evidence) over motivational/mindset pressure (weak/conditional for this cohort).
  - _Evidence check:_ All three sources verified as real, correctly cited, and correctly characterized. Buehler, Griffin & Ross (1994), JPSP 67(3), 366-381 — confirmed via the JPSP PDF and multiple secondary sources; the 33.9-day predicted thesis-completion figure is accurate and the concept's read (underestimation -> reference-class/own-history remedy) matches the paper's actual thesis; effect direction correct. Dunlosky et al. (2013), PSPI 14(1), 4-58 — confirmed; "only practice testing and distributed practice earned high utility" is exactly right. Zimmerman (2002), Theory Into Practice 41(2), 64-70 — canonical SRL-cycle overview (forethought/performance/self-reflection feeding the next cycle); characterization accurate. No shaky citations. The science is sound; the problem is that the literature genuinely supports only the right-sizing sub-feature, NOT the absence->topic mapping headline, which has no data to stand on and would require fabrication.

### `exam-reps`
Current — content depth **4/5**, functionality depth **4/5**. Exam Reps is a single-card, four-beat (intro -> attempt -> mark -> done) retrieval-practice loop over real Leaving Cert questions. The student picks Subject -> Level -> Topic in SubjectPicker.tsx (subjects default to the student's onboarding subjects, with an All toggle; subjects/topics without content are marked "soon" or hidden). Per rep: they commit a confidence (Unsure/Maybe/Confident) before seeing the question (calibration); attempt it in a textarea, a steps box, or on paper depending on answerKind; tap a highlighted command word to see what it's asking; then self-mark against examiner mark "ribbons" (I had this / I didn't), with a live "marks on the table" tally and an all-or-nothing "gate" ribbon concept that zeroes the answer if missed. The done beat shows one examiner-sourced lesson with a citation, the captured/total marks, a calibration nudge comparing stated confidence vs marks landed, and an all-time "marks banked" tally persisted to Firestore (progress/{uid}.examReps via useExamReps). A soft marks-to-minutes timer arc runs during the attempt (never a red countdown). The content bank (examRepsData.ts) holds 128 agent-forged cards, every one carrying a Chief Examiner / marking-scheme citation.
  - _Thinness:_ 128 total RepCards in examRepsData.ts; 83 Mathematics + 38 English account for 121 of them, leaving 7 cards spread across the other 3 subjects (Geography 3, History 2, Business 2).; Only 5 of 41 curriculum subjects have content; SubjectPicker renders the other 36 with a static 'soon' badge (components/ExamReps/SubjectPicker.tsx line 117).; Max cards per (subject,level,topic) combo is 2; 8 combos have exactly 1 card, so 'Next question' just re-serves the same item via the modulo round-robin (index.tsx line 208-211).; Dead/unused tracking: useExamReps.ts maintains seenCardIds + leakCardIds (lines 25, 69-72) but index.tsx never reads them for selection - only state.banked is read (index.tsx line 223).; No answer key: the 'mark' beat (index.tsx lines 332-415) is pure self-report against ribbon labels; no model answer, no diff against the student's typed answer.; Calibration message is 2 hardcoded conditional branches plus a default string (index.tsx lines 419-422).; 40 of 128 cards are answerKind:'paper' (do it on paper, no input captured), so for ~31% of cards the app collects no answer text at all before self-marking.; Single string-replace command-word highlighter falls back to no-highlight if the anchor token is not found verbatim in the stem (commandResolves gate, index.tsx lines 91-94).
  - _Gaps:_ Subject coverage is extremely lopsided: of 41 curriculum subjects, only 5 have any reps (Mathematics 83, English 38, Geography 3, History 2, Business 2). 36 subjects show a permanent 'soon' chip with zero content. Even within covered subjects it is thin: Business and History have only 2 cards each and only at Higher level; Geography has 3 HL cards and no OL.; The rep loop is shallow because the picker scopes it to ONE (subject, level, topic) combo: across 68 such combos, 60 have exactly 2 cards and 8 have exactly 1 card (max cards in any combo is 2). So after 1-2 'Next question' taps the round-robin (pickNext = (cardIndex+1) % activeCards.length) immediately repeats the same question. A student who picks a topic effectively has a 1-2 question session.; The hook's spacing/interleaving infrastructure is built but unused by the UI. useExamReps tracks seenCardIds and leakCardIds with comments promising 'interleave fresh questions' and 'later resurfacing', but index.tsx reads only state.banked. seenCardIds/leakCardIds are never consulted for card selection, and selection never crosses topics, so the file-header claim 'Spacing/interleaving is handled invisibly' is aspirational, not implemented.; Self-marking is entirely self-reported with no answer key or model answer shown. The student decides 'I had this' / 'I didn't' for each ribbon against the ribbon label alone; there is no worked solution, no exemplar, and no comparison of their typed answer to a correct one. Marking accuracy depends wholly on student honesty/competence.; Confidence calibration is reduced to three coarse hardcoded thresholds (confident + <60% -> revisit; unsure + >=60% -> trust yourself; else 'Good calibration') and is not persisted or trended over time.; OL/HL imbalance in the only deep subjects is uneven (e.g. Geography/History/Business have no Ordinary-level cards at all), and only years 2019-2025 are represented, with 2019 contributing just 2 cards.

- **Mixed Paper — the interleaved rep queue** [M] — _verdict: build, score 4.21_
  - Turn the one-topic round-robin into an invisible, mixed-topic rep queue across a whole subject that resurfaces the marks you actually leaked, so a session is 10-15 genuinely varied questions instead of the same 1-2 re-served.
  - _Content:_ No new card schema is needed to start — the win is reach across what already exists. (1) Lift the scoping unit from one (subject,level,topic) combo to the whole (subject,level), so a Maths Higher session draws from all 40+ HL Maths cards across algebra, geometry, calculus, etc., not the 1-2 in a single sub-topic. (2) Tag every card with the `taskType` field that already exists on the type (only Geography uses it today) so the queue can mix question *types* and never serve two of the same type back-to-back. (3) Author a small 'why this mix' micro-line per subject ('Real papers don't tell you which method — this queue won't either') sourced to the interleaving evidence. (4) As a content side-effect this makes the thin subjects usable: 7 cards across one subject is a real 7-question round, where 2 cards in one topic was a dead end — buying time to deepen the bank without leaving the picker showing dead topics.
  - _Functionality:_ Replace `pickNext = (cardIndex+1) % activeCards.length` with a weighted interleaver that finally *reads* the `seenCardIds` and `leakCardIds` the hook already tracks (today index.tsx reads only `state.banked`). Selection rules, all invisible to the student (they still just tap one orange 'Next question'): (a) never repeat the most-recent card or `taskType`; (b) bias toward unseen cards first; (c) once the unseen pool thins, resurface a `leakCardId` (a card where they left marks on the table) at a spaced interval — realising the 'later resurfacing' the hook's own comments promise but never deliver. Add an optional 'Mixed' vs 'One topic' toggle on the picker (defaults to Mixed) so a student revising one weak area can still narrow. Persist a tiny per-card last-seen timestamp inside the existing `examReps` namespace (additive merge, no rules change). Keep the four-beat loop and single-screen design exactly as-is.
  - _Science:_ Interleaving topics (mixing question types so the student must first decide WHICH approach to use) outperforms blocked practice and is near-immune to forgetting, because it trains strategy-selection — the exact skill a mixed real paper demands. (Rohrer, Dedrick & Stershic (2015), 'Interleaved Practice Improves Mathematics Learning', Journal of Educational Psychology 107(3), 900-908.) | Spaced/resurfaced retrieval of items previously got wrong drives durable retention; a single act of retrieval outperforms repeated study, and the benefit compounds when weak items come back later rather than being drilled in a block. (Roediger & Karpicke (2006), 'Test-Enhanced Learning', Psychological Science 17(3), 249-255.)
  - _App-in-its-own-right:_ This is the difference between a demo and a study app: a student can sit down and do a genuine 15-minute mixed Higher Maths set that mirrors the unpredictability of the real paper, with their own leaked marks quietly coming back around. It becomes the daily-driver retrieval engine the whole Launchpad can point at, rather than a card you exhaust in two taps.
  - _DEIS framing:_ Inclusive by design: no rep-count gamification, no 'hours' leaderboard (the volume evidence says that overclaims). Framing is autonomy + incremental mark-gain — 'the marks you left on the table came back, and this time you took them.' A not-yet-ambitious student gets a low-stakes mixed round they can stop after one question; resurfacing weak items is framed as the app quietly having their back, never as 'you failed this before'.
  - _Evidence check:_ Both sources verified REAL via web search with exact bibliographic match. (1) Rohrer, Dedrick & Stershic (2015), 'Interleaved Practice Improves Mathematics Learning', J. Educational Psychology 107(3), 900-908 — confirmed (ERIC ED557355, author's USF PDF, publisher). On-point: the paper's own stated mechanism (interleaving trains strategy-selection because the student must choose the approach before solving) is exactly what the concept's interleaver instantiates; effect direction (interleaved > blocked) correct. (2) Roediger & Karpicke (2006), 'Test-Enhanced Learning', Psychological Science 17, 249-255 — confirmed (PubMed 16507066, SAGE doi 10.1111/j.1467-9280.2006.01693.x). On-point for the retrieval-over-restudy / delayed-benefit claim (this is THE testing-effect paper). SHAKY SUB-CLAIM: the concept also cites it for spaced resurfacing of previously-wrong items 'compounding when weak items come back later' — Roediger & Karpicke 2006 is a testing-effect study, not primarily a spacing study, so that half over-extends this specific citation. Recommend re-aiming the spacing claim to Karpicke & Roediger (2008, Science) or Bjork's desirable-difficulties. Not fabrication or inversion — a minor over-reach on one of two cites. Codebase claims independently verified: 128 cards, HL Maths 41/21 topics, Geography 3/Business 2/History 2 (the thin-subject dead-ends), only 1 card carries taskType, pickNext is naive round-robin, hook tracks seen/leak ids but component reads only banked, hook uses getDoc-on-mount (avoids staleness trap), Mixed/One-topic pill pattern exists in SubjectPicker.

- **Show Me One — the fading model-answer scaffold** [L] — _verdict: build, score 4.41_
  - Add an optional, source-grounded model answer that a struggling student can study FIRST (worked-example mode) and a confident one skips — a scaffold that automatically fades as their captured-marks signal climbs, fixing the 'cold attempt for everyone + pure self-report with no answer key' gap.
  - _Content:_ Author one new field per card: a `modelAnswer` — a short, exam-format exemplar that visibly *earns* each existing mark ribbon (the ribbons already paraphrase real scheme points, so the exemplar maps 1:1 onto them). Critically these are agent-forged from the SEC marking scheme + Chief Examiner reports already in `/examiner-reports/`, with the same citation discipline as the lessons — not invented. For the 40 `answerKind:'paper'` cards (currently capturing no input and self-marked blind) the exemplar is the highest-leverage add: it gives the student something concrete to mark their paper work against. Roll out exemplars subject-by-subject behind a per-card flag so cards without one simply behave as today.
  - _Functionality:_ Insert a 'support level' the loop chooses automatically from the capture/calibration signal the hook ALREADY tracks (`leakCardIds`, per-card captured marks): (1) Novice on a topic / repeatedly leaking marks → 'Show me one first': study the marked model answer, see each ribbon light up on it, THEN attempt a fresh card of the same `taskType`. (2) Mid → 'completion' mode: the model answer is shown with 1-2 ribbons blanked for the student to supply (faded worked example). (3) Competent (capturing high) → straight to the cold attempt, no scaffold (expertise reversal: the exemplar would only get in the way). On the existing 'mark' beat, after self-marking, a 'Compare with a full-mark answer' reveal becomes available for every card that has an exemplar — turning today's blind self-report into a checkable diff. The student can always tap to fade the scaffold themselves ('I've got it — just give me the question').
  - _Science:_ Worked examples beat unguided problem-solving for NOVICES by cutting extraneous load, but the advantage reverses for competent learners who learn more by generating — so support must FADE as competence grows rather than being one fixed mode. (Sweller & Cooper (1985), 'The use of worked examples as a substitute for problem solving in learning algebra', Cognition and Instruction 2(1), 59-89; Kalyuga, Ayres, Chandler & Sweller (2003), 'The Expertise Reversal Effect', Educational Psychologist 38(1), 23-31.) | Errorful generation followed by clear corrective feedback enhances later learning (the pretesting effect) — but the boundary condition is that the attempt must be followed by immediate, clear corrective feedback, or the error sticks; a blind self-report with no answer key risks leaving the error uncorrected. (Kornell, Hays & Bjork (2009), 'Unsuccessful Retrieval Attempts Enhance Subsequent Learning', Journal of Experimental Psychology: LMC 35(4), 989-998.)
  - _App-in-its-own-right:_ This closes the credibility gap that stops Exam Reps being a standalone study app: today self-marking depends entirely on the student already knowing the right answer. A real model answer keyed to each mark makes it a self-contained 'answer the question, then see exactly what a full-mark version looks like and where yours fell short' tool — the thing a student currently leaves the app to find. The fading logic means it serves both the DEIS student who needs the model and the strong student who'd be slowed by it.
  - _DEIS framing:_ This is the most DEIS-protective concept: a low-prior-knowledge student is no longer thrown into a cold blank box with no idea what 'good' looks like — they get the model first, which the worked-example evidence says is exactly right for novices and lowers anxiety. Feedback stays strictly task-level ('your answer had the definition but not the contrast the marks needed'), never self-level ('you're weak at this'), per the one-third-of-feedback-backfires caution. The student controls the scaffold and can dismiss it — autonomy, not remediation labelling.
  - _Evidence check:_ All three citations are real, correctly attributed, and directionally correct. Verified via web search: Sweller and Cooper 1985 (Cognition and Instruction 2(1), 59-89); Kalyuga, Ayres, Chandler and Sweller 2003 (Educational Psychologist 38(1), 23-31); Kornell, Hays and Bjork 2009 (JEP LMC 35(4), PubMed 19586265). Effect directions match the claims, and the pretesting boundary condition (errorful attempt helps only when followed by corrective feedback) is exactly what the concept invokes to argue blind self-report is insufficient. No laundering: the mechanisms live in the core loop. Codebase claims independently verified: examRepsData.ts has exactly 40 answerKind paper cards (38 English, 2 History) with no answer key today; the hook fields leakCardIds/capturedMarks/banked/seenCardIds exist in hooks/useExamReps.ts; the hook already uses useFreshProgress (getDoc-on-mount) so it avoids the staleness bug; English cards already cite SEC marking schemes so exemplar sourcing is not hand-waved.

- **Your Calibration — the tracked over/under-confidence habit** [M] — _verdict: build, score 3.71_
  - Promote the one-rep confidence line into a tracked habit panel that trends predicted-vs-actual over many reps, with copy tuned separately for the over-confident and the under-confident, plus a launch-in from Syllabus X-Ray so meta-strategy cashes out in actual reps.
  - _Content:_ Author a small library of calibration-coaching lines, split by pattern rather than the current 3 hardcoded branches: (a) persistently OVER-confident (felt sure, marks didn't land) → paired every time with the concrete item-level 'marks on the table' breakdown so the gap is visible at the item, not just as a number; (b) persistently UNDER-confident (said unsure, captured plenty) → the 'trust that more' encouragement, aimed at the strong student the evidence warns gets pushed into underconfidence; (c) well-calibrated → quiet acknowledgement. Each line is generic-banned-phrase-free and framed around the next move, not ability. Add short 'why this matters' microcopy citing that calibration is a long game, not a verdict on one rep.
  - _Functionality:_ Persist the (confidence, captured/total) pair per rep in the existing `examReps` namespace and compute a rolling calibration trend — are you typically over, under, or on? Surface it as a small, optional 'Your calibration' card on the done-beat and as a one-screen summary, showing the trend over the last N reps (not a single verdict). Tune the calibration message off the *trend*, not just the current rep. Crucially, make Syllabus X-Ray's topic priorities hand off INTO this loop: a 'Start a rep on this high-value topic' button on the X-Ray seeds the Exam Reps queue (ties directly to the Mixed Paper concept), so the meta-strategic prioritiser ends in near-transfer retrieval rather than a static dashboard.
  - _Science:_ Confidence-before-reveal is defensible but practice-test feedback alone does NOT reliably recalibrate the weakest students (overconfidence persists) and can push strong students into underconfidence — so calibration must be treated as a habit tracked over many reps, paired with item-level missed-marks, with copy tuned separately for over- vs under-confident cases. (Osterhage (2021), 'Persistent Miscalibration for Low and High Achievers Despite Practice Test Feedback in an Introductory Biology Course', Journal of Microbiology & Biology Education 22(2), e00139-21.) | Meta-strategic activity (topic prioritisation) sits far from the act of answering, where transfer is weakest; far transfer usually fails while near transfer (shared surface AND structure) succeeds — so a prioritiser should hand off into authentic rep practice rather than standing alone as a dashboard. (Barnett & Ceci (2002), 'When and where do we apply what we learn? A taxonomy for far transfer', Psychological Bulletin 128(4), 612-637; Morris, Bransford & Franks (1977), 'Levels of processing versus transfer-appropriate processing', JVLVB 16(5), 519-533.)
  - _App-in-its-own-right:_ Calibration tracking is a genuine standalone metacognition feature — 'do I know what I know?' is a skill students are never taught and exam regret is full of 'I thought I had that question.' Tracked over time and wired to receive launches from Syllabus X-Ray, Exam Reps becomes the connective hub of the Launchpad: plan in X-Ray, practise here, watch your self-knowledge sharpen — a metacognition product, not just a question bank.
  - _DEIS framing:_ Built around the exact warnings in the science: never deliver a single-rep verdict, never an ability judgement. For the often-overconfident struggling student, the panel pairs the number with the concrete missed marks so the gap is task-visible and fixable, not a self-rating that shames. For the often-underconfident student, it actively tells them they're better than they think — countering the impostor pattern common among not-yet-ambitious DEIS students. Progress is framed as growing self-knowledge and belonging, never a leaderboard.
  - _Evidence check:_ All three citations verified REAL and on-point via web search. (1) Osterhage JL (2021), "Persistent Miscalibration for Low and High Achievers despite Practice Test Feedback in an Introductory Biology Course," J Microbiol Biol Educ 22(2):e00139-21, doi:10.1128/jmbe.00139-21 — confirmed; n=341; finding (miscalibration persists for both low and high achievers despite practice-test feedback) matches the claim and the stated effect direction is correct (low=over, high=under). This is the strongest kind of citation: the science warns against the naive design and the concept instantiates the warning. (2) Barnett & Ceci (2002), "When and where do we apply what we learn? A taxonomy for far transfer," Psychological Bulletin 128(4):612-637, doi:10.1037/0033-2909.128.4.612 — confirmed; near-vs-far transfer taxonomy (shared surface AND structure) supports the "hand meta-strategy off into authentic reps" claim. (3) Morris, Bransford & Franks (1977), "Levels of processing versus transfer appropriate processing," J Verbal Learning & Verbal Behavior 16(5):519-533, doi:10.1016/S0022-5371(77)80016-9 — confirmed; transfer-appropriate processing genuinely supports practising in a format matching the target (exam reps). No shaky or inverted citations. The only caveat is rhetorical not factual: the transfer citations justify the X-Ray hand-off, which is the feasibility-weak half — so the strongest evidence (Osterhage) backs the strong half, and the transfer evidence backs the half I'd defer.

### `syllabus-xray`
Current — content depth **4/5**, functionality depth **2/5**. Syllabus X-Ray is a "where the marks hide" exam-intel tool inside the Innovation Zone. Flow: (1) a 2-column subject picker showing the student's profile subjects (falls back to all 29) with a mastery-based progress bar and "{totalMarks} marks · N papers · N topics" caption; (2) on selecting a subject, a treemap of topic tiles sized by mark weight (computeTreemapLayout maps markWeight to grid col/row spans) where each tile shows the topic name and its potential marks; (3) tapping a tile opens an animated detail panel with the topic's potential marks, % of grade, a 3-segment confidence toggle (Not started / In progress / Confident) wired to the shared useTopicMastery Firestore hook, three metric cards (exam frequency /10, difficulty /5, computed marks/hour efficiency), and an italic strategy tip; (4) a static "Exam strategy" box showing the subject's keyAdvice. Data is fully self-contained in components/syllabusData.ts (29 subjects, 278 topic entries). Mastery is persisted to progress/{uid}.topicMastery and shared with WarRoom and SpacedRepTimetable. It also loads study debriefs and fuzzy-matches them to topics to compute logged study hours — but that computation is never displayed.
  - _Thinness:_ Component is 578 lines but ~150 of them are dead/unwired: _STATUS_CONFIG (48-52), _setSortBy fixed sort (102), _handleStatusChange (141-147), _masteryStats (174-187), _filteredTopics (211-243), _topicsByQuadrant (245-254) are all defined with leading-underscore names and never rendered/called — i.e. an entire filter+sort+stats layer was built then disabled.; Three pieces of interactive state (sortBy, quadrantFilter, searchQuery) have no controls in the JSX; the tool that the eyebrow calls 'Exam intel' renders only: subject grid, a static treemap, a tap-to-expand panel, and a static keyAdvice box.; Only one truly interactive control exists per topic — the 3-segment confidence toggle. Everything else (treemap, metric cards, tips) is read-only display.; studyHours/markWeight/examFrequency/difficulty are hardcoded literals per topic (278 topics) with no provenance; year-specific predictions are baked into static strings and will silently rot (e.g. 'last under the current syllabus' for the 2026 Chemistry paper).; The study-debrief fuzzy-match + logged-hours computation is real code that produces a value (totalLogged, remaining) that is never shown — built-but-unsurfaced functionality.; Empty/locked state is a single static card ('Complete your subject profile to get started.') with no fallback content.
  - _Gaps:_ No sort UI: sortBy is fixed to 'efficiency' via useState with a discarded setter (_setSortBy, line 102). The treemap actually sorts by markWeight, not efficiency, so the lone sort state is doubly inert.; No quadrant filter UI: quadrantFilter state exists and getQuadrant/QUADRANT_LABELS are fully implemented, but the only rendering is the quadrant label inside the detail panel. There are no filter chips/buttons to set the filter, and _filteredTopics (which applies it) is never rendered (lines 211-243).; No search UI: searchQuery state + a complete search filter exist (lines 221-228) but no search input is rendered anywhere; the only setSearchQuery calls are resets on navigation.; Study-time intelligence is computed but invisible: studyTimeStats.totalLogged and .remaining and the entire topicHoursMap debrief fuzzy-matching pipeline (lines 150-208) are calculated but only totalNeeded ('{x}h estimated') is shown. Logged-vs-remaining hours, the most actionable output, never reaches the UI.; Mastery is collected but barely surfaced: _masteryStats (notStarted/inProgress/confident/progressPercent, lines 174-187) is computed and discarded. The subject picker shows a mastery progress bar, but inside a subject there is no progress summary, no 'X of N topics confident', no dimming/marking of confident tiles on the treemap.; Treemap is not a real treemap: computeTreemapLayout (lines 56-80) buckets markWeight into 5 hardcoded col/row presets (5x2, 5x1, 4x1, 3x1, 2x1) on a fixed 10-col grid with no packing algorithm, so tile areas only loosely track mark weight and rows can leave ragged gaps.; No examiner-report sourcing: content rules in CLAUDE.md require examiner-sourced, cited insights (DossierRef / 'Chief Examiner YYYY noted...'). syllabusData.ts carries zero structured citations; markWeight/examFrequency/difficulty/studyHours are unsourced editorial estimates and tips are single prose strings, several making forward predictions ('likely for 2026', 'didn't appear in 2025') with no source.; Quadrant labels (QUADRANT_LABELS) and _STATUS_CONFIG use off-token blue/amber/emerald colours, conflicting with the project's accent(#F26B1F)/success(#3A8D5F) token system; the picker progress bar and selected-tile states also use raw emerald and #E0F0ED rather than tokens.

  - The X-Ray stops being a static treemap and becomes a living effort-vs-payoff map that turns every topic tile into a one-tap launch into a real rep on that topic — making the prioritiser hand off into doing instead of ending as a dashboard.
