Each topic gains a structured `marksProvenance` field: where in the paper the marks sit (already have `section`), a citation object `{claim, source}` mirroring Exam Reps' `lesson.source` pattern, and a `lastSeen`/`pattern` note rather than a forward prediction baked into prose. Replace the banned-style year predictions ('likely for 2026', 'last under the current syllabus') with dated, sourced observations ('Chief Examiner 2023 flagged that Section A experiments are the most reliably scored 120 marks' — citing examiner-reports/ where it exists, flagged-as-unsourced where it does not). Add a per-topic 'where students leak marks here' line drawn from the examiner-reports library (currently maths + business only) so the tile detail carries a mistake-first sentence, not just a strategy tip. Add a `repAvailable` join: each X-Ray topic resolves to Exam Reps `subjectId`/`topicId` so the tool knows which topics can launch a live rep today (5 subjects now, growing).
  - _Functionality:_ Wire up the dead layer the code already contains and add a handoff. (1) Surface the existing `_filteredTopics`/`quadrantFilter`/`searchQuery`/`sortBy` controls as real on-brand filter chips and a sort toggle so 'Start Here / High Value / Worth Knowing / Only If Time' become tappable, not just a label in the panel. (2) The hero action in every topic detail panel becomes 'Do a rep on this topic' (orange CTA) that deep-links into Exam Reps filtered to that topic; topics with no rep card yet show 'Mark how you feel' (the confidence toggle) plus 'Reps coming for {subject}'. (3) Surface the already-computed-but-invisible `studyTimeStats.totalLogged`/`remaining` and `_masteryStats` as a quiet subject header strip: 'X of N topics confident · ~Yh of high-value topics still untouched', pulled from the existing debrief fuzzy-match pipeline. (4) Dim/check confident tiles on the treemap (the data is in `subjectMastery`, never rendered). Animation encodes meaning: when a rep is completed and that topic's confidence rises, the corresponding treemap tile shrinks-then-settles to show 'this block of marks is more secure now' — the map visibly de-prioritises what you've banked.
  - _Science:_ Topic prioritisation is meta-strategic and sits far from the act of answering, where transfer is weakest; a prioritiser should hand off into rep practice rather than stand alone as a dashboard. (Barnett & Ceci (2002), 'When and where do we apply what we learn? A taxonomy for far transfer', Psychological Bulletin 128(4), 612-637; Morris, Bransford & Franks (1977), 'Levels of processing versus transfer-appropriate processing', JVLVB 16(5), 519-533.) | Examiner-sourced, item-level 'here is exactly where the marks leak' feedback is the safe, high-yield form; task-focused feedback helps while self-level feedback backfires in ~1/3 of cases. (Kluger & DeNisi (1996), 'The effects of feedback interventions on performance', Psychological Bulletin 119(2), 254-284; Hattie & Timperley (2007), 'The Power of Feedback', Review of Educational Research 77(1), 81-112.)
  - _App-in-its-own-right:_ Yes — a sourced, subject-by-subject 'where the marks hide' atlas for all 29 Leaving Cert subjects that any student can open the night before study to decide where to spend the next hour, then act on it immediately. The cited mark-provenance content + the launch-into-practice loop is a standalone exam-intel product, not a feature bolted onto a quiz.
  - _DEIS framing:_ Frames the whole tool around autonomy and incremental progress: 'where would one hour move you most?' and 'you've banked these marks' rather than 'top students study X'. The Start Here quadrant is renamed in copy to be invitational ('A good first hour') not exclusionary. No leaderboard, no all-time totals — progress is shown as marks-becoming-secure for THIS student. Topics with no rep yet still get a respectful 'mark how you feel' path so no subject is a dead end for a less-resourced student.
  - _Evidence check:_ All four cited sources are REAL, correctly attributed, and on-point (verified via web search). Kluger & DeNisi (1996), Psychological Bulletin 119(2), 254-284 — confirmed, including the specific "over one-third of feedback interventions decreased performance" claim (607 effect sizes, 23,663 observations). Hattie & Timperley (2007), Review of Educational Research 77(1), 81-112 — confirmed; the task-level-helps / self-level-backfires distinction is genuinely the paper's model. Barnett & Ceci (2002), Psychological Bulletin 128(4), 612-637 — confirmed, the far-transfer taxonomy. Morris, Bransford & Franks (1977), JVLVB 16(5), 519-533, doi 10.1016/S0022-5371(77)80016-9 — confirmed, the foundational transfer-appropriate-processing study. No shaky or invented citations. One caveat, not a fabrication: the transfer pair (Barnett & Ceci; Morris et al.) supports the design by INFERENCE — they establish that transfer is fragile and format-dependent, but neither paper states "a prioritiser should hand off into practice"; that is the author's reasonable extrapolation, so the evidence is genuine but the link to the specific design choice is one inferential step removed. The feedback pair is a tight, direct, correct-direction match.

- **Mark Budget (plan your remaining hours by the marks they buy)** [L] — _verdict: build, score 4.04_
  - The invisible study-time intelligence becomes the headline: a calm, sourced planner that takes your real logged study (from debriefs) and your confidence, and shows which untouched high-value topics would buy the most marks per hour you have left — quality of the next hour, never a leaderboard of hours banked.
  - _Content:_ Keep and source the per-topic `studyHours` estimates but reframe them honestly: each becomes `studyHours` + a provenance note and is presented as 'roughly how long to get this from shaky to solid', never as a debt to grind. Add a per-topic `firstHourMove` micro-step authored from the examiner-reports library where available ('First hour on Calculus: drill differentiation from first principles — Chief Examiner repeatedly notes lost method marks here'), so the planner always names a concrete next action, not 'study more'. Add a small set of subject-level 'mark-budget' templates (e.g. a 4-week, a 1-week, a night-before plan) that weight topics by the existing `computeEfficiency` (marks/hour x frequency) and `getQuadrant`.
  - _Functionality:_ Build the planner around the already-computed `studyTimeStats` and `topicHoursMap` that the current code throws away. (1) A 'You've logged Xh on {subject}; here's what's left' panel that finally renders `totalLogged` and `remaining`, broken down by topic via the existing `fuzzyMatchTopic` debrief join. (2) An interleaved next-hour queue: instead of the prioritiser narrowing to one topic, it proposes a small mixed set of high-value-but-not-yet-confident topics to rotate through, weighting by efficiency — and hands each into Exam Reps. This realises the interleaving benefit the science calls for and that Exam Reps' own picker currently misses. (3) A confidence-aware fade: low-prior-knowledge topics (not-started + high difficulty) launch the rep in 'study the marked model first' mode; topics the student is already shaky/solid on launch straight into a cold attempt — the worked-example scaffold fades as competence grows. (4) Mistake-first surfacing: the panel leads with 'the topic most likely to cost you marks you could keep' (high weight + high frequency + low confidence), animating that tile forward, before showing the comfortable ones.
  - _Science:_ Interleaving topics within a subject (mixing question types so the student must choose the approach) is near-immune to forgetting and trains the strategy-selection a mixed real paper demands — so the next-hour queue should mix topics, not narrow to one. (Rohrer, Dedrick & Stershic (2015), 'Interleaved Practice Improves Mathematics Learning', Journal of Educational Psychology 107(3), 900-908.) | Worked examples reduce extraneous load and help novices, but the advantage reverses for competent learners who learn more by generating — so support should fade as competence grows rather than one fixed practice mode. (Sweller & Cooper (1985), 'The use of worked examples as a substitute for problem solving in learning algebra', Cognition and Instruction 2(1), 59-89; Kalyuga, Ayres, Chandler & Sweller (2003), 'The Expertise Reversal Effect', Educational Psychologist 38(1), 23-31.) | Deliberate-practice volume explains only ~4% of variance in education and the 10,000-hours dose-response failed to replicate — so a planner must sell the quality/specificity of the next hour, never gamify hours banked. (Macnamara, Hambrick & Oswald (2014), 'Deliberate Practice and Performance...', Psychological Science 25(8), 1608-1618; Macnamara & Maitra (2019), Royal Society Open Science 6(8), 190327.)
  - _App-in-its-own-right:_ Yes — a standalone 'what should I study next and for how long' planner is a product students would open every study session. It reads real logged study, knows the marking landscape of all 29 subjects, and answers the single hardest question a self-directed student faces, then launches the work. That is a complete study-planning app, not a sub-feature.
  - _DEIS framing:_ This is the most DEIS-protective concept: it explicitly rejects hour-grinding and 'study harder' framing. Copy is autonomy-first ('here's the one hour that moves you most'), reassuring ('you don't have to do it all — start with one'), and belonging-safe (no comparison to other students, no all-time totals). The fading scaffold means a student with low prior knowledge gets a worked model first instead of being thrown cold — directly serving the less-confident end of the range the design system calls out. Logged-hours are shown as evidence of effort already made ('you've put in Xh'), which is encouraging, not as a quota.
  - _Evidence check:_ All three sources verified via WebSearch as real and accurately cited. (1) Rohrer, Dedrick & Stershic (2015), J. Educational Psychology 107(3), 900-908 — confirmed exact title/journal/volume/pages; WWC-rated cluster RCT; supports the interleaving claim and the strategy-selection mechanism as stated. (2) Macnamara, Hambrick & Oswald (2014), Psychological Science 25, 1608-1618 — confirmed; the '~4% of variance in education' figure is accurate (paper reports 4% for education specifically). (3) Kalyuga, Ayres, Chandler & Sweller (2003), Educational Psychologist 38(1), 23-31 — confirmed exact citation; supports worked-example advantage reversing with expertise and the fading prescription. Sweller & Cooper (1985) Cognition and Instruction 2(1), 59-89 is a canonical, correctly-cited companion. Effect directions all match the literature, and the Macnamara citation is correctly used to ARGUE AGAINST hour-gamification rather than misapplied. One caveat: the interleaving instantiation here is cross-session topic rotation, which is nearer varied/spaced practice than Rohrer's within-assignment problem interleaving — defensible and same-family, but a shade looser than the cited paradigm; not a misuse, just slightly over-claimed precision.

### `command-word-reflex`
Current — content depth **3/5**, functionality depth **2/5**. A single-mechanic exam-technique drill. Home view (components/CommandWordReflex/index.tsx, 244 lines) shows an intro line, an optional "Your reflex" progress card (firstTry/seen counts, words-met, a progress bar over total questions), and a subject picker. Only one subject exists today (Business HL), so the picker is effectively a single button labelled "10 real questions"; subjects the student studies but that have no content show a "coming soon" line. Play view renders one question stem as space-split tappable tokens. The student taps the word they think is the command word; a correct tap (norm() lowercased letters-only match against q.commandWord) advances to a reveal phase, a wrong tap strikes the token through and shows a generic "that's a topic word, find the action word" hint. A "Reveal it" link exposes the answer (counts as not-first-try). Reveal phase highlights the command word in amber, shows a success line, a "What X is asking you to do" demand panel, a "The trap" panel with examiner citation + marks-at-stake, then Next/Finish. Finishing returns to home. State (seenIds, firstTryIds, wordsMet, attempts) persists to Firestore progress/{uid}.commandWordReflex via useCommandWordReflex.ts (66 lines), additive-merge, no rules change.
  - _Thinness:_ Content data file commandWordData.ts is 146 lines total, of which the actual content is exactly 10 question objects; component index.tsx is 244 lines; hook 66 lines; type 55 lines. Whole tool is ~511 lines including comments.; Hardcoded counts: 10 questions, 1 subject (Business), 1 level (all 'higher'), 10 distinct command words with exactly 1 question each — no word is practised twice.; commandSubjects() and the 'Yours'/'coming soon' branching are real code but resolve to a single static subject; the multi-subject scaffolding is unused placeholder capacity ('FIRST SLICE' per the file header comment).; Fixed, non-randomised queue: questionsForSubject filters in array order; same 10 questions in same order on every replay.; Progress fields firstTryIds and wordsMet are persisted and shown as counts but never feed back into question selection, ordering, or remediation — tracked-but-unused.; Single generic hardcoded wrong-answer hint string for all incorrect taps.; No unit/smoke tests for the tool. No closing/summary screen and no writePattern() cross-module signal (sibling Strategiser modules emit these).
  - _Gaps:_ Single subject only: commandWordData.ts holds 10 questions, all Business HL (subjectLabel 'Business' x10). The whole multi-subject picker, 'Yours' badge, and 'coming soon' UI exist but have nothing to populate them. A student who studies any other subject sees one option plus a 'more coming' apology.; 10 questions = 10 distinct command words, one example each (Illustrate, List, Discuss, Evaluate, Outline, Explain, Distinguish, Define, Describe, Analyse). There is no repetition/spaced practice of a word, no difficulty progression, no Ordinary-level content (all level:'higher' despite the type supporting 'ordinary'/'common'), and the queue is always the same fixed order every session.; No randomisation, shuffling, or session variety: questionsForSubject() returns the array in declared order; a returning student replays the identical 10 in the identical sequence. wordsMet/firstTryIds are tracked but never used to re-surface weak words or filter what's shown — the progress data is display-only.; Wrong-tap feedback is one hardcoded generic sentence regardless of which wrong word was tapped ('That's a topic word — look for the action word'). It is not always accurate (some mis-taps are not topic words), and it never explains why the specific tapped word is wrong.; No tests exist (no *.test/spec references commandWord). No quiz/recall check, no closing summary screen, and unlike sibling Strategiser modules it writes no cross-module pattern signal — so weakest-command-word insight is computed nowhere.; Stems are described in the file header as 'faithful, representative' LC Business stems rather than verbatim past-paper questions, and questionRef is a topic label (e.g. 'HL · Business Law') not a real year/question number (e.g. '2023 HL Q4(b)'), so the 'real exam question' framing is softer than it appears.; Matcher is brittle by design: it relies on the command word being a single whitespace-delimited token appearing once in the stem. It happens to hold for all 10 entries, but there is no guard — a future multi-word cue ('Compare and contrast') or a stem repeating the cue word would silently break tapping or highlight the wrong instance.

- **Read-the-Question Trainer (spot it, predict the trap, then write the fix)** [L] — _verdict: build, score 3.97_
  - The single-tap drill becomes a three-beat near-transfer trainer — spot the command word, predict how an answer falls short of it, then write the one corrective move — interleaved across many subjects and both levels so the reflex actually transfers to the real paper.
  - _Content:_ Grow the 10 Business-HL stems into a multi-subject, multi-level bank built straight from the examiner-reports library that already exists. Three concrete expansions of CONTENT: (1) Add Ordinary-Level content (the type already supports 'ordinary' but every entry is 'higher') — the 2015 insights file documents the OL-specific patterns verbatim: 'question cues such as outline, explain are often not addressed adequately' and 'only one valid point is often given when two or three points are required' (p.20). OL students get stems pitched at their level with the brevity/development trap, not HL law questions. (2) Multi-example-per-word with spaced re-surfacing: each command word gets 3-5 authentic stems across different topics (e.g. 'Evaluate' on management control, on a marketing strategy, on an economic policy) so a word is met more than once and weak words come back. (3) Add a per-word 'predicted answer' beat: for each stem, 2-3 plausible wrong answers drawn from the exact examiner-flagged failure (e.g. for 'Illustrate', a correct definition with no example; for 'Evaluate', name+explain that 'does not evaluate at all', p.17) plus the corrective rewrite. Every demand/trap keeps its existing page citation and gains a marks-at-stake figure from the 2025 marking scheme. Same authoring pipeline as the verbatim Exam Reps cards — examiner-grounded, sub-15-words-verbatim.
  - _Functionality:_ Turn the spot-only loop into a mistake-first three-beat: BEAT 1 spot the command word on the real stem (existing tap mechanic, kept). BEAT 2 — before any explanation — the student is shown a sample answer to that stem and taps/chooses 'does this answer the cue?' The wrong-answer is the examiner's documented failure; tapping reveals an animated mark-loss: a small marks ribbon fills to the partial mark, then the missing band (e.g. 'the example: +2', 'the judgement: +3') drops in greyed-out to visualise marks left on the table. BEAT 3 the corrective rewrite + the 'what this cue demands' panel. Add (a) an interleaved, shuffled queue within a subject that mixes topics and command words rather than the fixed declared order, weighted to re-surface words the student missed first-try (the wordsMet/firstTryIds data is finally USED, not display-only); (b) a closing summary screen naming the student's weakest command word and offering 'practise it again' or a handoff button into Exam Reps on a question using that cue; (c) a writePattern('commandWord', { weakestCue, perCueAccuracy }) cross-module signal mirroring the sibling Strategiser modules. Harden the matcher to support multi-word cues ('Compare and contrast') and guard against the cue word repeating in a stem.
  - _Science:_ Command-word misreading caps otherwise-good answers and decoding is a near-transfer skill that succeeds precisely because it shares the real paper's surface — so the lesson must stay anchored to an authentic stem, never the verb in the abstract. (Barnett & Ceci (2002), 'When and where do we apply what we learn? A taxonomy for far transfer', Psychological Bulletin 128(4), 612-637; corroborated by examining-body command-word guidance and the project's SEC Chief Examiner reports.) | Making a generative attempt and getting it wrong before seeing the correct answer (errorful generation / pretesting) enhances later learning, provided immediate corrective feedback follows — which validates leading with the wrong sample answer and the mark-loss visual. (Kornell, Hays & Bjork (2009), 'Unsuccessful Retrieval Attempts Enhance Subsequent Learning', J. Exp. Psych: LMC 35(4), 989-998; Bjork & Bjork (2011/2020), desirable difficulties.) | Interleaving topics so the student must decide which approach to use is near-immune to forgetting and trains the exact strategy-selection a mixed real paper demands; the picker should mix topics/cues within a subject, not rotate in declared order. (Rohrer, Dedrick & Stershic (2015), 'Interleaved Practice Improves Mathematics Learning', Journal of Educational Psychology 107(3), 900-908.) | Feedback is high-leverage but one-third of interventions backfire when they cue the self rather than the task; the mark-loss-and-next-move framing ('the example: +2 you didn't earn') is the safe, task-directed form. (Kluger & DeNisi (1996), Psychological Bulletin 119(2), 254-284; Hattie & Timperley (2007), Review of Educational Research 77(1), 81-112.)
  - _App-in-its-own-right:_ A standalone 'decode the exam paper' app: pick any of your subjects at your level, drill the full bank of command words across topics, see your reflex profile (which cues you read right, which trap you keep falling for), and hand off into full-answer practice. It owns the entire 'half of exam technique is reading the question' problem the SEC reports keep flagging — broad enough to be the daily 5-minute habit a student opens before studying any subject.
  - _DEIS framing:_ Framed as a level-appropriate, low-stakes 5-minute reflex any student can build — OL content is first-class, not an afterthought, so a not-yet-ambitious student practising at Ordinary Level is fully served rather than shown HL law questions they can't access. Progress is shown as 'cues you can now read right' and incremental mark-gain, not hours banked or a class ranking. The mistake-first beat normalises getting it wrong ('most candidates lost this mark too') so it builds belonging and familiarity-driven calm rather than exposing the student.
  - _Evidence check:_ Verified. Kornell, Hays & Bjork (2009), 'Unsuccessful Retrieval Attempts Enhance Subsequent Learning', J. Exp. Psych: LMC 35(4), 989-998 — REAL (PubMed 19586265; bjorklab/williams PDFs); supports errorful-generation-before-feedback, direction correct. Rohrer, Dedrick & Stershic (2015), 'Interleaved Practice Improves Mathematics Learning', J. Educational Psychology 107(3), 900-908 — REAL (WWC-rated RCT); the paper's own strategy-selection rationale directly supports the mixed-cue shuffled queue. Barnett & Ceci (2002) far-transfer taxonomy and Kluger & DeNisi (1996) / Hattie & Timperley (2007) feedback literature are canonical and correctly applied (task-vs-self feedback distinction instantiated as the mark-loss framing). Examiner quotes ('outline/explain often not addressed adequately' p.20; 'do not evaluate at all' p.17; 'only one valid point... at the Ordinary level' p.20) confirmed VERBATIM in examiner-reports/business/2015-insights.md. SHAKY: (1) the '3 of 7 marks in the 2025 grid' evaluation-weighting figure is in the shipped data file but I could not confirm it verbatim in 2025-marking-scheme.md — flag for per-question check. (2) Codebase-reuse claims are stale, not science: writePattern/nk:patterns and the WorkingShownAllocator mark-ribbon primitive belonged to the Exam Strategiser, deleted 2026-05-31 — they no longer exist to reuse.

- **Answer-Shape Studio (from spotting the cue to building the answer it demands)** [L] — _verdict: maybe, score 3.62_
  - Extend past the cue into the structural move it requires — the student assembles or completes a model answer to match the command word, with a scaffold that fades from study-the-model to build-it-yourself as competence grows, killing the 'I knew the topic but answered the wrong shape' mark-loss.
  - _Content:_ Each command word gains an 'answer shape' spec grounded in the marking scheme and Chief Examiner report: the ordered components a full-mark answer needs and the marks each carries. Concretely from the existing sources: 'Illustrate' = [precise definition +marks][concrete example +marks] (2015 p.17); 'Evaluate' = [name][explain][judgement+justification — 3 of 7 in the 2025 grid] (p.17); 'Distinguish' = [point-by-point contrast, not two separate descriptions] (p.20); 'Discuss' = [N distinct developed points, range over depth-on-one] (p.17). Content set adds, per word, (a) a fully-marked MODEL answer with each scheme band labelled, and (b) a set of answer fragments — some correct components, some examiner-documented filler ('telling the truth' for utmost good faith, p.19; 'paragraphs where naming was required' for List, p.17) — for the student to select/order. Authored the same examiner-grounded way; reuses the same multi-subject/OL expansion as Concept 1 so the two share one content bank. The Business ABQ theory+link structure ('business theory + a direct relevant quote from the case', p.15) becomes its own answer-shape — a genuinely subject-specific, high-value unit.
  - _Functionality:_ After spotting the cue, the student moves into a build step that ADAPTS to their tracked competence (the expertise-reversal fade): novices on a word (low first-try rate / not yet met) get a worked-example scaffold first — study the fully-marked model, then a completion problem (drag the 2-3 missing components into a part-built answer). As first-try accuracy on that word rises, the scaffold fades to a cold build (assemble the whole shape from a fragment pool, including distractor filler) and finally to free recall. The grading is structural, not content: the marking is 'did you include each band the cue demands and in the right relation', visualised as bands lighting up on a marks ribbon — the missing band (e.g. the judgement for Evaluate) stays dark to show the ceiling the student would hit. Fragment selection uses the 'phrase constellation / sequence-builder with model-order grading' idiom from the Strategiser. State extends to track per-word competence so the fade is driven by data already partly captured (firstTryIds/wordsMet), and emits the same writePattern signal as Concept 1.
  - _Science:_ Worked examples beat unguided problem-solving for novices (cutting extraneous load) but the advantage reverses for competent learners, who learn more by generating — so support must fade as competence grows rather than one fixed mode for everyone. (Sweller & Cooper (1985), 'The use of worked examples as a substitute for problem solving', Cognition and Instruction 2(1), 59-89; Kalyuga, Ayres, Chandler & Sweller (2003), 'The Expertise Reversal Effect', Educational Psychologist 38(1), 23-31.) | Transfer to the real paper depends on the practice sharing the exam's surface AND structure (transfer-appropriate / near transfer) — so practising the actual answer shape the cue demands, marked against the real scheme bands, is what makes the skill cash out, not abstract verb definitions. (Morris, Bransford & Franks (1977), 'Levels of processing versus transfer-appropriate processing', J. Verbal Learning & Verbal Behavior 16(5), 519-533; Barnett & Ceci (2002), Psychological Bulletin 128(4), 612-637.) | An unsuccessful generative attempt followed by clear corrective feedback produces better retention than study-only — supporting the completion/build problem with a fragment pool that includes the examiner-documented filler distractors. (Kornell, Hays & Bjork (2009), J. Exp. Psych: LMC 35(4), 989-998.)
  - _App-in-its-own-right:_ A standalone 'build the answer the question actually wants' app — the bridge between knowing a topic and scoring it. It teaches the hidden structural grammar of LC answers (definition+example, name+explain+judgement, point-by-point contrast, theory+case-link) that no syllabus covers but every marking scheme rewards, faded to each student's level. Distinct from generic essay-planning: it is cue-driven and scheme-marked, the precise lever the Chief Examiner reports say separates middle from top answers.
  - _DEIS framing:_ Directly serves the lower-confidence end of the DEIS range: a struggling student is never thrown cold at a blank answer — they study a fully-marked model and complete it first, which lowers load and builds success early, then the scaffold quietly fades only as they get stronger. A more confident student isn't held back by training wheels. Framing is 'here's the shape that earns the marks you're already capable of', autonomy- and competence-building, never an ability verdict; progress is shown as answer shapes mastered, not a score-as-grade.
  - _Evidence check:_ All four cited sources are REAL and accurately cited (verified via web search): Sweller & Cooper (1985), Cognition and Instruction 2(1), 59-89, DOI 10.1207/s1532690xci0201_3 — confirmed; Kalyuga, Ayres, Chandler & Sweller (2003), Educational Psychologist 38(1), 23-31, DOI 10.1207/S15326985EP3801_4 — confirmed, and the expertise-reversal claim direction (novice support reverses for experts) matches the paper exactly; Morris, Bransford & Franks (1977), J. Verbal Learning & Verbal Behavior 16(5), 519-533, DOI 10.1016/S0022-5371(77)80016-9 — confirmed, TAP/encoding-retrieval congruency; Kornell, Hays & Bjork (2009), JEP:LMC 35(4), 989-998 (PubMed 19586265) — confirmed, unsuccessful retrieval + feedback beats study-only. No shaky or inverted citations. The marking-scheme grounding also checks out against the local examiner-reports library: Illustrate-needs-example (2015 p.17), utmost-good-faith 'telling the truth' filler (p.19), ABQ business-theory+direct-quote link, and the cue-drives-the-grid weighting are all present verbatim in examiner-reports/business/2015-insights.md and 2025-insights.md. The ONE shaky empirical claim is internal, not cited: the 'reuse Strategiser primitives / writePattern' feasibility assertions are stale — that tool was removed from the repo.

### `points-passport`
Current — content depth **3/5**, functionality depth **4/5**. Points Passport is a 627-line single-file CAO-points dashboard with three tabs (Overview / Mock Tracker / Best Bargains) built on the student's onboarding subject profile (currentGrade, targetGrade, level per subject).

OVERVIEW tab: three stat cards (Current / Target / Gap points out of 625, computed live via computeBestSixTotal); an identity-reframing banner that picks a trajectory message from the student's points band; a per-subject breakdown list (dot, current→target grade, current points, "+N possible" pill); an optional "From Your Simulator" card that getDoc-reads the CAO Simulator's whatIfScenarios and computedPoints from progress/{uid} (real cross-tool wiring, top 3 scenarios shown); and a motivational micro-story quote chosen by nearest points-band match.

MOCK TRACKER tab: fully interactive. Students record mock exam results via a form (4 preset labels + per-subject grade picker constrained to the subject's level), which persists to Firestore via the shared useMockResults hook (also consumed by War Room — unified mockResults store with migration from legacy pointsPassport/warRoom shapes). Saved mocks render as a bar-chart "Points Trajectory" (Framer Motion height animation, per-bar delta badges, a dashed "Now" marker) plus a deletable list. Empty state present.

BEST BARGAINS tab: ranks every subject's next-grade-up improvement by points gained (computeBargains in subjectData.ts), showing rank, from→to grade, +points pill, an effort hint, a "+25 Maths Bonus" flag where the HL maths threshold is crossed, and a "total potential from top 3 bargains" summary projecting current→projected points. This is the genuinely smart, fully-derived core of the tool.

Points math (HIGHER_POINTS/ORDINARY_POINTS tables, +25 HL maths bonus at H6+, best-six) lives in components/subjectData.ts and is accurate to the real CAO scale.
  - _Thinness:_ TRAJECTORY_DATA: 7 hardcoded points-band rows with invented improvement ranges and motivational copy (lines 46-54).; MICRO_STORIES: 5 hardcoded fake student testimonials with 'Student, 2024/2023' labels (lines 57-63).; MOCK_PRESETS: 4 hardcoded mock-exam name strings (line 43).; SUBJECT_DOT: 20 hardcoded subject->Tailwind-colour mappings (lines 90-98), several using banned/off-token colours.; effortHint is a 3-branch if/else on the grade's last digit only (subjectData.ts:318-323) — no real effort modelling.; getTrajectoryInfo is a 7-line if-ladder mapping points to one of the 7 static rows (lines 78-86).; Bargains are single-step only (currentIdx - 1) — no multi-grade modelling.; Trajectory 'chart' is a flexbox of divs with animated height; no axis, no date scale, no real charting; mocks rendered in stored (newest-first) order, not chronological.; caoData typed as any[] / any (lines 124, 319) — loosely typed cross-tool payload.; The CLAUDE.md project-overview text still describes this area as the removed Exam Strategiser; Points Passport itself has no module-level documentation or sourcing notes.
  - _Gaps:_ TRAJECTORY CHART BUG: useMockResults returns mocks newest-first (PointsPassport.tsx:78 prepends new mocks; hook line 62 sorts by timestamp DESC). The trajectory chart at line 369 iterates mockResults in that order WITHOUT re-sorting by date, so time runs right-to-left and the per-bar delta at line 372 (mock.totalPoints - mockResults[idx-1].totalPoints) compares each mock to the NEWER one before it — inverting the sign. A student genuinely improving across mocks will see red negative deltas; the chart actively misleads.; 'Now' marker (lines 394-405) is appended as the rightmost bar regardless of date, so it sits AFTER the oldest mock visually rather than at the chronological end — compounding the ordering bug.; The 'mock trends' headline feature reduces to a static bar height + a delta number. No trend line, no per-subject trajectory, no projection toward the exam date, no comparison of mock points vs target points on the same chart. examStartDate exists on the profile but is never used in the trajectory.; TRAJECTORY_DATA improvement ranges (lines 46-54) are explicitly self-described as 'anonymised, based on general LC patterns' — i.e. invented, not sourced. The whole codebase enforces examiner-report/dossier source-grounding for student-facing claims; this tool's central motivational claim ('Students scoring 300-350 typically improve by 25-50 points') has no citation and would fail that bar.; MICRO_STORIES (lines 57-63) are 5 fabricated student quotes labelled 'Student, 2024/2023' presented as real testimonials. These are placeholder/dummy social proof with no provenance — a credibility and arguably honesty risk in a DEIS-facing product.; effortHint in computeBargains (subjectData.ts:318-323) is crude: it keys ONLY off the grade's trailing digit (ends in 1/2 -> '6-10 sessions', 7/8 -> '2-4', else '4-6'). It ignores subject, current performance, time to exam, and any examiner-report difficulty signal. It's a 3-branch lookup dressed as an estimate.; computeBargains only ever considers ONE grade up per subject (currentIdx-1). It cannot model a 2-grade jump or surface that some subjects have a bigger cliff than others; the 'bargain' framing is shallow because the step size is fixed at one.; Bargains tab ignores best-six logic entirely — it sums raw per-subject gains even for a student's 7th/8th subject that wouldn't count toward their CAO total, so '+N pts' can overstate the real points impact.; No CSV/export, no sharing, no print, no goal-setting or reminder loop tied to the exam date — the 'passport' metaphor implies a longitudinal record but there's no timeline, no streak, no history beyond the raw mock list.; Visual palette violates the project's own design tokens: hardcoded #FAF7F4 cream surfaces (banned per memory feedback_no_warm_cream for Strategiser modules; used pervasively here), amber/yellow chips and the amber-50 Bargains banner (amber is on the NEVER list), and a 20-entry SUBJECT_DOT map (lines 90-98) using off-token emerald/lime/yellow/purple/fuchsia dots — exactly the green-migration + banned-colour debt the CLAUDE.md design system forbids.

- **The Calibration Lab — your real trajectory, decomposed into this-week moves** [L] — _verdict: build, score 3.97_
  - Points Passport becomes a goal-calibration engine that turns a vague 625-ceiling fantasy into an honest, self-set target sequenced into proximal this-week sub-goals, with a chronologically-correct mock trajectory that plots actual points against the target line on the same axis.
  - _Content:_ Rip out the fabricated TRAJECTORY_DATA (7 invented improvement-range rows, lines 46-54) and the fake MICRO_STORIES testimonials (lines 57-63) entirely — they fail the project's source-grounding bar and the anonymous 'Student, 2024' quotes are a DEIS-credibility risk. Replace the motivational copy with an honest, evidence-grounded calibration narrative authored from the existing examiner-reports library: each subject's 'next grade up' card cites a real, specific where-marks-are-lost insight (e.g. 'Chief Examiner 2023: most lost marks on simultaneous equations come from compounded algebraic slips — substitute back to catch them'), so the path from current to target is paved with concrete, subject-specific moves rather than a generic 'students like you gain 30-60 points' line. Add a per-subject 'this-week move' content bank derived from those reports (one bargain → one named, examiner-sourced action). Add a short self-generated 'why this target matters to me' free-text/selection step on each subject the student raises (utility-value is strongest when the student writes it, not when it's asserted at them).
  - _Functionality:_ FIX THE CHART BUG FIRST: re-sort mockResults ascending by date for the trajectory (the hook returns newest-first; the chart at line 369 iterates that order without re-sorting, inverting every delta sign so a genuinely-improving student sees red), and place the 'Now' marker chronologically last by date, not as a hard-appended rightmost bar. Then upgrade the flexbox-of-divs into a real two-line trajectory: an actual-mock-points line and a horizontal target-points line on the same 625-scaled axis, with the gap rendered as the shrinking distance between them and per-mock deltas computed correctly (mock[i] vs mock[i-1] in date order). Make the target itself student-settable per subject with a slider constrained to 'just above current' grades — and animate a guard: if the student drags a subject's target into the fantasy zone (e.g. H6→H1 with no supporting mock evidence), the projection bar visibly bends/greys and a calm note says 'big jumps demotivate — try one grade first', because goal difficulty only helps up to the limit of ability. Each committed target terminates in an if-then plan builder ('IF it's Tuesday 7pm THEN one Exam Rep on [weakest topic]') saved to the existing progress doc, and uses the unused profile.examStartDate to show weeks-remaining and pace the proximal sub-goals across them.
  - _Science:_ Distal goals alone behave like no goal at all for motivation, competence and interest; proximal sub-goals do the heavy lifting — so a CAO-points target must be decomposed into concrete this-week sub-goals to motivate behaviour. (Bandura, A., & Schunk, D. H. (1981). Cultivating Competence, Self-Efficacy, and Intrinsic Interest Through Proximal Self-Motivation. Journal of Personality and Social Psychology, 41(3), 586-598.) | Specific, difficult goals beat 'do your best' in ~90% of studies and difficulty correlates with performance (r≈0.82) only up to the limit of ability — beyond it the correlation collapses (~0.11) and impossible targets demotivate; commitment and progress feedback are required moderators. (Locke, E. A., & Latham, G. P. (2002). Building a Practically Useful Theory of Goal Setting and Task Motivation: A 35-Year Odyssey. American Psychologist, 57(9), 705-717.) | Implementation intentions ('IF situation X THEN I will do Y') have a medium-to-large effect (d=0.65) on goal achievement — a cheap, high-leverage bolt-on to each committed sub-goal. (Gollwitzer, P. M., & Sheeran, P. (2006). Implementation Intentions and Goal Achievement: A Meta-Analysis of Effects and Processes. Advances in Experimental Social Psychology, 38, 69-119.)
  - _App-in-its-own-right:_ A standalone 'Leaving Cert goal-calibration coach': honest current-points engine, a self-set-and-guarded target, a real mock-vs-target trajectory over time, and an examiner-sourced this-week action plan. That is the full set-a-realistic-goal / track-it / act-on-it loop a points-anxious student needs — it no longer depends on any sibling tool to be useful.
  - _DEIS framing:_ Frames the whole tool around autonomy and reachable steps for students who aren't ambitious yet: the student sets their own target (not the app), the gap is always shown as a sequence of one-grade moves rather than a daunting cliff, and the overshoot guard actively protects a low-confidence student from setting a demotivating fantasy goal. Removing the anonymous fake testimonials removes a credibility risk; the replacement copy normalises incremental progress and belonging ('one grade first') rather than high-achievement bravado.
  - _Evidence check:_ All three citations verified real, correctly attributed, correctly paginated, effect-direction matching the literature. Bandura & Schunk 1981, J Pers Soc Psychol 41(3):586-598 — confirmed; proximal-subgoal > distal/no-goal finding is exactly as the concept claims. Locke & Latham 2002, American Psychologist 57(9):705-717 — confirmed; 'specific difficult goals beat do-your-best' verified (search corroborates meta-analytic effect sizes .42-.80); the up-to-limit-of-ability moderator and demotivating-impossible-goals claim are real Locke-Latham findings, though the precise r≈0.82/0.11 figures are paraphrased from the broader corpus rather than lifted verbatim from this single paper — directionally sound, not inverted. Gollwitzer & Sheeran 2006, Adv Exp Soc Psychol 38:69-119, d=.65 medium-to-large from 94 tests — verified VERBATIM. No buzzword laundering: each mechanism's active ingredient is instantiated (decomposition = proximal subgoals; if-then builder = implementation intention; overshoot guard = goal-difficulty ceiling; self-written 'why it matters' = utility-value). Codebase claims also verified: fabricated TRAJECTORY_DATA (lines 46-54) and 'Student, 2024' MICRO_STORIES (lines 57-63) exist as described; the delta-sign chart bug is real (newest-first hook order iterated without re-sort); banned #FAF7F4/amber/emerald surfaces are real; useFreshProgress is genuine getDoc-on-mount; examStartDate exists and is unused. ONE shaky spot, not in the citations but in feasibility: the examiner-reports library covers only 2 subjects, so the per-subject 'this-week move' content claim is over-scoped for the other ~18 subjects.

- **The Bargain Engine — what to do next, sourced and effort-honest** [M] — _verdict: build, score 3.43_
  - The genuinely-smart Best Bargains tab grows into a full strategic-priority engine: best-six-aware, multi-grade-aware, and effort-honest, where every ranked points bargain opens into an examiner-sourced 'why students lose this grade and the one move to gain it' card instead of a 3-branch effort guess.
  - _Content:_ Replace the crude effortHint (subjectData.ts:318-323 keys ONLY off the grade's trailing digit) with a content layer authored from the examiner-reports library: each bargain expands to a card naming the specific, subject-and-grade-band error pattern that separates the current grade from the next ('what's actually capping you at H5 in Biology, per Chief Examiner reports') plus the single highest-leverage move to cross it. This is exactly the kind of strategy content the project already source-grounds and cites inline by year — and it turns a number into a decision. Add an honest effort signal: instead of inventing '6-10 sessions', describe the move's nature (e.g. 'this is a technique fix, not new content' vs 'this needs sustained topic coverage'), grounded in whether the examiner insight is about exam technique or content gaps.
  - _Functionality:_ Three correctness upgrades to computeBargains: (1) make it best-six-aware — currently it sums raw per-subject gains even for a 7th/8th subject that won't count toward the CAO total, overstating '+N pts'; only count a bargain's gain if that subject is in (or would enter) the best six. (2) Model multi-grade jumps, not just currentIdx-1 — surface that some subjects have a bigger cliff (a 2-grade move worth far more) so 'bargain' reflects real step-size variance, including correctly flagging the +25 HL-maths-bonus cliff which can make a single Maths grade worth more than two grades elsewhere. (3) Add a self-referential 'you vs your last mock' lens: pull the latest mock per subject via the existing useMockResults.getLatestBySubject and rank bargains off real recent performance, not just the static onboarding profile — so the engine recalibrates as mocks come in. Each ranked bargain ends in a one-tap 'make this my this-week move' that writes an if-then plan, closing the analysis-to-action loop. No global leaderboard — comparison is strictly student-vs-own-mocks.
  - _Science:_ Connecting learning to a student's own situation (utility value) raises interest and grades, with the LARGEST effect for low-confidence students — and it works best when students generate the connection themselves rather than having relevance asserted at them. (Hulleman, C. S., & Harackiewicz, J. M. (2009). Promoting Interest and Performance in High School Science Classes. Science, 326(5958), 1410-1412.) | Competitive ranking / global leaderboards erode intrinsic motivation, satisfaction and empowerment over time and specifically harm lower performers; self-referential progress ('you vs your last mock') is the safer motivational frame for a DEIS audience starting behind. (Hanus, M. D., & Fox, J. (2015). Assessing the effects of gamification in the classroom: A longitudinal study on intrinsic motivation, social comparison, satisfaction, effort, and academic performance. Computers & Education, 80, 152-161.) | Specific, difficult-but-attainable goals with progress feedback drive performance; a calibrated 'cheapest next grade' is precisely such a specific sub-goal, and surfacing the real points return makes the goal commitment-worthy. (Locke, E. A., & Latham, G. P. (2002). Building a Practically Useful Theory of Goal Setting and Task Motivation: A 35-Year Odyssey. American Psychologist, 57(9), 705-717.)
  - _App-in-its-own-right:_ A standalone 'where do my points actually come from?' strategy app: it ranks the highest-return, lowest-effort grade moves across a student's real subject set and mocks, explains each with sourced examiner insight, flags the Maths-bonus cliff, and hands back a committed weekly move. That decision support stands fully on its own without the Overview or Mock tabs.
  - _DEIS framing:_ Built for students who don't know where to start: it answers 'just tell me the one thing that's worth most for least effort' and grounds it in real exam evidence rather than bravado. Effort is described honestly (technique-fix vs content-gap) so a not-yet-ambitious student isn't sold a fantasy of easy points, and ranking off the student's own mocks (never a class leaderboard) keeps the frame self-referential and non-shaming for someone starting behind.
  - _Evidence check:_ All three sources are REAL and accurately cited (authors, year, journal, volume, page range all verified). Hulleman & Harackiewicz 2009, Science 326(5958):1410-1412 — confirmed: utility-value intervention raised interest and grades, effect strongest for students with low success expectations, and strongest when students self-generate the connection. Correct direction. BUT the concept's design asserts relevance rather than eliciting it, so it borrows the label more than the active ingredient — a real but fixable mis-application, not fabrication. Hanus & Fox 2015, Computers & Education 80:152-161 — confirmed: gamified (leaderboard/badge) course showed LOWER intrinsic motivation/satisfaction/performance over time, leaderboards specifically demotivate and embarrass lower performers; the concept's 'no global leaderboard, self-vs-own-mocks' design instantiates the protective lesson correctly. Locke & Latham 2002, American Psychologist 57(9):705-717 — confirmed: specific difficult-but-attainable goals + feedback beat 'do your best' (meta-analytic d .42-.80); a calibrated cheapest-next-grade is a textbook instance. No shaky or hallucinated citations; the only evidentiary weakness is the self-generation nuance of the Hulleman cite being inverted by the design.

### `cao-simulator`
Current — content depth **4/5**, functionality depth **5/5**. A fully interactive Irish CAO (Central Applications Office) points calculator built on the student's real subject profile (set up via SubjectOnboarding). It has two tabs. (1) Overview: ranks the student's subjects by points, highlights which 6 form the "Best 6" CAO total vs which fall "outside", shows Current vs Target totals with a delta and a "% there" gap bar, and pulls the student's #1 Future Finder course pick to show "On track" vs an exact "Npt gap" badge. (2) What-If Explorer: tappable grade chips per subject (e.g. H1-H8 / O1-O8) recompute the best-6 total live in a third "What-If" card whose number turns green/red on gain/loss. A "Biggest Gains Available" panel ranks the top 5 subjects by the NET best-6 gain from a one-grade improvement (correctly accounting for whether the subject is even in the best 6). It auto-detects when newer mock-exam grades exist (from shared context) and offers a one-tap "Apply mock grades to What-If" banner. A gap-analysis block lists all Future Finder picks with per-course "N pts to go" and suggests the single best quick-win subject. Computed current/target totals are debounce-saved to Firestore so the ComebackEngine and other tools can read them; what-if scenarios are also persisted. The maths HL +25 bonus is modelled and explained.
  - _Thinness:_ CAO points scale is a static hardcoded lookup: HIGHER_POINTS / ORDINARY_POINTS objects (subjectData.ts lines 225-231), 8 grades each, no year/source annotation.; SUBJECT_HEX is a hardcoded 28-entry colour map (CAOPointsSimulator.tsx lines 24-36) with a getSubjectHex fallback to grey; any subject not in the map (e.g. 'DCG', 'Technology', 'Design & Communication Graphics', 'Latin' which DO exist in LC_SUBJECTS/JC_SUBJECTS) silently renders grey #71717a.; The magic number 46 (maths-bonus threshold) is duplicated in 3 locations rather than a shared constant.; Future Finder picks are capped at 5 (.slice(0,5) in InnovationDataContext line 73) and 3 (mockUpdatesAvailable.slice(0,3) line 394; biggestGains.slice(0,5) line 240).; No internal CAO course/points database — course points come only from up-to-5 user-saved Future Finder picks; empty state if none saved.; Some success/status colours are inline hardcoded hex (#E8F2EC/#1F5F3E on line 351, #FDE8E8/#C53030 on line 353, '#ef4444' on line 488) rather than the COLORS.success / successTint tokens used elsewhere — consistent with the project's logged 'green migration debt'.
  - _Gaps:_ The CAO points table (subjectData.ts HIGHER_POINTS/ORDINARY_POINTS, lines 225-231) is hardcoded as a single static map. It correctly matches the official 2017+ common points scale (H1=100...H6=46, O1=56...O6=12) but there is no year-versioning and no source/citation comment, unlike the rest of the codebase which is fastidious about examiner-report citations.; Maths HL bonus rule edge case: the bonus is applied when getPointsForGrade(grade, false) >= 46, i.e. for H1-H6. This matches the real CAO rule (bonus for H6 and above), but the threshold is expressed as a magic number 46 duplicated in three places (subjectData.ts line 251, component lines 463 and 574) rather than a named constant or a single shared isMathsBonusGrade() helper — fragile if the points scale ever changes.; No course database. The simulator only knows the points of courses the student already saved in Future Finder (max 5, from Firestore). A student cannot type/search 'what course can I get with 450 points?' — there is no CAO course/points lookup inside this tool, so the 'which courses am I in range for' question is only answerable for pre-saved picks.; typicalPoints from Future Finder is treated as a single hard cutoff ('On track' vs 'gap'). Real CAO points are per-year, vary by round, and have OPTIONS/random-selection at the margin; the tool presents a single number as if deterministic, with no round-1/round-2 or historical-range nuance and no caveat.; Effort estimation is shallow: computeBargains() in subjectData.ts hands out effort hints purely off the grade band ('6-10 focused study sessions' for H1/H2, '2-4' for H7/H8) with no link to the student's actual topic mastery or time-to-exam — though note the live component uses its own biggestGains logic and does not surface these effortHint strings at all, so that data-layer feature is effectively dead in this view.; Best-6 only — no modelling of minimum entry requirements / matriculation (e.g. needing a pass in maths or a third language, HL/OL requirements per course), which is a real CAO eligibility factor the tool silently ignores.; Overview tab's target column relies on each subject carrying a targetGrade from onboarding; if a student only set current grades, target == current and the whole Target/gap apparatus collapses to no-ops with no prompt to set targets.

  - _Science:_ Distal goals alone behave like no goal at all for motivation, competence and self-efficacy; proximal sub-goals do the heavy lifting — children given near-term sub-goals gained skill, self-efficacy AND intrinsic interest while a distant end-goal was indistinguishable from no goal. (Bandura, A., & Schunk, D. H. (1981). Cultivating Competence, Self-Efficacy, and Intrinsic Interest Through Proximal Self-Motivation. Journal of Personality and Social Psychology, 41(3), 586-598.) | Specific, difficult goals beat 'do your best' in ~90% of studies and difficulty correlates with performance at r≈0.82 UP TO the limit of ability (collapsing to ~0.11 beyond it), but only when commitment and progress feedback are present — so a self-set, just-above-current target with visible feedback outperforms both vague aspiration and an impossible CAO number. Language centres incremental progress and belonging, not high-achievement prestige.

Bandura & Schunk (1981), JPSP 41(3), 586-598 — confirmed: 40 children with maths deficits/disinterest under proximal-subgoal vs distal-goal vs no-goal; proximal subgoals produced rapid progress, mastery, self-efficacy AND intrinsic interest; distal ≈ no goal. Claim characterised accurately, effect direction correct. Locke & Latham (2002), American Psychologist 57(9), 705-717 — confirmed: 35-year synthesis; specific-difficult goals beat 'do your best'; difficulty-performance relationship holds up to the limit of ability, with commitment and feedback as required moderators.
