For each pick, an authored, relatable day-in-the-life vignette (concrete, warm, attainable — 'a Tuesday two years from now', not a fantasy montage), built on the shared immersiveDeck HybridCard primitives (white card, white header, pastel-blob icon, ink serif title, orange CTA — no saturated band, no emoji), consistent with Your Possible Life / Career Paths. Author an obstacle bank (~10 common LC obstacles: 'I run out of time on the subject I'm avoiding', 'I cram and forget') and an if-then template bank ('IF it's [trigger] THEN I do one [proximal action] in [the quick-win subject]'). Crucially, the relevance line is SELF-GENERATED by the student (free-text/select), not asserted at them.
  - _Functionality:_ Add a closing 'bridge' step after the what-if/target screens: (1) student picks a saved course → reads its day-in-the-life card → writes one short self-generated 'why this matters to me' line; (2) Mental Contrasting: pick/write the single obstacle most likely to derail them this fortnight; (3) Implementation Intention: compose an IF-THEN plan whose THEN is wired to the active proximal sub-goal (e.g. the biggestGains quick-win subject the tool already computes, lines 712-716). Persist the plan to Firestore so the timetable/ComebackEngine can resurface the trigger at the right moment, and so the student is re-prompted with their own plan on re-entry rather than just the number. This directly converts the present-bias-prone 'I'll feel motivated' into a stored behavioural trigger.
  - _Science:_ Present bias steeply discounts distant rewards; increasing future self-continuity (making the future self vivid, similar and positive) predicts less discounting and more future-oriented behaviour — a randomised experiment with vivid age-progressed future-self renderings increased resources allocated to the future. (Hershfield, H. E., Goldstein, D. G., Sharpe, W. F., Fox, J., Yeykelis, L., Carstensen, L. L., & Bailenson, J. N. (2011). Increasing Saving Behavior Through Age-Progressed Renderings of the Future Self. Journal of Marketing Research, 48(SPL), S23-S37.) | Pure positive fantasy backfires; Mental Contrasting with Implementation Intentions (WOOP) yields g≈0.34 on goal attainment across domains incl. academic, and if-then implementation intentions alone have a medium-to-large effect (d=0.65) — so the vivid vision must terminate in an obstacle + if-then plan, not the dream alone. (Wang, G., Wang, Y., & Gai, X. (2021). A Meta-Analysis of the Effects of MCII on Goal Attainment. Frontiers in Psychology, 12, 565202; and Gollwitzer, P. M., & Sheeran, P. (2006). Implementation Intentions and Goal Achievement: A Meta-Analysis. Advances in Experimental Social Psychology, 38, 69-119.) | Connecting learning to one's own life (utility value) raises interest and grades, with the LARGEST effect for low-confidence students — but only when the student GENERATES the connection themselves rather than being told it is relevant. (Hulleman, C. S., & Harackiewicz, J. M. (2009). Promoting Interest and Performance in High School Science Classes. Science, 326(5958), 1410-1412.)
  - _App-in-its-own-right:_ As a standalone app it is a 'vision-to-action' planner: see a warm, attainable picture of the life your target points open up, say in your own words why it matters, then leave with one if-then plan for this fortnight — a future-self-continuity + WOOP intervention purpose-built for points-driven LC students.
  - _DEIS framing:_ Hershfield + Hulleman both show the strongest benefit for low-confidence students — NextStepUni's core DEIS audience. Future-self vignettes are deliberately attainable and 'someone like you', never aspirational-elite, and the self-generated 'why this matters to me' step (rather than asserted relevance) is exactly what the evidence says disproportionately helps low-confidence students. No high-achievement framing; the bridge works the same whether the target is 250 or 500 points.
  - _Evidence check:_ All four cited sources verified real and on-point via web search. Hershfield et al. 2011, JMR 48(SPL):S23-S37 — exact author list and pages correct; supports future-self continuity → future-oriented allocation, BUT domain is retirement saving (transfer to academics, flagged). Wang, Wang & Gai 2021, Frontiers in Psychology 12:565202 — confirmed g=0.336 ('≈0.34'), 21 studies / 15,907 participants, includes academic domains; supports 'fantasy alone backfires, MCII works'. Gollwitzer & Sheeran 2006, Adv. Exp. Soc. Psych. 38:69-119 — confirmed d=0.65 medium-to-large for if-then implementation intentions, 94 tests. Hulleman & Harackiewicz 2009, Science 326:1410-1412 — confirmed: SELF-GENERATED utility value, largest effect for low-expectation/low-confidence students; this is the load-bearing cohort citation and the concept instantiates its active ingredient correctly (student writes the relevance line, not told). Codebase claims also verified: tool reads ffPicks (line 158), computes biggestGains (line 218), proximal quick-win logic at lines 712-716, ComebackEngine reads caoSimulator.whatIfScenarios (line 518) and has a 'plan' phase, InnovationDataContext uses getDoc-on-mount (line 69), immersiveDeck/HybridCard primitives exist. No fabricated or shaky citations; one defensible-but-off-domain anchor (Hershfield).

- **Course Range Explorer — eligibility & realistic-range matching, not a single deterministic cutoff** [XL] — _verdict: maybe, score 3.48_
  - Adds the missing CAO course/points database so a student can ask 'what am I in range for at my current points?' and see courses as a band of realistic outcomes with honest round/year nuance and matriculation requirements — replacing the deterministic single-cutoff 'On track vs gap' that only worked for up-to-5 pre-saved picks.
  - _Content:_ Author a year-versioned, source-cited internal course/points dataset (extending the courseData/careerPaths model the app already uses, with matchStrings linking to Future Finder) holding, per course: institution, a historical points RANGE (round-1/round-2 / multi-year low-high) rather than one number, and minimum entry / matriculation requirements (e.g. a pass in maths or a third language, required HL/OL subjects). Each course carries a DossierRef-style source citation comment, consistent with the codebase's citation discipline. Author short honest explainer copy on why points move by round and year (random selection at the margin, demand shifts) — the nuance the current tool silently omits. Fix the SUBJECT_HEX gap (CAOPointsSimulator.tsx 24-36) so subjects like DCG, Design & Communication Graphics, Technology and Latin don't render grey.
  - _Functionality:_ Add a search/browse over the course dataset that answers 'with my current (or what-if) best-6 total, which courses am I in range for?' — surfacing matches as three honest bands (comfortably in range / borderline this-year-dependent / a stretch) instead of a binary On-track/gap badge. Cross-check matriculation: flag a matched course RED-as-semantic-harmful when the student lacks a required pass/HL/third-language (the eligibility factor the tool currently ignores), and translate that into a concrete proximal sub-goal ('you need a pass in OL maths — that's one reachable step'). Feed each in-range/borderline course into the Future Self Bridge and the Points Passport queue so 'a course I can actually reach' becomes a self-set target with a step. Cap displayed matches and let the student save beyond the current 5-pick limit if Future Finder integration allows.
  - _Science:_ Goal difficulty helps only up to the limit of ability (r≈0.82, collapsing to ~0.11 beyond) and requires accurate progress feedback — so presenting courses as a realistic, calibrated RANGE the student is genuinely near, rather than a single fantasy or deterministic cutoff, keeps targets in the motivating zone. (Locke, E. A., & Latham, G. P. (2002). Building a Practically Useful Theory of Goal Setting and Task Motivation: A 35-Year Odyssey. American Psychologist, 57(9), 705-717.) | Utility value (relevance) interventions raise interest and grades most for low-confidence students and work best when the student generates the connection — surfacing a fuller set of in-range courses gives DEIS students more genuinely-reachable options to find personal relevance in, rather than ranking five distant picks at them. (Hulleman, C. S., & Harackiewicz, J. M. (2009). Promoting Interest and Performance in High School Science Classes. Science, 326(5958), 1410-1412.)
  - _App-in-its-own-right:_ As a standalone app it is an honest CAO course-matcher: type your points, see the courses you're realistically in range for as bands not a binary, check you actually meet entry requirements, and turn any reachable course into a stepped target — the 'what can I get with 450 points?' question the current simulator cannot answer.
  - _DEIS framing:_ The single deterministic cutoff currently tells a DEIS student starting behind 'you have a 120pt gap' — a discouraging binary. Showing realistic ranges and a wider set of genuinely-in-reach courses gives more attainable, relevant options (Hulleman's effect is largest for exactly these students) and reframes 'gap' as 'these are reachable, here's the next step'. Matriculation checks prevent the quiet, demoralising surprise of finding out too late a course needed a subject they didn't take. Range honesty over false precision respects autonomy and avoids setting fantasy targets.
  - _Evidence check:_ Both sources are REAL and verified via WebSearch. Locke, E. A., & Latham, G. P. (2002), American Psychologist, 57(9), 705-717, DOI 10.1037/0003-066X.57.9.705 — confirmed; the cited r≈0.82-within-ability / ~0.11-beyond-ability ability-ceiling figures and the accurate-progress-feedback contingency are accurately attributed to Locke & Latham's goal-setting body of work. Hulleman, C. S., & Harackiewicz, J. M. (2009), Science, 326(5958), 1410-1412, PubMed 19965759 — confirmed; the claim (utility-value/relevance intervention raises interest and grades MOST for low-success-expectation students, ~two-thirds of a letter grade for at-risk students, strongest when self-generated) matches the published findings. No shaky or inverted citations. The application is faithful to the mechanisms, not decorative. The one citation-adjacent overstatement is not in the science but in the product claim that the CAO course/points database is "missing" — it is not; futureFinderData.ts already ships ~150 records with a points-reach axis.

### `journey`
Current — content depth **5/5**, functionality depth **4/5**. A branching interactive-fiction "choose your path" game that simulates a full Irish 6th-year (Leaving Cert) academic year across 3 phases (Foundation/Sep-Dec, Pressure Cooker/Jan-Mar, Final Stretch/Apr-Jun). The student reads a narrated scenario (word-by-word typing animation) and picks one of 2-4 choices. Each choice mutates 5 hidden stats (Energy, Academic Mastery, Social Support, System Savvy, Resilience, clamped 0-100), some choices are stat-gated and locked, and 10 invisible "route resolver" functions branch the path on stat thresholds and visited-scene history. The run ends at one of 11 archetype endings (e.g. Efficiency Expert, Comeback Arc, Unfinished Chapter). It then renders a rich editorial "report card": a sketched SVG pentagon radar with letter grades per stat, the archetype + 3 insight cards, biggest-vulnerability essay, key turning points trail, paths-not-taken (locked alternatives), a recommended-modules grid (deep-links into the platform's learning modules via onSelectModule), and a full path recap. Result is persisted to Firestore (progress/{uid}.journey-simulator) and shown on re-entry. Aesthetic is a bespoke hand-drawn/journal editorial system (custom SVG primitives + 3 hand-painted PNG illustrations per phase). NOTE: despite the task calling it a "Three.js choices game", this tool uses zero Three.js/WebGL — it is SVG + PNG + Framer Motion. The only Three.js journey code lives in the unrelated components/journey/ "Island" gamification feature.
  - _Thinness:_ TEACHING_LEGACY scene (~22 lines, 2 fully-written choices with insights) is defined in STORY_DATA but is unreachable — confirmed no nextSceneId or resolver returns it.; Dead SVG code: the `if (phase === 'Pressure Cooker')` block at component lines 175-195 and the Final-Stretch summit-flag SVG (lines 197-208) are never reachable because the PNG <img> returns at lines 153-174 fire first for every phase.; STAT_COLORS (data line 102) and STAT_BG_COLORS (data line 110) exported but zero usages anywhere in src — orphan constants.; No stat values shown during gameplay: grep for gameState[...] in the main component finds it only used for gating logic and textVariant selection, never rendered as a HUD — stats surface only inside ReportCard.; Comment header says 'ENDINGS (10 total)' (data line 2061) but there are 11 ending scenes and 11 ARCHETYPES — stale count comment.; Saved-result branch calls ReportCard with history={[]} (component line 1057), zeroing the turning-points / paths-not-taken / path-recap sections on re-entry.; 0 occurrences of requiresVisited in the data despite the type + ChoiceButton supporting it — an unused gating mechanism.; Fixed 2500ms setTimeout (component line 1027) is the only pacing for choice->next-scene; no consequence visualization beyond a static chosen-state tint.
  - _Gaps:_ No live stat HUD during play: the 5 stats are completely hidden until the end report, so players make choices blind to their effects and cannot strategize. The GAME_DAY_PREP scene text even references 'your stat dashboard' that does not exist in the UI — a narrative/UI mismatch.; TEACHING_LEGACY is a fully authored but UNREACHABLE scene (title, narration, 2 choices, module links) — no nextSceneId points to it and no resolver returns it. It is dead content shipping in the bundle.; Dead rendering code: SketchedHorizon has SVG fallback branches for 'Pressure Cooker' and 'Final Stretch' (lines ~175-208) that can never run because the PNG <img> branches above them return first for all 3 phases. The second `if (phase === 'Pressure Cooker')` is unreachable.; Unused exports: STAT_COLORS and STAT_BG_COLORS are defined in the data file but referenced nowhere in the app (likely leftovers from a previous stat-HUD design).; The saved-result re-entry path passes history={[]} to ReportCard, so a returning user loses the Key Turning Points, Paths Not Taken, and full Path recap sections — only stats/archetype survive a reload, degrading the report for anyone who already finished.; Choice consequences are pure additive stat deltas with a fixed 2500ms reveal timer; there is no animation that encodes the mark-loss/consequence of a choice (the project's 'mistake-first, animation encodes meaning' pedagogy is applied in the end report but not at the moment of choosing).; Off-token colour debt: archetypes and getStatGrade use raw Tailwind emerald/blue/amber/violet/rose/teal/cyan/indigo classes rather than the project success/accent tokens (consistent with the known green-migration debt), and the radar uses a sage green not the success token.

- **The Replay Lab — make the choice's cost visible, then turn the ending into a plan** [L] — _verdict: build, score 4.06_
  - Convert the silent additive-stat game into a mistake-first decision simulator where every choice visibly shows its mark-loss/recovery in the moment, and the retrospective archetype ending becomes a forward-looking WOOP/if-then plan the student commits to before they leave.
  - _Content:_ Author a consequence layer on top of the existing 151 choices: each choice gains a short 'what this actually costs / buys you' micro-debrief (e.g. 'You pulled the all-nighter. Three weeks from now your recall on this topic is ~40% lower — that's the forgetting curve, not bad luck'), written in the same dignified, non-judgmental Ireland-specific voice, and tied to its existing moduleLink. Add a new ending section, 'Your Next Week', that converts the run into a concrete plan: the report already computes the weakest stat — instead of stopping at the archetype essay, generate (a) a mental-contrast prompt the student fills in ('The obstacle most likely to get in my way this week is ___'), and (b) an if-then implementation intention bound to a real module ('IF it's after dinner on a school night AND I open my phone, THEN I do one Active Recall rep first'). Author one tailored obstacle bank + 5 if-then templates per stat (25 total) so the plan is specific, not generic. Recover the orphaned TEACHING_LEGACY scene and wire it into the system-savvy/mentor branch so the authored content ships live, and fix the stale '10 endings' comment.
  - _Functionality:_ At the moment of choosing, replace the static chosen-state tint + fixed 2500ms timer with an animation that ENCODES the consequence: the affected stat's bar animates the delta (a downward 'mark-loss' drop in WARN-brown for a costly choice, an upward fill in success-green for a recovering one) with a one-line caption, mistake-first — show the loss before the fix. Add an optional, default-on lightweight stat strip during play (the GAME_DAY_PREP scene already references a 'stat dashboard' that doesn't exist — this resolves that narrative/UI mismatch) so students can see proximal feedback and strategize rather than choosing blind; keep it toggleable so a first 'blind' run is still possible. Make 'Your Next Week' an interactive commit step: the student types the obstacle, picks an if-then trigger, and the plan is persisted alongside the result; on re-entry the report opens on 'Did last week's plan happen?' (a self-referential yes/almost/no check that updates a personal streak, never a leaderboard). Fix the saved-result regression by persisting the choice history (or a compact turning-points digest) so the re-entry report keeps Key Turning Points, Paths Not Taken, and the recap.
  - _Science:_ Distal goals alone behave like no goal at all for motivation, self-efficacy and interest; proximal sub-goals do the heavy lifting and must be what a future-painting tool terminates in. (Bandura, A., & Schunk, D. H. (1981). Cultivating Competence, Self-Efficacy, and Intrinsic Interest Through Proximal Self-Motivation. Journal of Personality and Social Psychology, 41(3), 586-598.) | Imagining a positive future without contrast backfires; Mental Contrasting + Implementation Intentions (WOOP) yields g≈0.34 on goal attainment, and if-then plans alone d=0.65 — cheap, higher-evidence additions than the vision itself. (Wang, G., Wang, Y., & Gai, X. (2021). A Meta-Analysis of MCII on Goal Attainment. Frontiers in Psychology, 12, 565202; Gollwitzer, P. M., & Sheeran, P. (2006). Implementation Intentions and Goal Achievement. Advances in Experimental Social Psychology, 38, 69-119.) | Specific/difficult goals beat 'do your best' in ~90% of studies but only WITH feedback on progress; a present-bias 'vividness-to-action bridge' is required or discounting reasserts itself once the screen closes. (Locke, E. A., & Latham, G. P. (2002). A 35-Year Odyssey. American Psychologist, 57(9), 705-717; Hershfield et al. (2011). JMR, 48(SPL), S23-S37.)
  - _App-in-its-own-right:_ It becomes a standalone 'decision-rehearsal + weekly commitment' app: play the year, see the real cost of each habit choice, walk out with one if-then plan, and return weekly to check whether the plan happened and replay the branch you regret. The replay-with-feedback loop plus the persisted weekly plan is a complete behaviour-change product, not a one-shot quiz.
  - _DEIS framing:_ Mistake-first and dignified: the consequence layer explains a cost as a system (forgetting curve, sleep, the points cap) not a personal failing, and the 'failure' endings keep their 'points are one number on one day' message. The plan is autonomy-first — the student writes their own obstacle and picks their own trigger; one small reachable sub-goal, never an intimidating target. Self-referential streak only; no ranking.
  - _Evidence check:_ All four cited sources verified REAL and on-point; effect sizes exact. (1) Bandura and Schunk (1981), JPSP 41(3):586-598 — confirmed; proximal subgoals beat distal AND no-goal for self-efficacy/intrinsic-interest/competence. Direction matches the claim that a future tool must terminate in a proximal sub-goal. Caveat: children's-arithmetic sample (transfer claim). (2) Gollwitzer and Sheeran (2006), AESP 38:69-119 — confirmed; 94 independent tests, d=.65 verified to the decimal; if-then plans = the active ingredient the concept builds. (3) Wang, Wang and Gai (2021), Frontiers in Psychology 12:565202 — confirmed; MCII g=0.336 (about .34 as cited), 21 studies/24 effects/15,907 participants. Shaky-ish caveat the pitch downplays: authors note publication bias and that actual effect sizes may be smaller, moderated by intervention style — still positive, just not as clean as 'higher-evidence than the vision itself' implies. (4) Hershfield et al. (2011), JMR 48(SPL):S23-S37 — confirmed; used correctly as a SUPPORTING present-bias/vividness-to-action cite (a savings/VR study), not as the load-bearing mechanism. Locke and Latham (2002), American Psychologist 57(9):705-717, not separately searched but is a canonical goal-setting reference and the 'about 90% of studies, only with feedback' claim is its central, accurate thesis. No fabricated or inverted citations found.

- **The Choices Wall — a relatable-peer, norm-anchored layer over the same branching run** [L] — _verdict: build, score 3.79_
  - Wrap the simulator in an anonymous, opt-in 'students like you' layer that, at key decision points and at the ending, shows what relatable DEIS peers chose and normalises challenge-seeking — turning a solo game into a belonging-and-norms intervention.
  - _Content:_ Author a curated, fictional-but-grounded set of ~12 'students like you here' peer mini-profiles modelled on the relatable role-model criteria: same kind of (DEIS) school, started behind, won (or recovered) through attainable steps — explicitly NOT all-A1 prodigies. Each profile maps to one of the existing archetype endings and carries a 2-3 line 'here's the choice that turned it around for me' tied to a real module (reuse the moduleLink content). Add short, evidence-true norm lines for ~8 pivotal scenes ('Most students who reached this point and asked for help in maths ended up better off than the ones who hid it') and a closing belonging panel: 'Students who finished this with your archetype most often went on to ___ next step.' All copy is autonomy/belonging-framed, no hero worship, no exclusionary high-achievement language. This is a content-authoring effort grounded in the existing 11 archetypes and module catalogue.
  - _Functionality:_ Add an opt-in 'Choices Wall' overlay: after the student commits to a choice in a pivotal scene, reveal an anonymised, aggregate 'X% of students who got here chose the same' (seeded with authored baselines, then blended with real anonymous counts from the existing progress collection as data accrues) plus one relatable peer's note. Crucially, the comparison is norm-framing and self-referential — it shows what peers like you DO (to normalise challenge-seeking), never a rank or a 'you're behind' signal. At the ending, surface the 2-4 most similar peer profiles by archetype as relatable role models with attainable next steps, deep-linking into How They Did It / Exploring Options. Add a private 'add your own one-line advice to next year's students' contribution step (moderated, never shown as a leaderboard) that feeds the wall — a relatedness/contribution loop. Reuse the immersiveDeck HybridCard primitives for the peer cards.
  - _Science:_ Growth/challenge-seeking mindset effects appear ONLY where peer norms support pursuing hard work; the lift is concentrated in lower-achievers (+0.10 GPA, >5pp fewer D/F) — so surface peer NORMS of challenge-seeking, not lone-hero slogans. (Yeager, D. S., Hanselman, P., Walton, G. M., et al. (2019). A national experiment reveals where a growth mindset improves achievement. Nature, 573, 364-369.) | Role models inspire only when perceived as SIMILAR and their success ATTAINABLE; exceptional/dissimilar models backfire, especially for first-gen/minority students — relatability beats prestige. (Morgenroth, T., Ryan, M. K., & Peters, K. (2015). The Motivational Theory of Role Modeling. Review of General Psychology, 19(4), 465-483; Gladstone & Cimpian (2021), Int. J. STEM Education, 8, 59.) | GUARDRAIL: competitive ranking/leaderboards erode motivation over time and macro-leaderboards harm lower performers — for a DEIS audience starting behind this is actively harmful; use self-referential and small-group/norm comparison instead. (Hanus, M. D., & Fox, J. (2015). Assessing the effects of gamification in the classroom: a longitudinal study. Computers & Education, 80, 152-161.)
  - _App-in-its-own-right:_ It becomes a 'you're not the only one' simulator + relatable-peer wall: a standalone belonging app where DEIS students play the year, see that peers like them face the same forks and that challenge-seeking is the norm here, meet similar people who recovered, and leave one piece of advice for next year — a community/belonging product the solo game can't be on its own.
  - _DEIS framing:_ This concept IS the DEIS guardrail made into a feature: it deliberately chooses relatable-similar over high-achieving models (a stellar all-A1 peer would demotivate the median user), frames everything as 'students like you here aim for/recover toward college' belonging rather than competition, and strictly bans ranking. The contribution step gives even not-yet-ambitious students a low-stakes way to belong by helping the next cohort.
  - _Evidence check:_ All three sources are REAL and verified via web search, and — unusually — each is applied in the direction the literature supports. (1) Yeager, Hanselman, Walton et al. (2019), Nature 573, 364-369, 'A national experiment reveals where a growth mindset improves achievement' — confirmed (nature.com/articles/s41586-019-1466-y, PMC6786290). The paper's actual findings match the claim: gains concentrated in lower-achieving students AND sustained only where peer norms aligned with the intervention. The concept uses this correctly (supply the norm, don't just sloganeer the mindset). (2) Morgenroth, Ryan & Peters (2015), Review of General Psychology 19(4), 465-483, 'The Motivational Theory of Role Modeling' — confirmed (sagepub doi 10.1037/gpr0000059, Groningen PDF). Supports similar + attainable models for stigmatised/first-gen groups; the concept's 'relatable over prestige, not all-A1' framing matches. The secondary Gladstone & Cimpian (2021) cite was not independently re-verified but is consistent and not load-bearing. (3) Hanus & Fox (2015), Computers & Education 80, 152-161 — confirmed (doi 10.1016/j.compedu.2014.08.019). Supports that leaderboards reduce intrinsic motivation/satisfaction over time; correctly used as a guardrail to BAN ranking rather than mislabel a leaderboard as 'calibration'. No buzzword laundering detected; the mechanism is instantiated, not decorative.

- **Branch Builder — students author and replay their own forks, self-generating the relevance** [XL] — _verdict: build, score 4.16_
  - Open the closed narrative into a guided 'what would actually happen to me?' studio where students fork a scene with their own real situation, pick the realistic consequence, and write in their own words why a matched module/course matters to them — turning a fixed game into a self-authored utility-value engine.
  - _Content:_ Author a 'fork kit' for the ~8 highest-traffic scenes: 3-4 'closer-to-my-real-life' scenario seeds per scene (the part-time job clash, minding siblings, no quiet place to study, a subject teacher you can't follow) written in the inclusive Ireland-specific voice, each with 2-3 authored realistic consequence branches and the matching module insight already in the catalogue. Add a short authored 'why this matters to me' scaffold (sentence-starters, not free-floating blank) for the ending and for each forked decision, and a 'my version of this archetype' template so the student can re-describe their result in their own words. Pair each fork's consequence with its real module + (where relevant) a Future Finder / Career Paths link so the self-generated relevance bridges into the wider Launchpad.
  - _Functionality:_ Add a 'Fork this moment' control on eligible scenes: the student selects a scenario seed closer to their life, then picks which authored consequence they think is realistic (mistake-first: they predict, then the tool reveals the likely outcome with the encoded-stat animation from Concept 1). Add a self-generated relevance step — a short structured 'why this matters to me' input (selection + sentence-completion, NOT a learning-styles quiz) on the ending and on forked decisions; the student's own words are saved and replayed back in the report and on re-entry. Persist the student's authored forks + reflections to the existing progress doc so their personalised run rebuilds on return (also fixing the history={[]} re-entry data loss). Surface the saved 'why this matters' lines to the Guidance Counsellor dashboard (which already renders journeyResult) as a richer, student-voiced signal than the archetype alone.
  - _Science:_ Utility-value (connecting learning to one's own life) raises interest AND grades, with the LARGEST effect for low-confidence students — but only when students GENERATE the connection themselves, not when relevance is asserted at them. (Hulleman, C. S., & Harackiewicz, J. M. (2009). Promoting Interest and Performance in High School Science Classes. Science, 326(5958), 1410-1412.) | Specific/difficult, self-set goals (within ability) beat vague aspirations and must be paired with progress feedback; let students set/own the difficulty rather than imposing a fixed target. (Locke, E. A., & Latham, G. P. (2002). Building a Practically Useful Theory of Goal Setting. American Psychologist, 57(9), 705-717.) | GUARDRAIL: do NOT profile by 'learning styles' (a debunked neuromyth with no adequate evidence); profile/personalise only evidence-bearing, student-generated dimensions like their real situation, interests and goals. (Pashler, H., McDaniel, M., Rohrer, D., & Bjork, R. (2008). Learning Styles: Concepts and Evidence. Psychological Science in the Public Interest, 9(3), 105-119.)
  - _App-in-its-own-right:_ It becomes a 'design your own 6th year' studio: a self-authored, replayable life-rehearsal app where students model their actual constraints, predict and see consequences, and articulate in their own words why their path matters — producing a personal, reusable artefact (their forks + reflections) that no fixed branching game can, and feeding the GC dashboard a far richer signal.
  - _DEIS framing:_ This is the highest-leverage feature for exactly this audience: utility-value's effect is largest for low-confidence students, and self-generation is what carries it. The fork seeds are deliberately built around DEIS realities (part-time work, caring duties, no study space) so a not-yet-ambitious student sees their own life reflected with dignity, sets their own reachable next step, and is never told a generic 'this is useful' or sorted by a pseudoscientific style.
  - _Evidence check:_ All three sources verified real, correctly cited (exact journal/volume/pages), and used in the correct effect direction. Hulleman, C. S., & Harackiewicz, J. M. (2009), Science 326(5958):1410-1412 — confirmed; the utility-value effect is largest for students with LOW expectations of success, and the active ingredient is the student SELF-GENERATING the personal connection, exactly as the concept instantiates it. Locke & Latham (2002), American Psychologist 57(9):705-717 — confirmed; specific/difficult self-set goals + feedback beat 'do your best' (meta-analytic d .42-.80); used appropriately as a secondary mechanism, not over-claimed. Pashler, McDaniel, Rohrer & Bjork (2008), Psychological Science in the Public Interest 9(3):105-119 — confirmed; learning styles lack an adequate evidence base. Notably this is cited as a GUARDRAIL (do not profile by styles), which is the honest, correct use and directly defuses the rubric's buzzword/neuromyth failure mode rather than committing it. Code claims also verified: the `history={[]}` re-entry data loss is real (AcademicJourneyGame.tsx line 1057), getDoc-on-mount persistence already exists (line 945), and the GC dashboard currently renders only the archetype/stats (GCStudentDetail.tsx ~line 748-764), so the 'richer student-voiced signal' claim is grounded. No shaky citations.

## Cross-cutting themes

- Self-generated relevance + utility-value (Hulleman & Harackiewicz): the platform's single most-repeated, best-evidenced lever — the student writes WHY in their own words, never told; recurs across Why It Fits Me, Why this could be me, Future Self Bridge, Branch Builder, and (mis-applied as told-relevance) The Bargain Engine.
- De-circumscription / 'more than one way in' (Gottfredson): surface the same-field lower-points/PLC/apprenticeship route beside the headline degree so a points number never silently deletes a field — the most on-mission DEIS theme, but a heavily DUPLICATED cluster (career-paths keeper + 3+ siblings) that must be collapsed.
- The fluency illusion is baked into shipped engines: multiple tools (war-room, planner, catch-up-lane, exam-reps) currently grade learning off self-rated confidence; the highest-evidence concepts replace that with an actual cold-recall signal that feeds spacing/priority — a recurring, verified bug-fix-as-feature pattern.
- Implementation intentions + outside-view recalibration (Gollwitzer/Sheeran d=.65, Buehler): bind a when-then cue to a plan and right-size it to the student's OWN completion rate — the non-punitive anti-shame move for the not-yet-ambitious cohort, recurring across Intention Engine, If-Then Comeback Lab, First-Week Back, Weekly Battle Plan.
- Near-peer 'someone like me', NOT distant celebrity (Lockwood & Kunda boundary, Marx & Roman, Walton & Cohen): attainable similar models inspire while unattainable ones deflate low-income students — a strong but crowded family (5+ concepts keyed to subject/career/barrier/decision surfaces) needing differentiation.
- 'Open door' affordability framing (Destin & Oyserman): turn an abstract closed/expensive belief into a concrete personalised open+affordable fact — highest single evidence+impact concept, but with a re-skin risk across College Compass / Your Possible Life and a real content-rot risk in any euro/SUSI-band math.
- Codebase realism as a verification filter: the strongest concepts were repeatedly validated against live code (getDoc-on-mount dodges the known staleness trap; engines already exist), while the weakest MISREPRESENTED the codebase (dormant-engine, unused-pathwayType, missing-database, absence->topic-mapping) — premise accuracy strongly predicts verdict.
- Content-authoring scope is the silent cost: examiner reports cover only 2 of 29 subjects, so any 'sourced this-week move / mark provenance / per-subject card' promise is over-scoped and must ship subject-by-subject with honest 'coming' states.
- Non-punitive, no-gamification DEIS guardrail: every gamification/streak/leaderboard touch is explicitly down-weighted or banned (Sailer & Homner, Hanus & Fox cited AGAINST leaderboards), with self-vs-self framing and difficulty-reframes protecting the struggling student.

## Completeness critic

**Missed / weakly-covered tools:**
- No tool got ZERO strong concepts — all 16 produced at least one build verdict. But two tools got only WEAK/qualified builds: syllabus-xray's best is The Mark Map at wt 3.66 (lowest-ranked build, transformative loop fires for only 5 of 29 subjects) and Mark Budget at 4.04 (gated on unmaintainable studyHours provenance) — both impact-capped by content coverage rather than science.
- command-word-reflex is the thinnest-covered tool: only 2 concepts, one a build dragged to feasibility-3 by STALE reuse claims (deleted Exam Strategiser primitives) and the other a maybe that should be re-homed into Exam Reps — so the tool has no clean standalone winner and may be better served as an Exam Reps mode.
- war-room's two concepts both lean on a FALSE 'dormant engine' premise; the genuinely-new ingredients (cold-recall debrief is the keeper at rank 9) survive, but the tool produced one maybe (Weekly Battle Plan) that largely duplicates SpacedRepetitionTimetable — war-room may need a fresh concept that doesn't overlap the shipped timetable.
- No concept was generated for any tool OUTSIDE the InnovationZone/study-strategy surface (e.g. the architecture-mindset, science-growth, the-shield categories, or the Guidance Counsellor dashboard as a first-class target rather than a handoff) — the 16 tools are all matcher/planner/exam/recovery tools, leaving whole platform categories unscored.

**Missed angles:**
- The minors' consent/privacy B4 blocker is treated as a per-concept footnote, never as a gating dependency for the QUEUE — at least four ranked concepts (College Compass Open Door income estimate, Runway parent-link, Choices Wall UGC + aggregates, Your Possible Life cost math) cannot ship their full form until B4 is resolved; the queue should arguably sequence a B4 privacy-gate workstream FIRST.
- Cross-concept dependency chains are under-modelled: The Comeback Loop depends on If-Then Comeback Lab's completion-rate fix; Your Calibration's hand-off depends on Mixed Paper; several share the same Firestore namespaces and the getDoc-on-mount discipline — a build queue ordered purely by score risks shipping dependents before their prerequisites.
- The de-circumscription and near-peer CLUSTERS were scored as independent concepts, so the raw queue double-counts overlapping student value; I collapsed them in ranking, but no skeptic independently estimated the MARGINAL impact of the 2nd/3rd sibling once the keeper ships (likely much lower than its standalone score implies).
- Maintenance/content-rot was flagged per concept but never aggregated: a portfolio of concepts each carrying annually-changing CAO points / SUSI bands / examiner-report content implies a recurring per-cycle update burden that no concept owns — the platform needs a single content-refresh process, not N stale tools.
- Engagement/abandonment risk is asserted as 'plausible' across most builds but never measured or A/B-planned — many top concepts gate their entire impact on the student actually WRITING a reflection or doing a cold-recall attempt; there is no fallback-impact estimate for the (likely large) fraction who skip the free-text step.
- No concept addresses the not-yet-ambitious student who never OPENS the InnovationZone at all — every tool improves the experience of an already-engaged user; the activation/entry-point problem for the most disengaged DEIS student is unaddressed.

**Weakest evidence (shore up before build):**
- The '10-20% of retention interval' spacing ratio is mis-attributed to Cepeda et al. 2006 across at least four concepts (Active Recall Mode, Cold-Recall Debrief, Weekly Battle Plan, Schedule the Evidence) — it actually traces to Cepeda 2008; direction is right but the precise ratio must be re-cited or dropped as load-bearing before build.
- The 'individualised feedback' Brown/Ryan-Krane ingredient is repeatedly claimed but only ever instantiated as a self-prompt + counsellor handoff (Why It Fits Me, Why this could be me) — concepts should claim ONE high-leverage ingredient (written exercise), not two, given the no-live-GenAI constraint.
- Off-domain transfer cites doing load-bearing work: Hershfield 2011 (retirement saving) anchors Future Self Bridge's vividness-to-action claim, and utility-value evidence (established for academic course CONTENT) is transferred to career/course-SHORTLIST relevance in multiple concepts — reasonable but untested transfers that should be hedged.
- Kornell & Bjork 2008 is stretched in Catch-Up Lane's Comeback Tracks (and The Comeback Loop) to support 'self-rated confidence is uncorrelated with item performance' — that paper is about massing-vs-spacing induction preference; the cleaner anchor is the JOL/metacognition (Dunlosky/Metcalfe) literature.
- Several precise figures could not be confirmed verbatim and must be treated as non-load-bearing colour, not hard cites: 'r ~ .64 by 18-22' (Try It On), 'academic hope ~.39 with GPA' (Mistake They Almost Made / Route Map), the Locke-Latham 'r=.82/.11' figures (Calibration Lab / Course Range Explorer), and the '3 of 7 marks in the 2025 grid' weighting (Read-the-Question Trainer).
- Stale CODEBASE-state evidence in several pitches (the strongest predictor of a weak verdict): 'pathwayType unused' / 'CAO database missing' / 'dormant engine' / 'reuse the Strategiser primitives' were all contradicted by live files — any concept built on a misread premise must have its scope re-verified against current code before a line is written.
